 III

Executive Summary
 The goal of this project was to enhance the understanding of the fatigue behavior of
concrete structures for the purpose of improving the design and assessment of towers and
foundations that support wind turbines, which must endure repeated loadings from wind, waves,
operations, and other dynamic effects that cause material degradation. This was achieved through
physical experiments, a review of the technical literature, and collaboration with experts in key
subject areas. The project produced a comprehensive database of publicly available fatigue data,
several technical papers presenting the research findings, and two Technotes to be published by
the American Concrete Institute that advance best practices in fatigue testing and enable the
development of concrete-specific fatigue (S-N) (stress-life) curves. These contributions are
expected to enhance the durability and cost-effectiveness of wind turbine support structures.
 The outcomes of this project provide broad benefits to the public by supporting the
transition to renewable energy. Wind turbine support structures are critical in this shift, and
ensuring their long-term durability is essential for design and maintaining viable wind energy
systems. By facilitating the development of accurate models for predicting concrete fatigue, the
project enables more efficient construction of wind farms with longer service lives and reduced
costs, thus contributing to affordable and reliable renewable energy infrastructure. This supports
sustainable energy, minimizes the environmental impact of construction, and advances the
transition to a clean energy future.
 The primary motivation for this project was to address the limitations of existing fatigue
models, which consist of “one-size-fits-all” S-N curves. These models are generally overly
simplified, and fail to consider variations in concrete composition, reinforcement types, and
specific loading conditions; this leads to overly conservative designs that increase construction
costs to the degree that can make wind energy projects unviable. The goal of this research project
was to develop more accurate fatigue models and design processes that account for these factors
and enable engineers to design concrete structures with improved reliability and efficiency.
 This research conducted in this project has advanced the understanding of how concrete
responds to fatigue loading. The project demonstrated that aggregate size, material composition,
types of reinforcement, and other factors influence the fatigue performance of concrete. Key
observations included that: (i) the use of large angular aggregates lead to lower fatigue capacities
(number of cycles to failure) than when smaller round aggregates are used, (ii) the use of steel
reinforcements can increase fatigue capacity by an order of magnitude, (iii) saturated concrete
has greatly reduced fatigue capacity relative to dryer concrete, (iv) the order of stressing affects
fatigue capacity (either increase or decrease relative to the order), (v) load eccentricity affects
fatigue capacity, (vi) concretes made with lightweight aggregates showed a tendency for earlier
crack initiation and more brittle failures, (vii) 3D printed concrete has considerably more fatigue
capacity compared to similar range strength of cast in plain concrete, and (viii) there is a strong
link between strain development phases and the visual crack appearance on the concrete samples
under fatigue loading. These results also identify where additional research is warranted.
 The project demonstrated the technical effectiveness of developing customized fatigue
models based on the specific properties (e.g., type of coarse aggregate and presence of
reinforcement) of the structural concrete being used. These models are much more accurate than
using generic models, which reduces the need for overly conservative designs (larger than
needed concrete structures). This improves the structural safety and longevity of wind turbine
 IV

support structures while increases efficiency. With this, engineers can optimize material use by
tailoring fatigue models to specific concrete types, lowering construction costs without
compromising structural integrity.
 Key deliverables from this project included:
  Database of all publicly available fatigue test results
  Detailed experimental data from fatigue tests done at Tufts University and the
 University of Illinois
  Several technical papers that have been published, submitted, or are in the pre-
 submission phase.
 o Two papers on compressive fatigue characteristics of regular plain concrete were
 published in the Structural Journal of the American Concrete Institute (ACI) and
 the Construction and Materials Journal.
 o One conference paper on the behavior of bar-reinforced concrete cylinders under
 constant amplitude compressive loading was presented and published at an
 International Federation for Structural Concrete fib Symposium.
 o One paper on fatigue characteristics of 3D-printed concrete was submitted for
 publication to the ACI Materials Journal.
 o One paper related to the review of the existing concrete fatigue database is under
 preparation.
 o One paper is in draft format that explores the impact of coarse aggregate size and
 shape on concrete fatigue behavior and the link between fatigue-related strain
 accumulation in concrete and the development of visual cracks.
 o Two papers containing the recommended protocols for compressive fatigue testing
 and developing a customized concrete fatigue model.
  Technotes for publication through the American Concrete Institute Fatigue Committee
 (ACI215) that present these protocols for compressive fatigue testing and developing
 customized concrete fatigue models (S-N curves for specific concretes).
  The results presented in this report, its appendices, and two PhD theses.

 V

1 Introduction, Fatigue Models and Prior Test Data
1.1 Motivation, Goals, and Scope of Project
1.1.1 Motivation: Concrete as an Attractive Alternative to Steel for Wind Turbine
 Support Structures
 In light of the nation’s ambitious offshore and onshore wind development plans and the
limitations of steel structures, concrete emerges as a highly attractive material for wind turbine
towers and foundations. The use of concrete support structures (over steel) can increase the
operational life of wind turbine farms - thereby reducing the Levelized Cost of Energy (LCoE).
Their selection can also benefit the consumers paying for the transition to a clean energy future
because concrete structures are more likely to be constructed close to the location of the wind
farms in which they will be used- thereby creating local jobs and tax revenues.
 Concrete Gravity-Based Foundations (GBFs) for offshore wind turbines offer significantly
longer service lives (up to 100 years over 25-35 years for steel structures) and they have lesser
need for maintenance and replacements [1]. This is in large part because steel corrodes in marine
environments, even when installed with extensive coatings and cathodic protection; because the
sharp edges created by this corrosion greatly decrease the fatigue capacity of steel, the
operational (or service) life of steel monopiles and jackets is quite limited. Repair of such
damage is often prohibitively expensive or otherwise impossible [2-5]. Properly designed
concrete structures, in contrast, have demonstrated excellent long-term durability in the marine
environment [6]. For onshore applications, the experience gained in segmental concrete bridge
construction is now being extensively used in Europe and China for the construction of tall
towers (up to 185 m) that support very large wind turbines (rotor diameters more than 200 m)
and that can capture the much stronger winds that exist at higher altitudes 1,2&3 [7]. It is observed
that the much greater stiffness provided by segmental onshore concrete wind towers, and the
fewer bolted connections, offer significant advantages over segmental steel towers.
 Even though concrete support structures for wind turbines are cost-competitive with steel
structures, one challenge in selecting concrete support structures (over steel) is that the
construction of concrete structures is considerably more labor-intensive, and this construction
typically needs to be done much closer to where the wind farms are being developed than for
steel structures. However, this challenge is also an advantage because it favors public decision
makers and energy consumers interested in the significant employment benefits, tax revenues,
and other benefits that stem from large, new local industries.

1.1.2 Motivation: Modeling Fatigue for Design of Support Structures
 Fatigue must be understood and accounted for, as turbines are subject to high-cycle
loading due to varying wind and wave loads as well as turbine operation. But, the most common
fatigue models and rules in design standards for concrete structures can lead to unnecessarily
conservative designs and uncertainties for long-term performance. A wide variety of concrete
wind turbine support structures have been designed, analyzed, constructed, and/or installed for

onshore and offshore wind turbines (Figure 1), and the variation among such structures in shape,
type of concrete (normal strength, high strength, lightweight), types and amounts of
reinforcement, and level of prestressing is not adequately accounted for in the “one-size-fits-all”
fatigue models that are dominantly used in current design and assessment practices.

 Figure 1 Examples of Different Shapes of Offshore Wind Support Structures Made of Concrete
 This project is concerned with improving the design, reliability, and cost-effectiveness of
concrete structures by improving the design and analysis of these structures for fatigue. In
current practice, the impact of fatigue on structural concrete is treated the same for all concrete
structures regardless of the type of concrete material, level of reinforcement, and other
conditions that have been found to influence fatigue performance. Current models for calculating
fatigue capacity can be conservative by a factor of 10 or more, which leaves existing fatigue
capacity on the table and does not support simple design solutions that enable higher fatigue
demands to be tolerated. This can significantly reduce the cost-effectiveness of concrete support
structure solutions to the point of them being uncompetitive with other design solutions or with
the effect that projects are not economically viable.

1.1.3 Goals and Scope: New Data, Models, Understanding, and Recommendations
 Regarding Design for Concrete Fatigue Demands
 A primary goal of this project was to advance innovation in concrete support structures for
offshore and onshore wind turbines through experimental quantification of the effect of fatigue
on the strength, stiffness, and durability of marine structural concretes, and the use of the
resulting data and other existing data to advance understanding of models, standards, and the
processes used in design and analysis.
 This project delivered new data in addition to making a literature experimental database
available to the public. It also presents some sets of recommendations for developing customized
fatigue models, standardized method of compressive fatigue testing, and approaches to give
researchers, designers, and developers the tools they need to drive innovation, reduce costs, and
produce more resilient concrete support structures for wind turbines.
 To achieve the project’s overall goal of improving fatigue design and analysis of concrete
support structures for wind turbines, four types of work were conducted as described below. This
scope of work is consistent with that presented in the Statement of Project Objectives (SOPO)
for this project.

 1. Data Collection and Analysis:
 •  All publicly available data from previous fatigue tests on concrete was collected and
 organized into a comprehensive database (in Excel).
 •  The database was analyzed for key trends and gaps in the current understanding of
 concrete fatigue performance. The missing key features of the fatigue data in the
 literature were identified.
 •  Recommendations have been made to standardize compressive fatigue testing and to
 support the creation of a more helpful database for further model development and
 assessment.
 2. Experimental Testing:
 •  Fatigue tests were conducted on various types of concrete, including normal strength
 concrete, 3D printed concrete, and lightweight concrete; concrete made with different
 sizes and types of large aggregates (stones); and concrete with and without
 longitudinal and/or circumferential reinforcement. The tests quantified the impact of
 different stress levels, different concrete mixes, and the presence or absence of bar
 reinforcement on the test specimen’s fatigue life, and the generated data was added to
 the database.
 3. Development of S-N Curves:
 •  The data from the fatigue tests conducted by the project team, and fatigue tests
 conducted by others, was used to try to develop more accurate and general S-N
 curves (concrete compression fatigue models) for different types of concrete that
 considered the influence of factors that were typically not considered in such models.
 The scatter across the range of fatigue tests conducted by others, the limited
 information from these previous tests (e.g., type of aggregates, measured strains,
 failure mode), and other uncertainties led to this not being viable. To address this, a
 methodology was developed and implemented for conducting fatigue tests for a
 particular concrete that could be used to develop S-N curves for particular concretes
 and which only required a modest duration of fatigue testing. This was considered to

 4

 be both a more pragmatic and useful outcome than adding another fatigue model to
 the literature that only worked for a subset of test data.
 4. Advancement of Standards and Models:
 •  Based on the experimental results, the project team proposed recommendations for
 compressive fatigue testing and data presentation to provide more comparable
 experimental test results for future analysis. These recommendations will be
 published as Technotes in the fatigue committee under the American Concrete
 Institute for public use.

 Organization of the Report
 The remainder of Part 1 includes a general introduction to how concrete responds to
fatigue loading (behavior associated with cracking and straining), an overview of the most
common models for concrete fatigue that have been developed by researchers and used in design
standards, and an overview and partial analysis of the experimental test data that was available
prior to the start of this project. This rather detailed background is presented to prepare the reader
for the descriptions in Part 2 “Experimental Testing Project”, Part 3 “Project Products”, and Part
4 “Summary and Recommendations for Future Research”. This main report is accompanied by
extensive appendices that present this project's full findings and products; these are introduced in
Part 3 and a list of the appendices is given in Part 6.

1.2 Introduction to Concrete Compressive Fatigue Behavior
 Because structures that support wind turbines are subjected to high-cycle fatigue loading,
the effect of fatigue on reducing stress capacity must be carefully considered to provide an
effective and optimized design. A literature review on the fatigue process in concrete material is
now presented to show how its behavior (e.g., strain evolution, cracking, other types of damage)
is influenced by fatigue loading, as well as to introduce methods for characterizing fatigue
behavior in such material.
 Concrete fatigue is a process directly related to the propagation of microcracks inside the
material [9-11]. Pre-existing defects like pores and shrinkage microcracks, in addition to the
discontinuity of material at the interface between the cement paste and aggregates, act as triggers
for the formation and propagation of cracks [12].
 Generally, microcracks exhibit a similar formation and origin in both static and dynamic
loading. Fatigue-related microcracks typically initiate and propagate around aggregates and in
the cementitious material. Cracks run parallel to the loading direction under compression and
perpendicular under tension. In both cases, the microcracks show an average orientation
perpendicular to the maximum tension/extension direction in the cementitious/mortar matrix
[12].1 Under cyclic loading, microcracks are more numerous and can spread more extensively,
whereas, in static loading, microcracks are fewer but more developed (i.e., larger and wider).

1.2.1 Microcrack Formation and Propagation
 Fatigue microcracking is irreversible, contributing to a slow but progressive deterioration
of the inner structure of concrete. Crack propagation is quantifiable, which allows for an

1
 For more details, refer to Appendix B - Review of Concrete Fatigue Standards, Models, and Recommendations.
 5

evaluation of the deterioration process and its evolution to failure. When microcracks form, they
produce sounds that can be recorded in the laboratory for a given specimen under fatigue loading
[13-15]. The crack propagation process can also be monitored using sensors such as strain
gauges and extensometers (which track deformation)[16]. Heat dissipation can also quantify the
deterioration process since energy is released as the microcracks propagate [12].

1.2.2 The Three Phases of Concrete Fatigue
 Deterioration of concrete due to fatigue can be thought of as occurring in a series of phases
of strain damage accumulation. See Figure 2, where strain (ε) is shown as a function of the
nominal number of applied loading cycles (n/Nf) where “n” is the number of loading cycles and
“Nf” is the number of loading cycles at failure. Thus, a n/Nf value of 0.5 corresponds to being
50% through the measured fatigue capacity.

  Phase I: In this phase, small holes within the concrete start to close and this process will
 initiate some microcracks. This phase covers about 5-10% of the total number of loading
 cycles (NF) necessary for fatigue failure.
  Phase II: The rate of deterioration then stabilizes. This phase is the main part of the fatigue
 capacity/life of the material, accounting for 80-90% of the total number of cycles to failure.
 In this phase, microcracks start to propagate in a constant rate and plastic straining is in
 slow rate.
  Phase III: In this phase, the microcracks will merge together and create macrocracks which
 will lead to large cracks widths and slip along crack faces, as measured by the larger level
 of straining. In other words, as the concrete approaches its fatigue life, it deteriorates
 progressively quicker, until it fails.

 Figure 2 Concrete’s strain evolution under cyclic loading [12].

1.2.3 Fatigue Strength and Number of Cycles to Failure
 Concrete’s resistance to fatigue is typically represented by the relationship between the
magnitude of the applied stress and the number of cycles to failure (or estimated fatigue
capacity). This relationship is often illustrated using a Wöhler (σ-N) curve, as shown in Figure 3,

 6

which connects the imposed cyclic stress to the fatigue capacity (Nf). This curve represents the
stress level, referred to as “endurance limit” or “fatigue strength”, plotted against the number of
loading cycles (Nf) of fatigue capacity. Therefore, one of the main assumptions is that by
keeping the stress value fixed, it is possible to estimate the number of cycles of failure capacity.
Conversely, for a given number of cycles, the corresponding stress level that will cause failure
can be determined. This model is not a perfect representation of concrete behavior under cyclic
loading. There are other important parameters that are proven to have an effect such as loading
frequency [17-19], and multiple possible impactful parameters which need to be explored and
considered.

 Figure 3 The Wöhler’s curve in the σ-Nf diagram [12].

1.2.4 Classifications of Fatigue
 Cyclic fatigue loading of a structure is generally classified into three categories based on
the number of cycles to failure (Nf):
 ● Low Cycle Fatigue (Nf ≤ 10³)
 ● Large Cycle Fatigue (10³ ≤ Nf ≤ 10⁷)
 ● Very Large Cycle Fatigue (Nf ≥ 10⁷)
 The relationship between stress and the number of cycles to failure is commonly
represented by Wöhler’s formula, where α and β are constants specific to the material and
derived from experimental data:

 σ = α – β Log NF (Wöhler’s formula)
 Concrete fatigue capacity is highly variable, often exhibiting significant scatter. For a
given loading condition, the number of cycles to failure (Nf) can vary dramatically, with values
sometimes differing by a factor of 1000 or more. To account for this variability, fatigue life is
represented probabilistically, often using a Weibull distribution to describe the fatigue strength
of concrete at various cycle counts.

 7

1.2.5 Factors Affecting Concrete Fatigue Behavior
 The fatigue behavior of concrete depends on several parameters, including:
 ● Material Composition and Strength: The type of cement, water-to-cement ratio,
 and aggregate characteristics all influence concrete’s fatigue resistance.
 ● Loading Cycle: The level, frequency, and shape of the loading cycle (sinusoidal,
 trapezoidal, etc.) also play a critical role.
 ● Environmental Conditions: Factors such as temperature and moisture conditions
 can significantly affect fatigue performance.
 The concrete’s age, method of casting, curing conditions, and porosity also influence its
fatigue capacity. Notably, both static and cyclic strengths of concrete depend on these variables.
 Figure 4 presents the behavior of concrete material subjected to cyclic loading. The peak
and valley stains are the uniaxial strain related to the maximum and minimum uniaxial
compressive stress imposed on the test specimen. The progression of strain depicted versus the
number of cycles (or the normalized number of cycles) is referred to as the strain evolution plot
(see Figure 4, right side). On the left side, the monotonic stress-strain response of the concrete
with the same strength is plotted along with a few down-sampled fatigue hysteresis curves (in
green). It also depicted the secant stiffness lines for those hysteresis curves, which are plotted (in
blue) by connecting the maximum and minimum stress-strain points (𝜎 ,𝜀 ) and (𝜎 ,
𝜀 ). The test failed when the last loading cycle crossed the descending branch of the
monotonic test result. This is a very typical behavior observed by other researchers [20-23]. The
peak and valley strain evolution plots are shown on the right side of Figure 4. The strain
evolution plot has a S-inverted shape. Like other concrete materials, it can be seen that there are
three distinctive phases of strain development. Phase I has a rapid strain accumulation due to the
closing of the holes and pore spaces inside the material, and it usually continues up to about
0.2*𝑁 . Phase II has a steady and constant linear strain accumulation. In Phase II, the
microcracks continue to progress. Phase III starts at about 0.8*𝑁 and shows a rapid strain
increase due to cyclic loading. The S-inverted behavior is normally concluded to be due to
microcracks merging and initiating macrocracks and failure zones [19, 21, 22].

 8

 Figure 4 Behavior of concrete subjected to fatigue under cyclic compression (3D Printed Concrete)
Left side; Stress-strain response of cylinder to monotonic compressive loading, along with a few cycles
of hysteresis and peak and valley stress and strain development over the cycles. Right side; The strain
 evolution plot shows peak and valley strain increments over the cycles of loading up to failure.
 (Appendix H)
 Both the static and cyclic strength of the concrete depends on the same parameters [24-28];
type and grading of the aggregates, type of cement, water-to-cement ratio, porosity, method of
casting, and curing conditions during the period of hardening. The other important issue is the
size of the specimen. Thus, a conventional σ-N f curve is valid for a given concrete based on its
specific age and characteristics. In this formulation, the stress parameter can be normalized by
the static strength of the material, or the cyclic stress to static strength ratio: S=σ/f c. The stress
ratio can be represented by S, and Wöhler’s curve can be modified to the S-N curve. S is
normalized stress by static monotonic compressive strength of the material, and N f is the number
of cycles to failure. With this representation, the fatigue curve only depends on the loading
parameters [12]. The S-N curve considers not only the material’s static compressive strength but
also factors like stress ratios (S = σ/fc).
 In summary, understanding concrete fatigue behavior is crucial for designing structures
that need to withstand high-cycle cyclic loading. The development of accurate fatigue models
based on experimental data helps engineers predict fatigue life more precisely and ensures the
safety and durability of structures, especially in applications such as offshore and onshore wind
turbines.

1.3 Introduction to Concrete Compressive Fatigue Models
 The primary way for determining the reliable fatigue capacity to use in design is through
the use of S-N curves that are published in codes and standards. These curves depict the
relationship between the applied cyclic stress and the number of cycles that concrete is expected
to be able to endure before failure. Many models have been proposed over the years,
incorporating different stress states and environmental conditions. Therefore, this project delved
into details of existing fatigue models, the origins and application of the data used to generate
these models, and then assessed their advantages and limitations before doing additional testing
or analysis. This section presents a summary of the most common fatigue models and their
application to concrete fatigue prediction.1

1.3.1 Existing Fatigue Models for Concrete
 A comprehensive review of fatigue standards, models, and other relevant documents was
made; a digital library of documents was assembled, and the most relevant documents were
gathered. The collected documents were systematically reviewed, and the differences in fatigue
design standards and models were analyzed. A key challenge was ensuring not to miss existing
research due to lack of awareness. To mitigate this risk, national and international technical
committees familiar with concrete fatigue research were contacted by the authors to ensure that
the most useful and available data was considered.

1
 For more information, refer to Appendix B - Review of Concrete Fatigue Standards, Models, and
Recommendations.
 9

 Several fatigue models for concrete have been developed over the years. These models
are empirically based on experimental data and are applied to different stress states. Below are
summaries of some of the most widely used models, including their key characteristics and
limitations.

1.3.1.1 Model Code 1990
 The Model Code 1990 model (MC1990), developed by the International Federation for
Structural Concrete (fib), was one of the first comprehensive attempts to predict the fatigue life
of concrete. The model provides equations for three primary stress states: pure compression,
compression-tension, and pure tension. MC1990 assumes that concrete fatigue life follows a log-
normal distribution, and it is specifically designed for concrete structures tested under sealed
conditions.
 A notable feature of MC1990 is its focus on large concrete sections with low permeability,
which ensures more uniform fatigue behavior. The model is based on normalized compressive
and tensile stresses and provides S-N curves for different stress ratios [29].

 Figure 5 Model Code 1990 S-N curve for the pure compression stress state with different normalized
 minimum compressive stress (Scmin) [29]
1.3.1.2 Model Code 2010
 Model Code 2010 model is an updated version of the Model Code 1990, designed to
incorporate high-strength and ultra-high-strength concretes. It provides fatigue life predictions
for up to 107 cycles and includes provisions for self-compacting concrete. However, the Model
Code 2010 model does not fully account for environmental conditions such as water saturation,
which can significantly impact the fatigue life of concrete structures.
 Like its predecessor, Model Code 2010 covers three stress states: pure compression,
compression-tension, and pure tension. While it is a more refined model than MC1990, its
limitations in addressing environmental factors remain a challenge for practical applications [30].

 10

 Figure 6 Model Code 2010 S-N curve for the pure compression stress state with different normalized
 minimum compressive stress (Scmin) [30]
1.3.1.3 Model Code 2020
 The Model Code 2020 fatigue model presents several improvements in how fatigue is
characterized compared to Model Code 2010. The 2020 model also adjusts the fatigue life
predictions to include new material types, such as high-performance concrete (HPC) and fiber-
reinforced concrete (FRC), which were not thoroughly addressed in Model Code 2010.
 Moreover, Model Code 2020 accounts for the effects of the presence of water more
explicitly. Model Code 2020 provides a more comprehensive and material-specific framework
for understanding fatigue in concrete, addressing gaps in Model Code 2010 by considering a
broader range of materials, stress conditions, and environmental factors in fatigue
characterization. According to the Model Code 2020, the presence of water will reduce the
fatigue life by a factor introduced as Ww = 0.75. Figure 7 shows the effect of wet conditions on
the fatigue S-N curves and estimated fatigue life [31].

 11

 Figure 7 Model Code 2020 S-N curve for the pure compression stress state with different normalized
 minimum compressive stress (Scmin) [31]
1.3.1.4 Eurocode 2
 Eurocode 2 (1992), the European standard for concrete structures, provides fatigue life
predictions for concrete subjected to pure compression. It offers a simpler approach than some
other models, making it suitable for general use in building design. However, it does not consider
factors such as reinforcement or environmental conditions like water saturation level, which
limits its application in more complex or extreme environments [32].

1.3.1.5 DNVGL-ST-C502
 The DNVGL-ST-C502 standard is used to predict the fatigue life of concrete structures in
offshore environments, where water saturation plays a critical role. Unlike many other models,
DNVGL-ST-C502 considers the effect of water presence and concrete saturation, making it more
applicable to offshore wind turbines and marine structures.
 However, the model does not consider the effects of confinement and longitudinal
reinforcement, which are crucial for reinforced concrete structures. Additionally, the model does
not explicitly address the loading frequency, which can be a limitation in structures exposed to
dynamic environmental loads, such as waves and wind [33].

1.3.2 Limitations and Challenges
While the aforementioned models provide valuable tools for predicting concrete fatigue life, they
come with significant limitations. One of the key challenges is that most models are designed for
plain concrete and thereby, do not account for reinforcement, which can have a substantial
impact on fatigue behavior. Reinforced concrete structures with longitudinal and transverse
reinforcement exhibit different fatigue characteristics than plain concrete.
 Another limitation is the lack of consideration for environmental factors, including
temperature and humidity. Water saturation, in particular, can greatly affect the fatigue life of
concrete, as it would occur in offshore/marine structures, and this critical item has recently been
considered in the design codes. Additionally, many models do not fully address loading
frequency, which is important for structures exposed to dynamic loads.
 The effect of coarse aggregate size, shape, and type, which can considerably impact fatigue
life capacity due to differences in microcrack initiation and propagation characteristics, is not
considered in any design codes. The other important item that is not very well addressed in the
design codes is the variable loading and the impact of loading order on the final fatigue capacity
of the structure. They mostly consider linear damage accumulation formulation known for steel
material by the Palmgren-Miners rule. This is in contrast to the nonlinear complex nature of
concrete material.
 The authors conducted a study on the concrete fatigue models introduced in design codes
and technical papers. For instance, Table 1 shows the partial material safety factors defined by
either of the codes and standards to account for material imperfections. 1

1
 For more information, refer to Appendix B - Review of Concrete Fatigue Standards, Models, and
Recommendations.

                 Eurocode2                       -                -                1.5          1.15
               JSCE.15 (2007)                    -                -                1.3          1.05

 * For accurate design

1.3.3 Conclusion
 Concrete fatigue models, primarily represented by S-N curves, provide a valuable
framework for estimating the fatigue life of concrete structures. However, these models have
limitations, particularly in accounting for the effects described in the limitations of these models.
Future research should aim to refine these models to improve their accuracy and applicability to
real-world structures, especially those exposed to extreme conditions in the marine environment.

1.4 Available Test Data Prior to the Project
 An important first step in the assessment and development of empirical models is the
collection and analysis of existing experimental test data. Since no prior comprehensive
collection of fatigue test data has been made, this was a dominant initial task on this project. This
section presents the process of gathering the available data published by others over the years.
 The project adopted approaches used in previous global database projects to create a
repository template [34]. This template was reviewed and disseminated among researchers at
Tufts University and the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign. These teams were responsible
for verifying, correcting, and classifying data as reliable or potentially unreliable. The initial
repository was populated with test data from prior studies conducted at Tufts, the University of
Illinois. A digital library of all the available papers on experimental fatigue tests containing data
was made. The development of the concrete fatigue experimental test data repository focused on
accommodating different test data variables. Data on concrete fatigue behavior were collected
from more than 2500 fatigue tests that were reported in the literature. These tests included
various specimen shapes, stress conditions, and fatigue regimes. The repository was created in
the form of an Excel spreadsheet with multiple tabs, each focusing on different variables. The
test data were presented in both U.S. Customary Units (USCU) and International System Units
(SI) for consistency (see Figure 9). 1

1
 For more information, refer to the Appendix A - Concrete Fatigue Test Data Base.
 14

 Figure 9 Screenshot #1 of the experimental test data repository – Tabs definition

 The test data repository contains detailed information on concrete specimen geometry,
material properties, and loading protocols used in fatigue tests. This includes maximum and
minimum stress levels, fatigue cycle counts, and material properties like aggregate size, water-
cement ratio, and concrete strength (see Figure 10 and Figure 11). The repository also includes
comparisons of the test data with existing S-N models, enabling researchers to evaluate the
accuracy of existing fatigue models. Additionally, researchers used the repository to identify
outliers in the data, which could influence fatigue life predictions.

 Figure 10 Screenshot #2 of the experimental test data repository – Specimen Geometry and Material
 Properties

 15

 Figure 11 Screenshot #3 of the experimental test data repository – Material Properties and cyclic
 fatigue loading regime
 With the details about the specimen geometry, material properties, and fatigue loading plan
obtained, fatigue experimental test results were populated next in the spreadsheet. The most
basic fatigue test result reported in almost all fatigue investigations is the number of cycles to
failure, Nf, also referred to as the fatigue capacity. Additionally, considering concrete's inherently
nonlinear material behavior, it is important to understand and report the behavior of concrete
through the fatigue loading process, as well as the details about the concrete mix and loading
protocol. However, only a small proportion of the papers and investigations have reported those
types of information. This finding was crucial in defining the next steps of this study and
thinking about the importance of detailed data presentation.
 After having all the data sets populated in the first tab, other tabs could be used to compare
the data with the existing characteristics and design S-N curves introduced by different standard
codes or researchers. For this purpose, a table was created to allow the user to play with the
variables to see specified data tests and compare them with the S-N curves. Figure 12 compares
the uniaxial cylinders test data with four different Smin levels with two different characteristic S-
N models. As shown, a huge scatter in every stress level can be observed. In some cases, this
difference in fatigue capacity is more than 1000 times different (3 order of magnitude) which is
enormous. Since fatigue models are usually derived by a curve to a subset of data, there is an
uncertain level of conservative or unconservative in design criteria. The causes for this huge
scatter include the lack of reported key data on most of the datasets, the lack of standard
procedure for conducting fatigue testing, the inefficient procedure of defining fatigue load
protocols only based on the average strength of the whole concrete batch and ignoring the
inherent variability of the concrete strength, and other factors.

 16

 Figure 12 Comparison of different specified data sets with DNVGL characteristic S-N model

2 Experimental Testing Program
 The dominant portion of the total work on this project was the design and conduct of a
large experimental testing campaign on the compressive fatigue strength of concrete. The
objective of these tests was to assess the influence of key and often neglected factors on fatigue
capacity and to improve the general understanding of fatigue behavior. The broader technical
goal of this testing program was to improve design and analysis practices for fatigue.
 The development and conduct of this experimental testing campaign had several steps and
phases, including the selection of concrete. The industry advisory panel provided valuable
insight into designs and stress loading ranges, identified gaps, and shared their experiences with
marine concretes; this all helped guide the selection of materials for the testing program. An
extensive literature review was conducted to review additional documents and case studies from
the community regarding the performance of concrete in marine structures, such as oil and gas
platforms, offshore wind foundations, and towers 1.
 To assess the typical compressive and tensile stress levels encountered in designs based on
International Electrical Technological Commission (IEC) fatigue load cases, the team leveraged
the expertise of DNV GL and consulted with designers and industry professionals. Load and
stress demand assessment methods were evaluated and employed to do a primary analysis of
wind turbine structure, focusing on evaluating stress ranges relevant to both normal and extreme

1
 For more information, refer to Appendix C - Marine Concrete and Selection of Concrete Materials.
 17

IEC design load cases. The methodology for this assessment, along with an example, was
presented in Appendix D1.
 Gaps in available test data were identified through the analysis of the existing database.
Because the comprehensive testing required to fill these gaps (many thousands of tests) was well
beyond what could be accomplished in a reasonable amount of time (some individual tests take
days to conduct) a preliminary series of experiments was conducted to investigate the potential
effect of certain factors, and the results were used to determine the scope of work for the larger
test series. Because the range of possible tests was greater than what could be studied in depth;
therefore, the initial testing phase was designed to be broad rather than deep, ensuring that no
key factors were overlooked. By assessing the potential effect of factors, it was then possible to
develop a comprehensive set of tests to more fully investigate and quantify the effect of these
factors. For this purpose, one type of casted concrete was used, and then sets of initial tests were
conducted to measure the fatigue capacity of the plain concrete under:
  Concentric constant amplitude loading; to measure the concrete fatigue capacity
  Concentric constant amplitude loading on saturated specimens; offshore structures are
 submerged and saturated; there were few previous studies on this [35].
  Concentric variable amplitude loading; variable amplitude loading is closer to the real-
 world loading of the structures.
  Eccentric constant amplitude loading; the structural components are mostly loaded
 eccentrically, and it is more logical and realistic to see the effect of eccentric loading on
 fatigue capacity. Tests were performed to see the effect of the concrete’s ability to
 adjust the load share based on the degradation of the material in some parts with high
 stress and its effect on final fatigue capacity.
  Concentric constant amplitude loading on reinforced concrete specimens; the fatigue
 capacity of concrete and reinforcement steel are tested and considered separately for
 design purposes. It is important to see the combined effect all together. In this initial
 phase, a few cylindrical samples were cast using longitudinal bar reinforcements, to
 investigate the concrete degradation, steel yielding, and load share change due to these
 on final fatigue capacity2
 After the initial phase, the testing program was designed to be broad, covering a range of
concrete with different coarse aggregate sizes, shapes, and types. Figure 13 shows examples of
the fabrication and casting process. This plan was based on the findings of earlier efforts, and it
considered where test data was particularly lacking. Given the uncertainty surrounding the
performance of different materials under various conditions, the testing was conducted in
batches3&4&5. The initial testing highlighted the gaps and the need for more standardized testing.
More detailed testing later on proved to be necessary to highlight the importance of

 The next section presents an overview on the experimental program, equipment, and data
acquisition process. Then, Part 2 delves into the conducted test results (both initial and extensive
ones) including constant amplitude cyclic testing on plain concrete, the effect of variable
amplitude cyclic loading order on the final fatigue capacity, the effect of reinforcement on
fatigue capacity and so on. These summaries of results help to better understand the needs for the
products delivered by this project and justify the need for a more standardized method of testing.

2.1 Overview of the Experimental Testing Program and Equipment
 A 200-kip compressive servo-hydraulic testing machine was used for the present testing
program. Monotonic tests were conducted in a quasi-static displacement-controlled loading
protocol to capture concrete's post-peak stress-strain response to compressive loading. The force-
control mode was used for the fatigue loading to maintain the peak and valley stress targets
within an acceptable range. Sinusoidal waveform defined for the cyclic loading. Two
extensometers mounted on both sides of the cylinder (see Figure 14) were used to measure the
change in the length over the gauge length (6 in, 150 mm) which provided the average straining.
The stress and strain hysteresis data were collected using a separate data acquisition system at a
frequency of 25 Hz. Over 500 individual tests were conducted in different stages of this project;
the collected data from these tests with more details were added to the data repository. The
fatigue loading protocol is defined using the improved strength estimation method, ISEM 1, to
have the fatigue loading protocol defined as precise as possible. Compressive loading protocol
consists of maximum and minimum loading ratio ( 𝑆 = _
 and 𝑆 = _
 ),
 _ _
where 𝑓 _ represents the monotonic strength, 𝑓 _ represents the maximum imposed
stress during fatigue cyclic loading, and 𝑓 _ represents the minimum imposed stress during
fatigue cyclic loading. Usually, the average strength of the material from recent monotonic tests
is used as monotonic strength, and this can lead to unrealistic results due to the variation in actual
strength from one specimen to another specimen. To resolve this issue, the Improved Strength
Estimate Method (ISEM) was developed and used to estimate each cylinder's strength before
doing the fatigue testing (or through a post-test correction). For this purpose, each cylinder was
loaded up to 0.95 ∗ 𝑆 level (defined based on the average strength of the batch), considering
the standard loading rate defined in ASTM C39 [36]. Then, the stress-strain response was plotted
versus the monotonic test results. The closest monotonic test result to the plotted stress-strain
response would then be that specific cylinder's improved estimated/assumed strength. For
instance, Figure 15 shows that fatigue test #7 has the closest stress-strain behavior to monotonic
test result #5. Thus, the estimated strength of this specimen for defining the fatigue loading
protocol using the ISEM method would be 7400 psi (51 MPa) (rather than 6800 psi (47 MPa),
which is the average strength of this batch of concrete).

1
 To know more about ISEM, please refer to Appendix J - ACI Technote 4 Recommended Protocol for Compression
Fatigue Testing of Concrete using improved strength estimation method.

  monotonic loading using the ISEM method to estimate the actual strength of the cylinder

2.2 Constant Amplitude Cyclic Testing
2.2.1 Eccentric Loading
 A series of fatigue tests were conducted under conditions where the loading was eccentric,
resulting in compressive stress on one face being up to three times higher than on the opposing
face. The results revealed that the fatigue capacity, when measured based on the maximum
stress, was significantly higher than if the entire test cylinder had been uniformly subjected to
that same stress level. This outcome is particularly promising for structural components exposed
to bending and significant strain gradients. Figure 16 shows the setup and strain gradient
expected in eccentric loading over a cross-section of the cylindrical sample. An example of the
test that has been done and predicted fatigue capacity based on the Model Code 2010 S-N model
is presented in Figure 17.

 (a) Cylinder (b) Strain Gradient
 Figure 16 Eccentric loading setup

 22

 Figure 17 Example of eccentric loading effect on the fatigue capacity

2.2.2 Saturation Effect
 Current research indicates that the saturation level of concrete has a significant effect on its
fatigue capacity. Studies have shown that saturated (or submerged) concrete specimens exhibit
reduced fatigue capacity due to the presence of moisture, which affects the internal
microstructure and accelerates material degradation under cyclic loading. This decrease can be
attributed to factors such as increased pore water pressure and reduced interfacial bond strength,
leading to earlier onset of fatigue cracking. As a result, accurate consideration of the
environmental condition, such as water submergence, is crucial for determining the fatigue
capacity of concrete, especially for offshore wind turbine foundations. A new environmental
factor has even been proposed to account for these effects in design codes like those of DNV and
Model Code 2020 to ensure safer and more reliable fatigue predictions for concrete structures
[31, 33, 35]. The initial testing program in this study also considered examining the effect of
saturation on fatigue capacity. For this purpose, test specimens were put inside a water tank for a
couple of months to stay hydrated and saturated. Then siren plastic wrap used to keep the
moisture from evaporating and specimens subjected to cyclic loading. The result confirms the
hypothesis mentioned earlier in this paragraph and the final fatigue capacity is about 3/4 of the
fatigue capacity in the dry condition. (See Figure 18)

 23

 Figure 18 Effect of Saturation on the final fatigue capacity

2.2.3 Experimental Investigation of Plain Concrete Fatigue in Axial Compression
 In this study, fatigue tests were performed on concrete cylinder specimens subjected to
axial compressive loading until failure. Various maximum stress levels and stress ratios (S max,
R=Smin/Smax) were chosen to examine their effects on the fatigue capacity and behavior of
concrete. A brief overview of the experimental findings and subsequent analysis is provided
below.
 The experimental results, specifically the number of cycles to failure (fatigue life, N f),
were compared to existing models that relate maximum stress to fatigue life (S max-Nf) for
concrete under fatigue loading. The comparisons suggest that the Model Code (2010) fatigue-life
model provides the most accurate predictions for concrete fatigue life, especially at lower Smin
values (lower R). However, for higher Smin (or R), the model's predictions appeared
conservative when compared to the limited experimental data. As a result, further validation is
required to assess the model’s conservativeness and suitability at higher Smin values.
 For a limited set of (Smax, R) combinations, the effect of frequency on fatigue life (N f) was
also explored. On average, while Nf tended to increase as frequency rose, the change was
minimal and fell within the typical variability of results. Consequently, the common practice of
disregarding the influence of frequency within the 1 to 10 Hz range, particularly in the high-
cycle fatigue domain, seems to be a reasonable assumption.
 In addition to examining concrete fatigue capacity (Nf), the behavior of concrete under
fatigue loading was also analyzed, focusing on strain development and stiffness changes
throughout the fatigue testing process. The secant stiffness exhibited behavior that mirrored, but
was opposite to, the strain behavior. While the peak and valley strain magnitudes progressively
increased during the test (even though stress levels remained constant), stiffness continuously
degraded. Strain evolution plots for both peak and valley strains followed an “inverted-S” shape,
while stiffness evolution plots showed an “upright-S” pattern. The evolution plots could be

 24

divided into three distinct phases, each with unique characteristics. A consistent change in the
middle phase allowed for the calculation of gradients for both strain and stiffness in all tests.
When the logarithms of these gradients were plotted against the logarithms of N f, a linear
relationship was observed with a high correlation, matching trends found by other researchers.
 For specimens tested with the same (Smax, R, f) combination, normalizing strain evolution
plots to the fatigue loading cycles showed a strong similarity in shape and behavior across all
tests. Secant stiffness values at the start of the tests (E sec_st) were consistent across all specimens,
but at the end of the tests (Esec_end), the values were lower, with higher Nf tests exhibiting greater
Esec_end. Additionally, when comparing degraded stiffness with the monotonic stress-strain curve
of an undamaged specimen, it was observed that the point where degraded stiffness intersects the
descending branch of the monotonic curve could be a reliable indicator of impending fatigue
failure during fatigue analysis1.
 Figure 19 shows the S-N curve for axial compressive fatigue testing.

 Figure 19 Experimental test results – number of cycles to failure Nf, with respect to (Smax, R, f) [21].

1
 For more information, please refer to the Appendix E - Experimental investigation of concrete fatigue in axial
compression - ACI Structural Journal.
 25

2.2.4 Plain Concrete Strain Behavior Under Uniaxial Fatigue Loading
 This study involved conducting uniaxial compressive fatigue tests on concrete cylinder
specimens until failure, and it documented axial strains throughout the entire fatigue loading
process. The behavior of axial strains during fatigue loading, factors influencing their behavior,
and critical strain magnitudes at key points in the loading process were investigated. The
findings summarized below are applicable primarily to normal strength concrete without
reinforcement, and for the stress ranges used in this research.
 Strain Evolution Behavior: Strain gradient evolution plots revealed a convex-shaped,
continuously nonlinear strain evolution rate. During the second phase of fatigue loading, the
strain gradients were also nonlinear but exhibited smaller magnitudes, gradually decreasing until
about 50% of the fatigue life, then increasing until the onset of the final phase, marked by visible
surface cracks. While existing models assume a constant strain evolution rate in the middle
phase, the observed nonlinearity indicates that current theories capture only an averaged strain
evolution. The first and third phases, characterized by high strain gradients at the beginning and
end, are consistent with established theories. Understanding the true strain behavior in the middle
phase is valuable for estimating remaining fatigue life in fatigue-critical concrete structures.
 Effect of Failure Cycles on Strain Evolution: For tests at the same stress and frequency
levels (Smax, Smin, f), the strain evolutions were similar for specimens with comparable failure
cycles (Nf). However, specimens with significantly higher Nf (greater than 105 cycles) exhibited
larger strain evolutions, likely due to the contribution of sustained loading strain alongside the
elasto-plastic strain component. This suggests that for tests with high N f (greater than 106
cycles), larger strain magnitudes are expected due to gsustained loading effects.
 Effect of Stress Levels on Strain Evolution: For specimens tested at the same peak stress
level (Smax), similar peak strain evolutions were observed, regardless of differences in valley
stress levels (Smin) or frequency, provided Nf values were comparable. However, tests with
higher Nf exhibited larger peak strain evolutions. Increasing peak stress levels also led to
increased peak strain evolution, allowing for predictions of peak strain behavior based on stress
levels and expected failure cycles.
 Peak Strain at Failure (εmax_f): The peak strain at failure (εmax_f) was found to increase
with increasing Nf. A reasonable range of peak strain values, especially for Nf < 300,000 (log10
(Nf) < 5.5), was identified, which is useful for performance-based fatigue analysis and design.
For stress levels with anticipated Nf > 300,000, εmax_f is expected to exceed the strain on the
descending branch of the monotonic stress-strain curve, helping estimate peak strain values for
fatigue design.
 Peak Strain at the Transition from Phase-2 to Phase-3 (ε max_P23): The peak strain at the
transition between phase two and phase three (ε max_P23) also increased with Nf, similarly to εmax_f.
A reasonable range for εmax_P23 was identified, with values close to the strain at peak stress in the
monotonic stress-strain curve (εcm), particularly for tests with log10 (Nf )< 5. To prevent brittle
failure from rapid crack growth in the third phase, it is advisable to limit fatigue usage to the end
of the second phase during analysis and design.
 Figure 20 illustrates the strain evolution of concrete during fatigue loading .

 26

Figure 20 Evolution plots showing typical variation strain variation (on the left) and stiffness variation
(on the right)] with an increasing number of fatigue loading cycles, along with depiction of parameters
 used in fatigue models [22]
 Figure 21 serves as a summary, illustrating these findings and depicting damage
progression on the surface of concrete specimens with increasing fatigue loading, in relation to
peak strain evolution1.

 For more information, please refer to the Appendix F - Concrete compressive strain behavior and magnitudes
under uniaxial fatigue loading - Construction and Building Materials Journal.

2.2.5 Fatigue Performance of 3D Printed Concrete under Uniaxial Compression
 This part of the study examined the fatigue characteristics of 3D-printed concrete through
uniaxial concentric compressive cyclic tests. A new improved strength estimation method
(ISEM) was used to more accurately predefine the fatigue load protocol, minimizing the impact
of strength variation on test results. Below is a summary of the key findings from the
experiments and analysis.
 The experimental results from constant amplitude compressive fatigue tests yielded
promising results. On average, the fatigue capacity of the 3D-printed concrete exceeded the
estimated capacity predicted by three standard fatigue models. Additionally, the behavior of 3D-
printed concrete was similar to that of traditionally cast concrete, although the variability of
results was higher. This variation, typical in fatigue testing, is likely due to inconsistencies in the

 28

placement of holes and pores among the filaments, which could influence the development of
microcracks.
 The study also investigated the progression of peak and valley strains, as well as the
degradation of secant stiffness under cyclic loading. The results revealed the usual three phases
of strain development and stiffness degradation, which are consistent with observations in
traditional concrete. Strain evolution plots, showing strain against the failure number of cycles,
followed an inverted-S shape, while stiffness degradation plots displayed an upright-S shape.
These S-shaped behaviors align with findings from previous studies [16, 19-21].
 Strain measurements on both sides of the specimens indicated uniformly distributed strain
accumulation in almost all cases, with minor differences observed primarily during the final
phase of strain accumulation. This uniformity is attributed to the brittle nature of 3D-printed
concrete, characterized by small aggregate size, stronger cement paste, and high strength
properties. The normalized strain plot versus normalized N f demonstrated that specimens with
similar failure cycles had comparable strain evolution curves.
 The relationship between the average gradient of peak and valley strains, stiffness in phase
II, and the number of cycles to failure was also explored. A linear correlation was found between
the logarithms of these parameters and the logarithm of N f. The high correlation coefficient (R²)
suggests a strong relationship between these factors, and the resulting linear equations can be
used to predict the fatigue capacity of tests that run to completion.
 The study on 3D-printed concrete under uniaxial compression revealed important insights
into the behavior of this innovative material under cyclic loading. These findings highlighted the
potential of 3D-printed concrete for high-cycle fatigue applications, especially in structures such
as wind turbine towers1.
 Figure 22 illustrates the stress-strain behavior observed during fatigue tests on 3D-printed
concrete.

cylinder to monotonic compressive loading, along with a few cycles of hysteresis and peak and valley

compression - ACI Material Journal.
 29

stress and strain development over the cycles. Right side; Strain evolution plot showing peak and valley

2.2.6 Effect of Coarse Aggregate Size, Shape, and Type on Fatigue
 This part of the study explored the strain-related fatigue characteristics of four different
types of concrete through uniaxial compressive cyclic tests. A new improved strength estimation
method (ISEM) was employed to more accurately predefine the fatigue load protocol,
minimizing the influence of strength variation on the test outcomes. Below is a summary of the
key findings from the experiments and analysis.
 The progression of peak and valley strains, along with the degradation of secant stiffness
under cyclic loading, was examined. The results revealed the three typical phases of strain
development and stiffness degradation, which are consistent with previous observations in
conventional concrete. Strain evolution plots, which show strain against the number of cycles to
failure, followed an inverted-S shape, while stiffness degradation plots exhibited an upright-S
shape. These S-shaped behaviors align with findings from earlier studies [16, 19-21].
 The study also investigated the correlation between the average gradient of peak and valley
strains, stiffness in phase II, and the number of cycles to failure (N f). A linear relationship was
identified between the logarithms of these parameters and the logarithm of N f. The high
correlation coefficient (R²) confirms a strong relationship, and these linear equations were used
to predict the fatigue capacity of run-out tests.
 The experimental campaign also successfully identified the timing of visual crack
formation on the surface of the concrete under fatigue loading and examined its relationship with
strain evolution stages and fatigue capacity. Using two GoPro cameras to capture images at
regular intervals, the moment the first visual crack appeared was pinpointed accurately. The
corresponding average strain at this point was deemed the critical strain value for crack
initiation.
 The analysis showed that for river-based coarse aggregate concrete, the peak strain points
associated with the first visual crack typically fell within the strain range corresponding to the
concrete's strength. However, in lightweight concrete, the first visual crack appeared earlier,
indicating its brittle nature under monotonic loading. Additionally, it was observed that visual
cracks typically formed around 70% to 80% of the concrete's fatigue life, just before the
transition to phase III of strain evolution. Higher minimum stress ratios during fatigue loading
resulted in earlier crack formation, underscoring the impact of loading conditions on fatigue
behavior.
 This investigation examined the impact of coarse aggregate size, shape and type on the
fatigue performance of concrete. Tests were conducted on specimens with varying aggregate
sizes, including river base round aggregates, crushed angular granite aggregates, and concrete
with 20% replacement with lightweight aggregate. The results showed that concrete with round
shape and smaller size aggregates exhibited greater fatigue capacity, which was likely due to a
reduction in surface area exposed to microcracking. Strain evolution behavior was similar across
aggregate sizes, but the stiffness degradation and final strain varied depending on the aggregate
composition. These findings are critical for selecting concrete mixes in fatigue-sensitive
structures like bridges and offshore platforms 1.

1
 For more information, please refer to the Appendix I - Uniaxial compressive fatigue behavior of concrete with
different aggregate size and type.

fatigue capacity for varying aggregate sizes during compressive fatigue testing.

2.3 Variable Amplitude Testing on Plain Concrete
 The Variable Amplitude Testing (VAT) on Plain Concrete consisted of loading in two,
three, or five stages, with the load sequence going from high to low S max stress levels for one test
in each pair, and from low to high Smax stress levels in the other test of each pair. The objective
of this testing was to determine if the sequence of loads had any impact on fatigue capacity. It
was the hypothesis that having higher stresses occur first (e.g. hurricane occurring in first year of
operation) would be more detrimental than if these higher stresses occurred near the end of the
wind farm operational period. The basis for this hypothesis is that the higher early stresses would

 31

lead to greater damage/cracking making the effect of subsequent lower stresses being more
impactful on capacity utilization than if the order was reversed. This matter was discussed in
detail with members of the project team and advisors as well as others engaged in the
discussions; without exception, everyone felt that having the higher stresses occur in the early
part of the testing would lead to less fatigue capacity than in reverse. When asked why
Palmgren-Miner’s rule continued to be used despite this, the universal response was that it is the
only tool available to designers, certification verification agents (CVA), and regulators, and that
a new approach was needed.
 The results from a single two-stage test and a single three-stage test are presented below,
and these are generally representative of results from all other VATs.
 Figure 24(a) presents the results from a two-stage VAT where the S max of 0.80 is imposed
first (for 2800 cycles) and then the specimen failed after 158,558 cycles with an Smax of 0.70.
Figure 24(b) presents the results from a reverse loading in which an S max of 0.70 is imposed first
(for 172,801 cycles), and then the specimen failed after 16,037 cycles at an S max of 0.80. Smin was
0.075 in both cases. Using the fib Model Code and Palmgren-Miner’s rule, the fatigue capacity
ratio (summation of Ni/Nf) when the higher Smax was imposed first was 3.12, and the fatigue
capacity ratio when the lower Smax was imposed first was 8.91. Thus, the impact of sequencing
was found to affect the fatigue capacity by a factor of 2.86 (8.91/3.12).

 (a) Strain Evolution when Higher Smax is Imposed First

 (b) Strain Evolution when Lower Smax is Imposed First
 Figure 24 Strain Evolution and Number of Imposed Cycles in a Two-Stage VAT
 Figure 25(a) presents the results from a three-stage VAT where the S max of 0.80 is imposed
first (for 1553 cycles), followed by Smax of 0.75 for 5297 cycles, and then the specimen failed
after 2758 cycles at Smax of 0.70. Figure 25(b) presents the results from a reverse loading in
which Smax of 0.70 is imposed first (for 18,573 cycles), followed by S max of 0.75 (for 7275
cycles), and then the specimen failed after 2353 cycles at S max of 0.70. Smin was 0.075 in both
cases. Using the fib Model Code and Palmgren-Miner’s rule, the fatigue capacity ratio when the

 32

higher Smax was imposed first was 2.07, and the fatigue capacity ratio when the lower S max was
imposed first was 67.18. Thus, the impact of sequencing was found to affect the fatigue capacity
by a factor of 32.4 (67.18/2.07).
 The objective of these illustrations was not to make an overall comment on the
conservatism of the fib Model Code or to suggest that a three-stage loading is more impactful on
the difference than is a two-stage loading. However, the general effect presented here, and most
of the other results, do confirm the hypothesis that sequencing matters and that the Palmgren-
Miner’s linear damage accumulation rule does not hold for concrete.

 (a) Strain Evolution when Higher Smax is Imposed First

 (b) Strain Evolution when Lower Smax is Imposed First
 Figure 25 Strain Evolution in a 3-stage Variable Amplitude Test
2.4 Constant Amplitude Testing on Reinforced Concrete
 This study presents selected test data on fatigue capacity, stress-strain response, and visual
observations of damage from the testing of the Reinforced Concrete (RC) cylinders. Four of the
most significant findings are presented below:
 (1) Effect of Longitudinal Reinforcement on Fatigue Capacity
 The use of longitudinal reinforcement was found to have a dramatic effect on the number
of load cycles to failure as shown in Figure 26 for the ratios of S max/Smin = 0.80/0.05. Six test
results are shown, 4 for plain concrete specimens, and 2 for specimens with 4 #3 longitudinal
bars. As shown, the addition of reinforcing bars was shown to increase the fatigue capacity by a
factor of 5-10.

 33

 Figure 26 Comparing the Number of Cycles to Failure in Plain and Reinforced Specimens with
                                     Smax/Smin = 0.80/0.05
 (2) Plausible Explanation for Benefit of Longitudinal Reinforcement
 A likely explanation for this much long fatigue life for specimens with longitudinal
reinforcement is that concrete softens considerably even in the early segment of cyclic loading
leading to a transfer of load/stress from the concrete to the longitudinal reinforcement. The
reduction in compressive stress in the concrete is fairly dramatic as shown in Figure 27. This
stress can be calculated from equilibrium using the following expression:

 𝑓 = = 𝑤ℎ𝑒𝑟𝑒 𝑓 = 𝐸 𝜀 < 𝑓
 𝐴𝑟𝑒𝑎 𝑜𝑓 𝐶𝑜𝑛𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑒 𝐴

 34

  Figure 27 Calculated Compressive Stress over the Loading History for S max/Smin = 0.80/0.05 Tests
 Since even a small reduction in compressive stress causes a large increase in fatigue
strength, the large difference in total number of cycles to failure due to the presence of
reinforcement is to have been expected. The fatigue capacity for this range in stresses by the
Model Code can be calculated by dividing this curve into a finite set of stresses and associated
loading cycles as shown in Figure 28 and Table 2.

 35

 Figure 28 Bands for Calculating Fatigue Capacity Utilization
 Table 2 Fatigue Capacity Utilization Based on Model Code

 (3) Impact of Reinforcement on Uniformity of Axial Straining Around Cylinder
Circumference
 In the testing on plain concrete cylinders, a significant variation in axial straining around
the circumference of the concrete cylinders was frequently observed. This was expected because
of the natural variation in the stiffness of the concrete that would require more strain in the less
stiff regions to produce the same level of stress as required because of the use of a spherical seat
at the top of the cylinder (see Figure 14) that ensures a non-moment condition along the
centerline of the cylinder. With increasing levels of axial stress in these cylinders, straining and
damage often concentrated on one part of the plain concrete cylinder, and then the failure
initiated from this local region of damage. The pattern of deformation and straining needed for
this was not consistent with what would occur within the critically stressed region (or any region

 36

for that matter) of a large continuous structure for which the kinematic restraints of the
surrounding concrete would force a more uniform distribution of strain. The use of at least four
equally spaced longitudinally reinforcing bars around the perimeter of the cylinder substantially
reduced the variation in axial compressive straining across the cross-section; this reduced the
likelihood that a less stiff and weaker region could precipitate a failure. This observation raised
questions about how testing should be done on plain concrete. To better represent the state of
stress and kinematic constraints, the ideal test would have the use of a spherical seat to address
the non-parallelism of the ends of the cylinders, and that this seat would then be locked into
position once uniform stress conditions have been imposed and the test is past end seating effect.
 (4) Prediction of Compressive Stresses in Design
 While the compressive stress that is applied in fatigue testing can be easily calculated from
the known axial load, measured straining, and stress-strain characteristics of the reinforcement,
determining how best to estimate these values in the design process is more challenging. Based
on discussions with designers and Certification and Verification Agencies (CVA) 1, most
consider concrete to be a linear elastic material with some designers accounting for the stiffness
of the reinforcing steel while others do not. Some also consider the non-linear stress strain
response of concrete as well as some aspects of time dependent behavior either through creep or
a loss in stiffness due to the effects of fatigue loading 2.

2.5 Variability in Experimental Test Data and Impact of f’c
 The collected data is archived in an Excel sheet, where plots and tables have been
established so that the results can be viewed in the context of existing data and its significance
can be established. The results of the tests were used to evaluate, develop, and propose changes
to fatigue standards and models. Throughout the research project, the research team added new
data to the database. Figure 29 shows S-N models and the corresponding experimental data
points for fatigue tests with varying stress levels for a small range of minimum stress level,
which highlights the large scatter in results. Due to lack of data provided about the concrete mix
and loading protocol, the reasons for this huge scatter in the fatigue capacity is unknown. One
significantly contributing factor to this scatter is the variability in the concrete monotonic
compressive strength which has the effect that the actual loading ratios (Smax/Smin) were not
the intended loading ratios.

2.5.1 Significance of Concrete Compressive Strength in Fatigue Testing
 Concrete fatigue testing involves repeatedly loading specimens to measure their durability.
Small errors in estimating monotonic compressive strength, such as a 5% variation, can lead to
large discrepancies in the predicted fatigue life. This variability is particularly critical in fatigue
tests, where the number of cycles to failure is sensitive to changes in material properties.

1
  Certification verification agencies, like DNV (Det Norske Veritas), play a crucial role in the offshore wind industry
by ensuring that the design, construction, and operation of wind structures meet established standards and regulatory
requirements. Their primary responsibilities include Standards Compliance, Independent Assessment, Risk
Management, Quality Assurance, Type and Project Certification, Insurance and Financing, & Operational Lifetime
Assessment.
2
  For more information, refer to the Appendix G - High Cycle Fatigue Behavior of Reinforced Concrete - fib Oslo
Conference.
 37

Figure 29 S-N models and experimental data points for a minimum stress level of 0.05 [17, 19, 28-31,
 33, 37-45]
 Figure 29 illustrates the relationship between stress (S) and the number of cycles to failure
(Nf). Different researchers using varying assumptions for concrete monotonic compressive
strength produce significantly different predictions for fatigue life. This variability emphasizes
the need for accurate strength estimation methods in concrete fatigue testing.
 For instance, a set of compressive fatigue tests on 3D printed concrete cylinders were done
at Tufts University in the summer of 2022. Figure 30 shows the monotonic compressive test
results of a few of these cylinders with a high variation in the final strength; the coefficient of
variation is very high at 11%. The high variation in the strength was not preventable due to the
nature of the 3D printed concrete mixture and production process. The importance of this method
of defining the fatigue loading based on this protocol is highlighted in this case. Figure 30 shows
that fatigue test #7 has the closest stress-strain behavior to monotonic compressive test result #5.
Thus, the estimated strength of this specimen for defining the fatigue loading protocol using
ISEM method would be 7400 psi (rather than 6800 psi, which is the average strength of this
batch of concrete).

estimation can result in a 3-5 fold variation in predicted cycles to failure, highlighting the
significant impact of small deviations. This underscores the importance of minimizing errors in
strength estimation to achieve more reliable results in fatigue testing.

2.5.3 Conclusion
 The monotonic compressive strength of concrete is a critical factor that influences the
variability of fatigue test results. Errors in strength estimation, even as small as 5%, can lead to
significant differences in predicted fatigue life. Ensuring accurate strength estimation is essential
to reduce variability and improve the reliability of concrete fatigue testing 1&2&3&4.

2.6 Impact of Aggregate Type and Size on Compressive Fatigue Behavior
 This part of the study examined the influence of aggregate size and type on concrete
fatigue capacity. which was observed to have a very significant effect. This summary discusses
findings from experimental tests and analyses on how different aggregate sizes and types affect
the fatigue capacity of concrete.

2.6.1 Aggregate Types and Fatigue Performance
 The aggregate type plays a significant role in determining the fatigue capacity of concrete.
In the experimental tests conducted, three primary types of aggregates were used: river-based
round coarse aggregate (RBAC), crushed granite angular coarse aggregate (CGAC), and
lightweight coarse aggregate (LWAC). The mix design, mechanical properties, and fatigue test
results demonstrated that RBAC generally had better fatigue performance compared to CGAC
and LWAC. This is attributed to the smoother surface of RBAC, which leads to better bonding
with the cement paste.
 Figure 31 illustrates the gradation results of the river-based round aggregate (RBAC),
which was used in the concrete mixes for fatigue testing. The smoother surface of these
aggregates allowed for better bonding with the cement paste, contributing to improved fatigue
performance.

  For more information, refer to the Appendix E - Experimental investigation of concrete fatigue in axial
compression - ACI Structural Journal.
2
  For more information, refer to the Appendix F - Concrete compressive strain behavior and magnitudes under
uniaxial fatigue loading - Construction and Building Materials Journal.
3
  For more information, refer to the Appendix H - Fatigue performance of 3D printed concrete under uniaxial
compression - ACI Material Journal.
4
  For more information, refer to the Appendix I - Uniaxial compressive fatigue behavior of concrete with different
aggregate size and type.
