 Figure 31 River-based round aggregate gradation results

2.6.2 Influence of Aggregate Size and Type on Fatigue Capacity
 The type of aggregates also plays a crucial role in the fatigue performance of concrete.
Lightweight aggregate tended to result in higher fatigue capacity compared to regular aggregates
such as 3/4-inch crushed granite used in CGAC which confirms the effect of mechanical
characteristics of aggregates on the overall characteristics of the concrete.
 The size of aggregates and their shape also has effect on the fatigue capacity. Smaller
round aggregates lead to higher fatigue capacity compared to large crushed angular aggregates.
The increased surface area of larger aggregates can lead to more rapid crack initiation and
propagation, thereby reducing the overall fatigue life. CGAC had a more angular shape, which
led to a higher surface area and quicker initiation of fatigue cracks compared to the smoother
RBAC.
 The fatigue tests conducted on concrete specimens with different aggregates revealed
distinct differences in fatigue performance. Results show the fatigue capacity (number of cycles
to failure, Nf) varied significantly between the aggregate types. In general, river-based round
aggregates (RBAC) exhibited higher fatigue life than crushed granite aggregates (CGAC), with
lightweight aggregates (LWAC) performing the worst.
 Figure 32 and Figure 33 compare the fatigue capacity of concrete specimens made with
river-based round aggregate (RBAC), crushed granite angular aggregate (CGAC), and
lightweight aggregate (LWAC). RBAC exhibited the highest fatigue life, followed by CGAC and
LWAC.

 41

Figure 32 S-N curve plot for uniaxial compressive fatigue testing with Smin of %5

Figure 33 S-N curve plot for uniaxial compressive fatigue testing with Smin of %20

 42

2.6.3 Influence of Aggregate Type on Crack Initiation
 Two cameras were set on both sides of the specimen to take and record photos of the
cylinder with predefined time intervals to monitor the appearance of the visual crack. Then, the
results were post-processed, and the peak strain associated with the visual crack initiation was
identified and plotted below. The type of aggregate significantly impacts the initiation and
propagation of cracks during fatigue loading. Concrete made with lightweight aggregates
(LWAC) showed a brittle behavior, where visual cracks appeared earlier in the fatigue life
compared to concrete with larger and denser aggregates, such as those in CGAC and RBAC.
This early crack initiation in LWAC is attributed to the weaker bond strength between the
aggregate and the cement paste.
 Figure 34, Figure 35, and Figure 36 illustrate the crack initiation points in concrete
specimens with different aggregate types and sizes. Concrete with lightweight aggregate
(LWAC) showed cracks at an earlier stage compared to river-based round aggregate (RBAC)
and crushed granite angular aggregate (CGAC).

 base aggregate concrete

 granite coarse aggregate concrete

 Figure 36 Peak strain related to the stag of visual crack appearance due to fatigue loading for
 lightweight coarse aggregate concrete

2.6.4 Conclusion
 The type and size of aggregate used in concrete have a significant impact on the fatigue
capacity of the material. Concrete made with river-based round aggregates (RBAC) showed the
highest fatigue life, while lightweight aggregates (LWAC) exhibited the lowest fatigue capacity
due to early crack initiation due to presence of lightweight aggregates. Understanding the role of
aggregate type and size in fatigue performance is crucial for designing durable concrete
structures, especially those subjected to cyclic loading 1.

3 Project Products
3.1 Introduction
 Part 3 provides a description of key products (or deliverables) from this project. 3.2
presents the fatigue database, how it is organized, can be used, and examples of what can be
learned from this. 3.3 presents lessons learned and key takeaways about the value and limitations
of experiment test data, and how additional tools are needed by the research and design
community to advance fatigue models and to enable the design of efficient and reliable
structures. 3.4 presents a description of the Technotes to be reviewed, approved, and published
by the Fatigue Committee of the American Concrete Institute (ACI215). Finally, 3.5 provides a
description of the primary publications stemming from this project.

3.2 Experimental Fatigue Database
 This section provides a summary of the process of database creation, and the analysis done
by the authors. This includes analysis of the data by visualization (plotting), statistical analysis,
and, at the end, the use of machine learning and neural network. This will lay out the basis for
the necessity of recommended protocols and practices through ACI Technotes.
 The Concrete Fatigue Test Database (database) provides a comprehensive collection of
experimental data on concrete's performance under cyclic loading. The database, which includes
data on the number of cycles to failure (Nf) under varying stress conditions, is designed to

1
 For more information, please refer to the Appendix I - Uniaxial compressive fatigue behavior of concrete with
different aggregate size and type.
 44

improve the understanding of fatigue behavior in concrete. The goal was to support the
development of reliable fatigue capacity prediction models for different concrete mixes,
geometries, and testing conditions.
 The database has collected fatigue test data from various studies. It accommodates
different test variables, such as specimen geometry, material properties, and fatigue loading
regimes. Data is stored in U.S. Customary Units (USCU) and SI units, ensuring flexibility for
global use. The database includes monotonic stress-strain behavior, fatigue loading plans, and
failure results. Researchers can compare their data with characteristic S-N curves from various
standards, such as DNV and Model Code 2010.
 As stated earlier, Figure 37 provides an overview of the organization of tabs in the
experimental test data repository. The first tab contains the main data entry for fatigue
experiments, while subsequent tabs offer comparison features with different S-N models.

 Figure 37 Screenshot #1 of the experimental test data repository – Tabs definition
 The data collected in the repository highlights several important factors that influence
concrete's fatigue performance. The maximum applied stress (S max) and minimum stress (Smin)
levels significantly impact the number of cycles to failure. Additionally, concrete strength,
aggregate size, and water-cement ratio play critical roles in determining fatigue life.
An interesting finding is the impact of loading frequency on fatigue life. Low-frequency cyclic
loading allows microcracks to propagate more effectively, leading to earlier failure. Conversely,
higher frequency loading results in longer fatigue life but greater variability in the results.

3.2.1 Studies on the Database
3.2.1.1 Statistical Analysis
 The database was used to assess the influence of key factors on fatigue capacity (N f). For
instance, a general hypothesis is that in low-frequency cyclic loading (less than 1 Hz), concrete
microcracks will have a chance to propagate more effectively and quickly to macrocracks, which
will lead the process to failure. i.e. in the high frequency of loading (more than 1 Hz) will lead to
a high number of cycles of failure with respect to the low ones. The analysis of the results shows
that it is true. Table 4 shows that there is a considerable difference between the data sets A1 and
A2 comparing the mean and standard deviation.

 The database includes statistical analysis tools for examining the data. This allows users to
identify outliers, which are test results that fall far outside the expected range. Outliers may
occur due to variations in test conditions, errors in testing, or unexpected material behaviors. The
repository provides the ability to filter outliers and re-run tests to validate the data.
 Figure 40 compares experimental test data with characteristic S-N models from standards
and Model Code 2010. The figure shows the deviation of real test data from these models,
emphasizing the need for accurate and well-documented test results to refine fatigue life
predictions.

Figure 40 Comparison of different specified data sets with Model Code 2010 characteristic S-N model
3.2.1.2 Use of Regression and Neural Network Models
 As has been discussed previously, design code provisions, be that an equation or a S-N
fatigue curve, are frequently empirical which means that an equation (or curve) is set so that the
design requirement will mostly result in a conservative estimate of capacity based on the
experimental test data. The use of a neural network (NN) has the potential of providing for a
greatly improved estimate of capacity, which uses a portion of the available data to train a neural
network (relationship between parameters value) to minimum predictive error, which in this case
is the number of cycles to failure for plain concrete of the remaining dataset. A large portion of
the uniaxial test data has at least 6 parameters reported (Max Aggregate Size, water to cement
ratio, Strength, Smax, Smin, Frequency of loading). The larger the number of parameters, the
better the chance of having a better predictive NN model (minimize predictive error). The
number of parameters influencing capacity is often very large, but the number of parameters that
can be included in a design formula for practical reasons is limited. Like the regression models,
5-fold cross-validation is used to validate the model. A sensitivity analysis on the number of
layers, size of layers, type of activation function, etc. was performed to choose the model with
the lowest Mean Squared Error (MSE). If there is a relationship between collected metadata, and
a result, then this will be picked up by an Artificial Neural Network (ANN). Interestingly, the
NN was not able to perform better than regression models and it gave higher MSE and lower R 2.
This can be due to several scenarios; (i) Dataset is small, and NNs struggle to generalize due to
the need for substantial data, whereas regression models can perform well with fewer
parameters. (ii) NNs tend to overfit easily, especially with high variance or noise in data,
whereas simpler regression models with regularization can capture underlying trends more
 48

effectively. (iii) Poor feature scaling, insufficient hyperparameter tuning, and limited
computational resources can further hinder NN performance, while regression models are more
robust and efficient in these aspects.
 Unfortunately, an ANN does not lead to a general formula that is suitable for use in design
practice. As mentioned before, the standard model predictions are mostly conservative. Figure 41
shows the level of variation in the predictions in the higher Smax level (higher than 0.8) where
most of the experimental test data and the conservativeness and un-conservativeness of
predictions over different Smax levels using DNV fatigue model. Figure 42 presents the same
comparison for the NN model generated based on the available data set. Unfortunately, the
variation of the prediction is still high. Based on the literature review on the concrete beam shear
study, it is expected to see a tight band cluster around the prediction capacity ratio of 1. Even
though it shows a tighter band of variation, the variation is still very high. This shows the
incompleteness of the data set. In other words, if all possible parameters were reported for all the
data points, there was a better chance to develop a more comprehensive model. Moreover, there
is a chance that there are other influential parameters that we are missing from the data set. This
will highlight the importance of the development of a standard or some sort of a check sheet for
design certification by testing rather than having an uncertain predictive model.

 Figure 41 The ratio of predicted fatigue life by DNV model to the actual test data versus Smax

 49

 Figure 42 The ratio of predicted fatigue life by NN model to the actual test data versus Smax

Figure 43 The ratio of predicted fatigue life by NN model to the actual test data versus frequency of
 loading (Hz)

 50

The Concrete Fatigue Test Database that was developed through this project offers a valuable
resource for those studying the fatigue behavior of concrete. By collecting and standardizing
experimental data, the repository supports the development of more accurate fatigue life
prediction models. The integration of regression models and neural networks further enhances
the ability to predict fatigue life, though challenges remain in accounting for the wide variability
in concrete fatigue performance1.

3.3 Lessons Learned from the database analysis and reasons for the
 recommended practices in ACI Technotes
3.3.1 Risk of Unnecessary Conservatism due to Very Low Fatigue Capacities Measured
 by Others
 Figure 44 presents a subset of this test data for the case in which the lower end of the stress
range was 5% of the one-time loaded (monotonic) strength of the concrete. This figure shows
that as the maximum applied stress as a fraction of the monotonic strength (S max) increases (as
shown on y-axis), the fatigue capacity (number of cycles to failure) decreases (as shown on x-
axis in Log base 10 scale).

1
 For more information, refer to the Appendix A - Concrete Fatigue Test Data Base.
 51

 Figure 44 Example of fatigue test results plot developed based on initial database
 The results from the experiments (shown as markers points) illustrate that there is a three
order-of-magnitude range (factor of 1000) in the measured fatigue capacity for the many S max
stress levels where Smin is a constant. The fatigue capacity by five different standards or models
(shown by the solid lines) were found to differ by up to a factor of 10. The likely reason for the
very large differences in these models is that different subsets of data were used by committees
and researchers, and that different perspectives were taken in setting lower-bound capacities.
There are several possible explanations for the “outlying” low fatigue capacities that were
obtained. These reasons include: 1. Impact of Variability of Monotonic Cylinder Strength; 2.
Impact of Loading Rate; 3. Effect of Loading Frequency; 4. Effect of Waveform: 5. Effect of
Different Aggregate Types; and 6. Effect of a Weak Region. Given the influence of these six
factors and other factors (e.g. mix design, geometric factors, degree of saturation), it is not
surprising that there is a very wide range in measured fatigue capacities for the same
combination of Smax and Smin values from across different experimental research programs, and
also different models.
 While the variation in the tests conducted in this project at Tufts University and the
University of Illinois were much narrower (often within a factor of 5 and usually much higher
than the lower end of capacities shown in Figure 44), the low fatigue capacities measured by
others cannot be dismissed without any logical reason.

 52

3.3.2 Sensitivity of the Fatigue Capacity of Concrete Material to the Types of Concrete
 and Levels of Reinforcement
 The compressive response and fatigue capacity of concrete is sensitive to many factors
including the size, stiffness, and shape of large aggregates, the water/cement ratio which has a
major effect on the monotonic compressive strength, as well as the type, arrangement, and
amounts of reinforcements. The effect of these factors was investigated by conducting
experiments on three types of concrete and a couple of reinforcement conditions.

3.3.3 Potential Reasons for Low-Outliers and the Large Scatter
 1. Impact of Variability of Monotonic Cylinder Strength: The magnitude of the applied
loading (in kips or kN) to apply in a test for peak and valley stresses is calculated as F max = Smax
x f'c x Ac and Fmin = Smin x f'c x Ac where f'c is the monotonic strength and Ac is the cross-
sectional area of the test specimen. The value of f'c is being taken as the average from a few
monotonic test results. Any individual cylinder that is cast from the same batch may have
monotonic strength that differs meaningfully from this average, up to 5% in some cases, and this
will have a significant effect on fatigue capacity. For example, when the researcher may think
that they are applying an Smax of 0.80, they would actually be applying an S max of 0.84 on a
cylinder if that cylinder happened to have a monotonic strength that is 5% less than the mean.
For many fatigue models, this would result in a fatigue capacity that is expected to be about 5
times lower than if it were an Smax of 0.80. (refer to Table 3)
 2. Impact of Loading Rate: The monotonic compressive strength of concrete increases with
the rate of loading/stressing (psi or MPa per second). In most fatigue tests, typically done at 2Hz
or greater, the rate of stress is more than 500 times the rate of loading that is required to be used
in standardized monotonic testing (approximately 35 psi/second; 0.25 MPa/second). This higher
rate of loading is expected to result in a test strength that is 10 percent or so higher than when the
standardized cylinder loading rate is used. Thereby, the loading ratios that we use for S max and
Smin (fc_applied)/(f'c_monotonic from standardized cylinder test) is not really the correct stress ratio
if the effect of the loading rate were to be considered.
 3. Effect of Loading Frequency: The most common loading frequency used in testing is 2-
3 Hz, whereas the natural frequency of support structures is typically in the range of 0.2 – 0.4
Hz. While accelerated testing is needed to yield data in a reasonable amount of time, it is also
true that the longer the stress in the specimen is near the peak stress, the more time cracks will
have to propagate and progress damage. Thereby, the fatigue capacity (number of cycles to
failure) is expected to be overestimated from most test data (in which higher testing frequencies
were used).
 4. Effect of Waveform: The fatigue capacity when the loading is a triangular waveform has
been shown to be about twice that when a sinusoidal waveform is used and twelve times longer
than when a square waveform [46]. This provides further evidence that the longer that the stress
is at a high level in each cycle, the lower the fatigue capacity. While a sinusoidal waveform may
be more representative of pattern of stressing in the field, a square waveform may be a more
representative condition to use in accelerated testing because it yields a longer time under peak
stressing (Figure 45).

 53

 Figure 45 Cyclic loading wave forms

 5. Aggregate Type: The larger, sharper, and stiffer the aggregate, the lower is the expected
fatigue capacity because of the sensitivity of crack formation and propagation to stress
concentrations and planes of weakness.
 6. Effect of a Weakened Region: Fatigue damage in a cylinder will begin in a particular
region and this will result in a reduction in the stiffness of the concrete in this region. A spherical
seat is used in compression testing to produce a plane stress condition. As a consequence of these
two considerations, an area of weakened and softened concrete across a cross-section is
subjected to the same state of stress than in the other areas over this cross-section. This is in
contrast to the condition in most real structures because if an area of relatively local weakness or
softening develops, stresses will be “redistributed” because the boundary conditions are usually
closer to imposing a plane strain condition than a plane stress condition. As a result, the fatigue
capacity of parts of larger structures in the field may be significantly larger than what is
measured in standardized tests.
 Given the influence of the six factors listed above, other factors not mentioned (e.g. mix
design, geometric factors, degree of saturation), it is not surprising that there is a very wide range
in measured fatigue capacities (number cycles to failure) for the same combination of Smax and
Smin values from across different experimental research produce. This scatter is three orders of
magnitude for the broad database collected as part of this project.
 Consequently, what is deemed to be a safe model depends on what subset of test data is
used to assess this model, and where the model developers felt was a reasonable line to use for
establishing a lower bound to the reliable strength. It is for these reasons that the database is
published along this report to allow users to select subsets of the data to view, adjust parameters
in models, and understand the existing database for further analysis in their design. These
reasons also motivated the authors to develop a few sets of recommendations with consultation

 54

with academic and industry experts to create a path for more standardized testing and
development of a methodology for drawing fatigue models based on experimental test results.

3.4 ACI Technotes
 A task group was established within ACI Committee 215 at ACI Convention in Boston
(fall 2023) on Fatigue to shepherd this through the development and revision of two technotes.
The titles of these technotes were chosen to be “Technote 4 - ACI 215 Recommended Practice
for Compression Fatigue Testing of Concrete”, and “ACI Technote 5: Recommended Procedure
for Development of a S-N Curve for a Specific Concrete Mix”. The numbering is based on the
process of ACI committee 215.

3.4.1 ACI Technote #4 - Recommended Practice for Compression Fatigue Testing of
 Concrete
3.4.1.1 Changes in Approach and Reasons for Change
 As mentioned, the misreporting of data in concrete fatigue testing research frequently
results in unreliable conclusions and inconsistent outcomes across different studies. One of the
important factors that has a huge impact on the concrete fatigue testing result is the assumed
strength of the specimen. The variability of the strength has a considerable impact on the fatigue
loading protocol setup and, consequently on the final fatigue life observed during the testing. To
address this issue, the Improved Strength Estimation Method (ISEM) and its Neural Network-
based variant (NNISEM) were introduced. These methods aim to improve the accuracy of
predicting concrete's fatigue life by carefully accounting for key influencing factors and ensuring
thorough data documentation. ISEM employs five distinct base datasets to boost prediction
accuracy, reducing the average error to 2.68% compared to 3.57% with traditional methods.
NNISEM further enhances precision by using augmented data and optimizing hyperparameters,
lowering the average error to 1.92%. Both approaches highlight the importance of detailed
reporting, such as mix design, curing processes, and strain measurement, to increase the
reliability and repeatability of fatigue test outcomes. Standardized protocols are essential for
deepening the understanding of concrete's behavior under fatigue loading and creating more
accurate predictive models.

3.4.1.2 Recommended Protocol for Compression Fatigue Testing
 ACI Technote #4 outlines the recommended practice for compression fatigue testing of
concrete. The protocol emphasizes a standardized approach to testing and generating data for
creating S-N curves, accounting for variations in concrete strength and ensuring that fatigue life
predictions are specific to the tested concrete mix.
 The process includes conducting monotonic compression tests to establish a baseline for
fatigue loading, followed by fatigue tests at defined stress levels (S max and Smin). These stress
levels are calculated based on the concrete's monotonic strength, with adjustments made to
account for variations in individual test specimens.
 Figure 46 illustrates the recommended practice for compression fatigue testing of concrete,
highlighting the key steps involved in conducting fatigue tests and generating S-N curves
tailored to specific concrete mixes.
 55

 Figure 46 Improved Strength Estimate Method
 A NN model was trained from a subset of 30 monotonic tests on concrete samples. The
final trained model used data augmentation, hyperparameter tuning, and cross-validation to
choose 5/6 of the data randomly to train the data and use 5 data to test it. Table 6 shows the
NNISEM method consistently performs well in training the model and error percentage in
predicting the actual strength is 1.92% on average compared to 2.68% of ISEM method, which is
a “visual fit” method defined in experimental testing program section, and 3.57% of using
Average Strength method, which we use the average strength of the monotonic test as the
assumed strength of all the specimens.
 Figure 47 shows the error in the prediction of strength as bar plots. The blue and red bars
are the trained and test data, respectively, using the NN method to predict the strength. The gray
bars are the amount of error considering the average strength as the strength of all the specimens.
The average error using NNISEM method is shown as dashed lines, and the average error in
Average Strength method is shown as solid lines.
 Table 6 NN model performance on average
 Average percentage error
 Method
 Train model Test model
                        NNISEM                                  1.92%              5.56%
  Average Strength method (assuming the average strength) 3.57% 5.41%

 56

 Figure 47 Bar-plot comparing the error of the NNISEM model and Average Strength method
3.4.1.3 Recommended Data Acquisition in the Future
 Unfortunately, most of the published papers on concrete fatigue subject that presents
experimental data lacks critical basic information about the important parameters. To tackle this
issue and have a baseline for experimental fatigue testing and reporting on concrete, the
following items are recommended to be reported:
 i.  Reporting of mix design, including the amount, type, and maximum size of coarse
 aggregate
 ii.  Curing regime and moisture conditions at the time of testing
 iii. Reporting of strain evolution and how this was measured
  iv. Reporting of type of observed failure
 v. Reporting on when the first external damage was observed
  vi. Cast cylinders months before fatigue testing is started so that there is little strength gain
 over the duration of testing (at least 90 days)
 vii. Recommendations for ensuring the inclusion of a test result in a future evaluation
 database (for discussion)
 • Items (ii) – (v) above
 • 2-3 measurements of compressive straining around the circumference
 • The cylinder did not have irregular surface conditions
 The critical importance of accurate data representation in concrete fatigue testing to ensure
reliable and consistent results is highlighted. The development of the Improved Strength
Estimation Method (ISEM) and its Neural Network-based variant (NNISEM) provides a
significant advancement in predicting concrete's fatigue life, with ISEM reducing the average
prediction error to 2.68% and NNISEM lowering it further to 1.92% through data augmentation
and hyperparameter tuning. These methods, which leverage both traditional engineering and
 57

modern data science techniques, enhance the accuracy of strength predictions by accounting for
variations in test conditions and material properties. The study emphasizes the need for
standardized reporting of experimental conditions—such as mix design, curing regime, and
strain evolution—to improve reproducibility and comparability across studies. This
comprehensive approach to testing and documentation can significantly improve the
understanding of concrete behavior under fatigue loading, leading to more reliable predictive
models and better engineering practices1.

3.4.2 ACI Technote #5 - Recommended Procedure for Development of a S-N Curve for
 a Specific Concrete Mix
 A standardized approach to developing S-N curves for concrete through compressive
fatigue testing was developed which enables a greatly improved estimate of the fatigue capacity
of a specific concrete mix. This supports innovation in design practice, and it avoids project
designs being unnecessarily penalized by irrelevant and low fatigue capacity text data.

3.4.2.1 Overview of S-N Curve Development
 Fatigue in concrete refers to the progressive deterioration of the material under repeated
stress. S-N curves, which plot stress levels (S) against the number of cycles to failure (N), are
used to understand the fatigue performance of materials. More than 100 fatigue tests were
conducted on four different concrete types, focusing on compressive fatigue behavior. The tests
varied stress levels and recorded the number of cycles to failure, allowing for the creation of
probabilistic S-N curves that account for variability in fatigue performance. These curves serve
as a reliable guide for engineers when designing concrete structures to withstand repeated
loading.

3.4.2.2 Methodology
 The methodology employed in this study involves six key steps, starting with the
establishment of the monotonic compressive strength of the concrete. Subsequent steps included
short- and long-duration fatigue tests at various stress levels. These tests allow for the refinement
of the S-N curve, which represents the relationship between the applied stress and the number of
cycles to failure. The final S-N curves were developed using both short-duration and long-
duration test data, creating a comprehensive view of fatigue life for each concrete type tested.
This method enables engineers to accurately predict fatigue performance and design more
resilient structures.
 Figure 48 shows an example of the S-N curve development by testing procedure. It
illustrates the process of plotting maximum stress against the number of cycles to failure to
create an accurate representation of fatigue life.

1
 For more information, please refer to the Appendix J - ACI Technote 4 Recommended Protocol for Compression
Fatigue Testing of Concrete using improved strength estimation method.
 58

 Figure 48 S-N curve development method proposed within ACI Technote #5

 Two sets of S-N curves were produced, each for different minimum stress ratios (S min =
0.05 and Smin = 0.20). Figure 49 presents the S-N curve for fatigue testing under different
minimum stress ratios. The curve highlights the decrease in fatigue strength as the number of
cycles to failure increases, providing a detailed understanding of the fatigue behavior of the
concrete specimens.
 The curves in Figure 50 show that as the number of cycles increases, the maximum stress
that the concrete can withstand decreases. River-based round aggregate concrete (RBAC)
showed the steepest decline in fatigue performance, while other concrete types exhibited a more
gradual reduction in strength.

 59

 Figure 49 Development of Two S-N Curves with different Smin ratios

 Figure 50 S-N curve plot for uniaxial compressive fatigue testing with Smin of %5
3.4.2.3 Probabilistic Approach to S-N Curves
 The objective of this work was to incorporate a probabilistic approach to developing S-N
curves that accounts for variability in concrete fatigue performance. By analyzing the
experimental data, probabilistic curves representing different probabilities of failure were
created. These curves allow users to estimate the likelihood of failure at various stress levels,
offering a comprehensive view of potential outcomes. The probabilistic S-N curves provide

 60

valuable insights into how different concrete compositions respond to cyclic loading, allowing
for more informed decisions when designing concrete structures.
 Figure 51 shows the probabilistic S-N curves developed from the experimental data,
illustrating different probabilities of failure (5%, 50%, and 95%). These curves help engineers
assess the risk of failure under various loading conditions.

 Figure 51 Probabilistic S-N curve plot for uniaxial compressive fatigue testing for RBAC
 The standardized methodology for developing compressive fatigue S-N curves for concrete
provides an accurate representation of fatigue life for different concrete types. By incorporating
both short- and long-duration tests, as well as a probabilistic approach, the study offers a
comprehensive view of fatigue performance. The findings are valuable for engineers designing
structures subjected to cyclic loading, ensuring more reliable predictions of concrete durability
and service life1.

3.5 Bibliography of key papers
3.5.1 Papers published
 Viswanath, Somashekar, Daniel A. Kuchma, and James M. LaFave. "Experimental
 investigation of concrete fatigue in axial compression." ACI Structural Journal 118, no. 1
    (2021): 263-276.

1
 For more information, please refer to the Appendix K - ACI Technote 5 Procedure for Developing Concrete
Compressive Fatigue S-N Curve by Testing.
 61

 Abstract: The use of concrete in fatigue critical structures, such as wind turbine towers, has
necessitated the replacement of simplistic (and therefore insufficient) fatigue design
recommendations with more accurate and advanced fatigue models that can consider a greater
complexity of stress states and materials. As the first part of a multistage test series, an
experimental investigation was conducted to determine the fatigue life of concrete subjected to
axial compressive fatigue loading, focusing on the relatively high-cycle fatigue domain. A wide
range of values for maximum stress levels and stress ratios were considered. The experimental
results were compared against existing predictive models, and the most applicable model was
identified. Concrete strain and stiffness behavior during fatigue loading were also investigated
and reported. Based on the observed behavior, a hypothesis for identifying the impending
occurrence of fatigue failure, based on the monotonic stress-strain curve, is examined.
 Viswanath, Somashekar, James M. LaFave, and Daniel A. Kuchma. "Concrete compressive
 strain behavior and magnitudes under uniaxial fatigue loading." Construction and Building
    Materials 296 (2021): 123718.

 Abstract: Recent advances in concrete technology and construction have promoted an
increasing utilization of concrete in fatigue-critical structures, such as wind turbine towers.
While research into concrete fatigue is not a new topic and has been going on for several
decades, a large majority of this research has primarily been focused on the determination of
concrete fatigue capacity. However, understanding and advancing concrete fatigue behavior
(e.g., fatigue stress vs. strain response) has received limited attention, even though this is also
crucial, especially considering the inherently nonlinear behavior of concrete. To address this
knowledge gap and further advance our understanding of concrete fatigue behavior, an
experimental investigation consisting of uniaxial compressive fatigue testing of concrete cylinder
specimens until failure was undertaken, and axial strains were recorded throughout the duration
of fatigue loading. The behavior and evolution of concrete axial strains, including the rate of
accumulation of strains, through the fatigue loading process were extensively analyzed and are
reported. Additionally, the influence of key parameters, such as peak stress levels and number of
cycles to failure, on peak strain evolution were investigated and are described. Magnitudes of
peak axial strain at the end of the stable strain accumulation phase, as well as at failure, are also
reported, and recommendations are made for estimating strain magnitudes at these critical
junctures when conducting fatigue analysis and design. The results help advance the
understanding of concrete axial strain behavior under compressive fatigue loading. The results
also provide guidelines for estimating the magnitudes of concrete strains under fatigue loads,
which in turn will be beneficial to designers, as it allows them to also consider structural
performance during the analysis and design of fatigue critical concrete structures.
 Kuchma, Daniel A., Mohsen Minaeijavid, Somashekar Viswanath, and James M. Lafave.
 "High Cycle Fatigue Behavior of Reinforced Concrete" In 6th fib International Congress on
 Concrete Innovation for Sustainability, 2022, pp. 1720-1729. fib. The International
 Federation for Structural Concrete, 2022.

 Abstract: Towers and foundations that support wind turbines must be able to sustain high-
cycle fatigue stress demands that are caused by waves, winds, and turbine operations. These
demands often have high mean stress levels due to prestressing and/or mean wind speed, they
can range into the millions of cycles of occurrence, and they often control design. In current

 62

practice, the fatigue design life of these structures is determined using “S-N curves” and
Palmgren-Miner’s linear damage accumulation model. While this may be suitable for most steel
structures, it is not suitable for most concrete structures. With funding from the U.S. Department
of Energy, an experimental testing program is examining the impact of steel reinforcement,
strain variation, saturation levels, order of stressing, and other factors on the compressive fatigue
capacity of concrete. This is one of the first significant experimental testing programs to study
the impact of steel reinforcement on the fatigue performance of concrete structures. The results
from completed tests have identified shortcomings in existing fatigue models, and they are being
used to suggest improved means to characterize damage and estimate fatigue life. This research
project also examined fatigue stress demands so to identify the primary sources of fatigue
capacity utilization (demand/capacity for each stress state) and to determine how they can be
mitigated by turbine control settings. This includes the consideration of time dependent behavior
including changes in the stiffness characteristics of structural concrete. This paper presents key
observations and conclusions from the completed experiments, and a discussion of challenges in
both fatigue testing and the development of suitable models and design requirements.

3.5.2 Papers submitted
 Mohsen Minaeijavid, Daniel Kuchma, Fabian B Rodriguez, Jan Olek, Morten Søgaard
 Anderson. “Experimental investigation of fatigue-related characteristics of 3D printed
 concrete under uniaxial compression", Submitted to ACI Material Journal (2024).

 Abstract: 3D-printed concrete has emerged as an important new type of concrete
construction material that is gaining use across many applications including as towers that
support onshore wind turbines, and potentially as foundations for supporting offshore wind
turbines.1. Unlike for most civil infrastructure (e.g. buildings, bridges), the design of support
structures for wind turbines are frequently controlled by high-cycles fatigue demands from wind,
wave, rotor rotation, and aerodynamic imbalances. While significant research has been done on
3D printed concrete materials, [40, 41], this study presented in this paper may be the first to
examine the high-cycle compression fatigue characteristics of 3D-printed concrete. In addition, a
new fatigue loading protocol is presented to reduce the apparent scatter in fatigue capacities.

and the fatigue capacity of concrete under uniaxial compressive fatigue loading. By synthesizing
previous research on concrete fatigue, an assessment was made of significant variations in
fatigue capacity and overlooked internal mechanisms that extend material service life.
Experimental results reveal a strong linear relationship between stiffness degradation in
cylindrical specimens, suggesting potential applications to structural components under specific
stress gradients. While Model Code 2010 incorporates a stress gradient factor, efforts to refine

1

deformation and stiffness degradation characterization are ongoing. This study involved over 500
tests on various concrete mixes, complemented by existing data. Also examined was the strain at
the first visual crack appearance due to fatigue loading, confirming it occurs around the end of

 Mohsen Minaeijavid, Daniel Kuchma, Morten Sogard Andersen. “Recommended Protocol
 for Compression Fatigue Testing of Concrete using improved strength estimation method,

 Abstract: The misrepresentation of data in concrete fatigue testing research often leads to
unreliable conclusions and inconsistent results across studies. To address this, the Improved
Strength Estimation Method (ISEM) and a Neural Network-based variant (NNISEM) were
developed. These methods focus on accurately predicting the fatigue life of concrete by
systematically considering critical influencing factors and ensuring comprehensive data
reporting. ISEM uses five distinct base datasets to enhance prediction accuracy, resulting in a
lower average error of 2.68% compared to the traditional method’s 3.57%. NNISEM further
improves accuracy by using augmented data and hyperparameter tuning, achieving an average
error of 1.92%. These methodologies emphasize the importance of detailed documentation, such
as mix design, curing conditions, and strain measurements, to improve the reliability and

advancing the understanding of concrete behavior under fatigue loading and developing more

 Mohsen Minaeijavid, Daniel Kuchma, Morten Sogard Andersen. “Recommended Protocol
 for Developing Fatigue S-N Curves for Concrete through Compressive Experimental Testing
 ", To be submitted to ACI Material Journal (2024).

 Abstract: This study presents a standardized methodology for developing compressive
fatigue S-N curves for concrete through extensive experimental testing. By conducting 160
fatigue tests on four different concrete types, the research explores the fatigue behavior under
cyclic compressive loading. The testing program varies stress levels and records the number of
cycles to failure, enabling the construction of S-N curves that accurately represent fatigue life.
Additionally, probabilistic S-N curves are generated to account for the variability in fatigue
performance, providing a comprehensive range of outcomes. This approach enhances the
predictability of concrete fatigue behavior, improving structural design and durability. The
results contribute to the existing literature by offering detailed experimental data and
methodologies, addressing gaps related to the fatigue performance of different concrete
compositions. This study is particularly valuable for infrastructure and high-rise buildings
subjected to repetitive loading.

4 Summary and Recommendations for Future Research
4.1 Motivation and Approach to Research
 The variability in wind and wave loadings, and the dynamics of wind turbine operations,
lead to significant cyclic loadings on towers and foundations that support offshore wind turbines.
These fatigue demands due to cyclic loading can control the design of these support structures.
While concrete structures generally have better fatigue resistance than steel structures, the
models and standards that are used for fatigue design and assessment can still result in fatigue
controlling the dimensions of concrete support structures. Fatigue design standards fail to
account for many influencing factors on fatigue capacity (allowable number of stress cycles for a
particular range of stresses) and they are too conservative (excessive level of safety). The goal of
this project was to improve the understanding of concrete compression fatigue, and to create a
design and assessment process that would increase the reliability and cost-effectiveness of
concrete support structures for wind turbines.
 The fatigue models in standards that we use in design and analysis practice are empirical
and derived from experimental test data. Thereby, the first major step in this project was the
collection and analysis of all available data in the public literature. This revealed that the
available test data on the concrete compressive fatigue capacity of concrete is quite limited. This
project then examined the potential effect of key variables through a preliminary testing
program, which was used to inform the subsequent test series. While the results from the
experiments were quite tightly banded for fatigue (number of measured cycles to failure for any
 65

particularly Smax/Smin stress range often being within a factor of 5), the scatter for a particular
stress range in the full collected database (i.e. tests from all researchers) could be more than 1000
(>3 orders of magnitude). The research team first tried to eliminate low outliers by repeating the
test of others, and then creating a more comprehensive model to account for other factors. This
did not come close to addressing this problem, which is that concrete mix details and testing
procedures from previous experimental testing programs were not provided in sufficient detail to
be able to remove outliers or to consider factors not reported by the researchers. Because of this,
the project team took a more pragmatic and useful approach of developing recommended
procedures and Technotes for how to develop an S-N curve for a specific concrete mix and for
how to conduct more informative and reliable fatigue tests. Extensive material characterization
and experimental measurements were made during all testing so that the results of this research
could inform the development and evaluation of fatigue models and standards for making
capacity assessments. All collected and new test results were disseminated in an Excel
spreadsheet, and other project findings have or are being disseminated in publications.

4.2 Findings
 While the development of an all-encompassing model for concrete compression fatigue is
not possible at this time, the results from the broad-based experimental testing that was
conducted in this project has helped to understand the influence of many key factors that were
previously untested or very lightly examined that can significantly help the development of more
comprehensive models. The most important observations from the experimental testing program
that was conducted in this project are as follows:
  The inclusion of longitudinal reinforcing steel considerably increased the measured
 fatigue capacity (number of cycles to failure) relative to a test specimen that did not
 contain longitudinal reinforcement. Based on measured straining, the reason for this
 improvement is because the cyclic loading softens the response of the concrete (along
 with strain damage accumulation) while the steel remains linear elastic with the same
 stiffness until yielding of the reinforcement. This leads to the position of the total load
 carried by the concrete to be shifted to the steel with the increasing in loading cycles, a
 decrease in concrete stresses for a particular loading range, and an increase in the
 concrete fatigue capacity by about an order of magnitude. This is a significant finding as
 it suggests that all fatigue models derived from tests on plane concrete are conservatively
 biased for reinforced concrete structures (which nearly all structures are).
  For test specimens subjected to variable amplitude loadings, the order of stressing
 impacts the measured fatigue capacity of concrete where this fatigue capacity is less
 when the higher stress levels are applied earlier in the loading sequence. This is expected
 to be due to the damage (micro cracking and cracking) caused by the higher stress levels
 leading to greater damage by subsequent lower stressing then would have been the case if
 these higher stresses had not already occurred. An impact of a factor of 3-10 or more on
 total fatigue capacity can be expected. This effect is not being considered in design
 practice which leads to assessments being less conservative than they would be
 otherwise.
  Saturated concrete has considerably less fatigue capacity (by up to an order of
 magnitude) less than unsaturated concrete. This is expected to be due to pore water
 stresses that crack the concrete under fatigue loading. This is an important consideration
 66

 for the design of those portions of support structures that are underwater. Some, but not
 all codes, have started to consider this effect. This effect should be considered in design
 practice.
  The size and roughness of the coarse aggregates that are used in a concrete mix design
 has a significant effect on fatigue capacity. Concretes that used large and angular
 aggregates were observed to have considerably less fatigue capacity, by about an order of
 magnitude, than concretes which had smaller and rounder aggregates. Thus, a designer
 can greatly improve fatigue capacity by the use of smaller and rounder aggregates.
  The mechanical characteristics of the aggregates has an effect on the fatigue capacity of
 the concrete. Lightweight aggregate concrete showed less fatigue capacity compared to
 the same size regular concrete.
  On average, the 3D printed concrete has considerably better fatigue capacity than regular
 casted concrete, and that is because of the uniformity of the mix and the layered
 hardening conditions of the 3D printed concrete which leads to a more uniform straining
 behavior due to cyclic loading.
  The variability in the monotonic compressive strength of cylinders cast from the same
 batch of concrete leads to significant apparent scatter in measured fatigue capacity by up
 to an order of magnitude. This can be accounted and corrected for through the use of
 improved methods for estimating the monotonic strength of specific test cylinders such as
 by the technique developed in this research project and presented in this report.

There were several key findings and observations made from the examination of the large
collection database that was created in this project, including:
  The observed scatter in the measured fatigue capacity for any particular stress range
 (Smax/Smin) was extremely large (up to three orders of magnitude and sometimes greater).
 This is attributed to the impact of the specific composition of concrete materials on
 fatigue capacity, the impact of influencing factors not considered, as well as
 shortcomings in many testing programs. The findings that were just presented from the
 researchers' testing explain the reason for some of this very large variation.
  Since concrete fatigue models and standards are empirically derived from a subset of the
 available test data in the literature, it is not surprising that the fatigue capacity in models
 and standards can differ by an order of magnitude or larger. The implications of this are
 quite significant with the use of one standard leading to an assessment that a concrete
 structure has a service life of 50 years while the use of another standard leading to a
 calculated service life of 5 years or less.
  Because of a lack of information presented by researchers on the composition of concrete
 used in their testing programs and the measured response of test specimens, and the
 sensitivity of fatigue capacity to many variables, it is not justifiable to simply remove the
 outlying low measured fatigue capacities that were observed in the scattered test data.
 This is a major obstacle to the creation of better general purpose fatigue models.
  Fatigue capacity decreases as the loading frequency increases. This was expected because
 a lower loading frequency leads to a longer period of time at which the highest stresses in
 a cycle occur, which gives more time for crack propagation/damage to occur. Given the
 typical natural frequencies of concrete support structures for wind turbines, the relevant

 67

 test data to use for establishing design and analysis should only use the results of tests
 done with loading frequencies of 2 Hz or less.

4.3 Deliverables
 The findings from this project are disseminated with this report, and more extensively in
two student PhD theses and other manuscripts listed in section 3.5 stemming from this work;
some of these are in print and some are still to be published. The developed compression fatigue
database, consisting of tests collected from the public literature and those conducted through this
project, have been organized in an Excel file that is publicly available.
 The overall objective of this project is primarily achieved through technical committee
documents of the American Concrete Institute’s Fatigue Committee (ACI committee 215) and
associated papers that are still in the process of being finalized and adopted at the time of
preparation of this final report. One of the ACI Technotes establishes a recommended practice
for concrete compression fatigue testing that if followed would result in future fatigue testing
leading to a much better understanding of compression fatigue behavior and ultimately the
creation of more comprehensive models. The other Technote establishes a process for the
generation of a S-N Curve for a particular concrete or structural concrete; this will allow
designers and innovators to create concrete support structure solutions that are safely designed
for fatigue and not unnecessarily impaired by conservative fatigue models and standards.
 It is further recommended that the suggested practices, tools, and methods developed
through this project are promoted for use by researchers, certification bodies, and by standard
organizations. This includes the continued expansion of the Excel database 1.
 The value to the industry and society of this research is that is avoids unnecessary
conservatism in designs (due to use of inappropriate fatigue models) thereby reducing LCoE,
encourages innovation in concrete material selection and structural design, and it supports the
development of a public and expansive concrete fatigue database that will lead to continually
improved understanding of fatigue performance.

4.4 Recommendations for Future Research
 While it is considered that this project has contributed significantly to available and
detailed experimental data sets on the compression fatigue capacity and behavior of concrete,
that the dissemination of the large Excel database of available data is significant, and that the
procedures in the Technotes can significantly improve research and professional practice, there is
much to be done to be able to make greatly improved and more reliable assessments of the
fatigue capacity and performance of concrete structures for wind turbines. One of these is simply
additional compression fatigue testing on different types of concretes with different level and
types of reinforcement conditions. Other recommendations for future work are described below:

4.4.1 Computed Tomography Investigation
 The literature review showed that there is relatively limited information on microcrack
development under fatigue loading in concrete materials, which is best examined using ultra-

1
 For getting access to this file, please use the following link:
https://tufts.box.com/s/sauwujyyw3i7da5nuir9n5lzrvmhfxkj
 68

high-resolution X-Ray Computed Tomography (CT). A useful research program would be to
conduct much more extensive CT scanning with ultra-high-resolution CT equipment on a wide
array of concrete and reinforced concrete test specimens and field samples. This would establish
a means of much more accurately assessing the fatigue capacity utilization and remaining
capacity in field structures, as well as inform and improve design practices [47-51]. For this
purpose, there are several items worth investigating, including:
● The effect of aggregate type, size, and shape on microcracking. This includes initiation,
 development and progression patterns, effect of fatigue loading on the micro-crack
 progression, number of cracks going through the aggregates itself in each cylinder and etc.
 later on, the analysis of these observations along with the stress and strain data gathered by
 Data Acquisition (DAQ) system might reveal some correlation among the microcrack
 pattern and development and the properties of the material.
● Evaluation of cores or samples taken from retiring/existing structures in the field
 (onshore and offshore) which experienced fatigue loading during their lifetime. This
 will help us to make some observations on crack growth patterns in the hot spots and gain
 some knowledge and experience by making connections between lab tests and in situ
 structures. Through this, a method can be developed to predict the remaining lifetime of the
 existing structures.
● The effect of reinforcement on the crack growth pattern and strain evolution under
 fatigue loading. Since almost all concrete structures are reinforced, the effects of the
 presence of steel inside concrete on fatigue performance and service life should be more
 fully assessed.

4.4.2 Influence of Complex States of Stress
 Limited research on the effect of lateral confining pressure on fatigue capacity has found
that the lateral confining pressure greatly enhanced the fatigue capacity and that the influence
was stronger for lower peak stress levels. This will have a very significant effect on the final
results and strain progression, but it is not considered in the codes and provisions of RC structure
designs for fatigue. This confinement can be bi-axial or tri-axial. Therefore, there is a need for a
new apparatus or facility to be able to do high-cycle fatigue testing on concrete elements that can
be subjected to bi-axial or tri-axial fatigue loading, which is common in many field structures.

4.4.3 Assessment of Impact of Softening due to Fatigue on Stress Demands
 As observed in this project, the presence of steel reinforcement has a significant effect on
the strain evolution, microcracks initiation and progression inside the structure, and on concrete
softening due to cracks development and load transfer from concrete to steel; this greatly effects
fatigue demand and capacity and has only just begun to be studied. Although there are some
studies on developing analytical or numerical models, no numerical or analytical tool is capable
of considering this softening effect under high-cycle fatigue loading. The complex geometry of
upcoming floating wind turbine structures and environmental condition and loading will increase
the importance of doing such a study.

 69

4.4.4 Structural Joint Test
 Finally, the most greatly needed area of future study regarding the fatigue capacity of
concrete wind turbine support structures is on the fatigue capacity of joints, which is what is
most likely to control the fatigue life of these structures. The variables that need to be
investigated for the high-cycle fatigue capacity of joints include the shape of connected panels,
construction sequence, use of grout, type of grout, thickness of grout, surface of joint
(intentionally roughened, match cast, smooth, CNC – machined flat), type of concrete, type of
connectors (long unbonded strands, short unbonded strands, bolted), and under what conditions
is gaping allowed. The design of a testing program on joints should be informed by an
examination of industry practices and extensive field monitoring campaigns.

 A-2

1 Concrete Fatigue Experimental Data Base Spread Sheet
Research into concrete capacity under fatigue loading is not a new topic, and it has been studied
for at least 50 years. Although several relationships exist relating applied stresses (S) and the
fatigue life of concrete (N), there is no consensus regarding any specific S-N equation or set of
equations that should be used. The first task was to create a test data repository that could be
used to examine the accuracy of existing S-N models and understand the differences in the basis
of these models. The created repository has a flexible format to accommodate different test data.
Both U.S. Customary Units (USUC) and international system metric units (SI) can be used. The
test database template accommodates test data entry based on the different independent variables
utilized in the research. Since test specimens vary in size and/or shape (cylinders, cubes, or
prisms), conversion factors were used based on previous research. This repository was created in
the form of an Excel spreadsheet with multiple tabs. The first and second tabs include the
concrete fatigue experimental test data both in USCU and SI units. The other tabs include
comparing the data with S-N models, with each tab focusing on one model. The salient features
that can be found within each tab of the developed experimental test data repository are further
explained below (Figure 1).

 Figure 1 Screenshot #1 of the experimental test data repository – Tabs definition

The first tab consists of multiple entries for fatigue experimental data. This tab is designed to
include all possible variables that could give the reader insight into concrete characteristics and
the variables that could be probably recognized as influential to the concrete fatigue response to
the cyclic fatigue loading. First, the name of the reference document, the type of test (uniaxial or
flexural), and information about the geometry of the specimens used in the experimental
investigations were added to the spreadsheet. Following this, the material properties of concrete
used in the specimens are listed (max aggregate size and type, water-to-cement ratio, cement
type, additives name and percent of usage, fibers name and percentage of use, and strength of
concrete). Shown below in Figure 2 are the stress-strain curves of concrete under monotonic
compressive loading from the tests conducted at the University of Illinois at Urbana- Champaign
(UIUC), and the dashed black-line in the figure represents a characteristic or an average concrete
stress-strain curve. It can be observed from this figure that concrete exhibits an inherently
nonlinear material response. Additionally, Figure 3 shows the progressive damage in the
concrete cylinder specimens with increasing loads. The letters a, b, and c in Figure 3 would
roughly correspond to the three last red dots along the concrete stress-strain curves shown in
Figure 2. Therefore, in order to capture a more complete behavior of the concrete material, the
spreadsheet was developed to request information at the locations of the four red dots in Figure 2
(which correspond to strains of 0.1%, 0.2%, 0.3%, and 0.4% respectively), as well as the stress
and strain magnitudes at the location of the ultimate stress that the concrete cylinders were able
to sustain, which is represented in Figure 2 with the black cross-mark (X).

 Figure 2 Concrete monotonic stress-strain behavior (from the tests conducted at UIUC)

 Figure 3 Progressive damage in the concrete cylinder specimens under increasing load

Figure 4 provides a screen capture of the experimental test data repository, representing
geometry and material properties information. The various tabs seen at the bottom of the
spreadsheet correspond to the various templates that have been created to accommodate the
differences in the experiments. Figure 4 shows the populated first tab, which includes concrete
cylinder specimens tested under uniaxial compressive fatigue and referred to the U.S. Customary
Units (USCU).

 A-4

 Figure 4 Screenshot #2 of the experimental test data repository – Specimen Geometry and Material
 Properties

Figure 5 Screenshot #3 of the experimental test data repository – Material Properties and cyclic fatigue
 loading regime

Next, information about the fatigue loading plan is requested or added to the spreadsheet. This
includes the average concrete monotonic strength (that would be used for determining the
magnitude of the applied stresses), the peak and valley fatigue stress levels, the frequency of
loading, and the waveform that was used in the various fatigue experiments. Figure 5 presents
another screenshot from the experimental test data repository. The mean concrete strength used
for computing the applied fatigue stress levels is represented by "fcm_test", representing the
control value of monotonic strength used in the fatigue tests. As mentioned previously, different

 A-5

fatigue models use different independent variables. For the tests conducted at UIUC, the fatigue
model used while selecting the fatigue stress combinations utilized the peak stress level, Smax,
and stress ratio, R (which is the ratio of the valley stress level to the peak stress level), as the
independent variables, which is also reflected in this tab of the database. The minimum stress
level, Smin, is computed from these two independent variables. The waveform and the frequency
of testing used in the experiments are also indicated in the database, as seen in Figure 5.
With the details about the specimen geometry, material properties, and fatigue loading plan
obtained, fatigue experimental test results were populated next in the spreadsheet. The most
basic fatigue test result reported in almost all fatigue investigations is the number of cycles to
failure, Nf, also referred to as the fatigue capacity. Additionally, considering concrete's inherently
nonlinear material behavior, it is essential also to understand and report the behavior of concrete
through the fatigue loading process. However, only a small proportion of the papers and
investigations have reported this. Therefore, in order to further the understanding of concrete
behavior under fatigue loading, it was also decided to request aspects related to concrete fatigue
behavior in the database to allow for a complete understanding of the performance of concrete
through the duration of fatigue loading.
After having all the data sets populated in the first tab, other tabs could be used to compare the
data with the existing characteristic and design S-N curves introduced by different standard
codes or researchers. For this purpose, a table has been created to give the user the power to play
with the variables to see specified data tests and be able to compare them with the S-N curves.
Figure 6 shows a screenshot of the table, which includes all the critical variables. Figure 7 and
Figure 8 show a comparison of the uniaxial cylinders test data with four different Smin levels
with two different characteristic S-N models.

  Figure 6 Screenshot #4 of the experimental test data repository – table for defining data range to be
 shown in the plots

 A-6

 Figure 7 Comparision of different specified data sets with DNVGL characteristic S-N model

Figure 8 Comparision of different specified data sets with Model Code 2010 characteristic S-N model

 A-7

Figure 9 presents the typical axial strain behavior of concrete through the fatigue loading
process, and Figure 10 presents the typical stiffness degradation in concrete through the fatigue
loading process, as obtained from the fatigue tests conducted at UIUC. Information about the
axial strains was obtained directly during the tests, and secant stiffness information was inferred
from the applied loads and the strain data. As can be seen from the two figures, there is a
continuous evolution of both (peak and valley) axial strain as well as secant stiffness through the
fatigue loading process. It has been reported in the literature that the strain and stiffness
evolution can be divided into three phases. Therefore, it was decided to record the axial strain
data (both peak and valley) at four boundary locations, namely, at the start of the test, at the
transition between phase 1 and phase 2, at the transition between phase 2 and phase 3, and at the
end of the test. Additionally, to obtain a more continuous variation of strains and stiffness, the
axial strain data was requested at the midpoint, i.e., at 50% of the failure number of cycles. The
locations at which strain information was requested are shown in Figure 11, in which the desired
locations where axial strain information is requested in the spreadsheet is represented by dots on
the strain evolution diagram (the same strain evolution diagram previously shown in Figure 9).

 Figure 9 Strain evolution plots from the tests conducted at UIUC;
 (left): Variation of peak and valley strain through the fatigue loading process in one test
(right): Variation of peak strains through the fatigue loading process in four tests conducted at the same
 combination

 A-8

 Figure 10 Progressive degradation of secant stiffness through the fatigue loading process

Figure 11 Axial strain (both peak and valley) information requested from the tests at 5 locations along
the fatigue loading process, namely, at the start of the test, at the transition between phases 1 and 2, at
 50% of Nf, at the transition between phases 2 and 3, and at the failure point.

 A-9

Figure 12 presents another screen capture of the experimental test data repository, populated with
information about the fatigue test results from the tests conducted at UIUC. As explained
previously, the information requested consists of the failure number of cycles, Nf (Nfail), peak
and valley axial strain information (e_p_ and e_v_) at five locations, namely, cycle 2, the
transition between phases 1 and 2 (P12), 50% of Nf, the transition between phases 2 and 3 (P23),
and at failure (fail), as shown in Figure 12. This is the minimum amount of information required
to gain an understanding of the behavior of concrete through the fatigue loading process. The
experimental test data repository includes these results from the fatigue tests conducted at UIUC,
as shown in Figure 12.

 Figure 12 Screenshot #5 of the experimental test data repository – Fatigue Test Results

Similar templates were created for the different combinations of the independent variables (units
– USCU vs. SI, independent variables – Smax and R vs. Smin, specimen shapes – cylinders, cubes,
or prisms). The experimental test data repository has now been populated with data from more
than 2500 tests obtained from investigations that have already been conducted and reported.
However, many papers only report the failure number of cycles, Nf. Therefore, information
regarding specimen details, material properties, fatigue loading plan, and fatigue capacity, Nf,
from these papers have been included in the database. Information about strain behavior is
unavailable in the public domain for many of these already conducted investigations, but efforts
are underway to procure the necessary reports to further populate this database. The hope is also
that all future investigations will more fully report fatigue behavior-related information, which
will help further the understanding of the behavior of concrete subjected to fatigue loading.
An additional challenge to creating such a database that compares tests conducted worldwide is
regarding the varying specimen shapes and sizes since the concrete compressive strengths are
sensitive to the specimen geometry. In order to permit the comparisons between the different
specimen geometries, the following concrete compressive strength conversion factors would be
used to standardize the strength values obtained from different control specimen geometries
(Table 1). The effect of the control specimen geometry was investigated by the PI in a previous
research effort that included the creation of a database regarding concrete shear strengths, and
these conversion factors were determined as a part of that investigation.

 Table 1 Conversion factors for concrete compressive strengths of specimens with different geometries

3 Outliers in the data set
Concrete fatigue capacity depends on several factors, including material properties, testing
specimen geometry, existence of water and reinforcement steel, and loading protocol. Almost all
of the available experimental data is on plain concrete material, which has been tested in dry
conditions. The main goal is to use this data set to gain some idea about the behavior of concrete
material under cyclic fatigue loading and to be able to estimate the fatigue capacity of a new
design concrete structure.
One of the concerns in the data set is the existence of outliers, which can lead to a very
conservative estimate of the fatigue capacity of concrete material. These outliers have been
defined by two main definitions: 1. They have very low or high numbers of cycles to failure 2.
They are apart from the main cluster of the data set.
Figure 15 shows the uniaxial experimental data set on plain concrete and Model Code 2010
prediction model curves. The vertical axis shows the maximum stress ratio (Smax) of loading,
and the horizontal axis shows the logarithm of the number of cycles to failure. There are five
different markers with different minimum stress ratios (Smin) of loading and five different
model code 2010 curves with five different Smin, the averages of each marker Smin range. The
same colors were used to tie the markers and prediction S-N curves. The wide spectrum of the
results is due to different influential factors discussed in previous reports and the nature of
concrete behavior. A few examples of the outliers are shown with red arrows. These so-called

 A-13

outliers can have some known or unknown causes, or they could be a fault in the testing (Table
2). Based on the information on these data points (outliers), there is a chance to redo these tests
to narrow down the scatter of the data points to the main data cluster. These new tests enable us
to decide on the credibility of the above mentioned data points, and to eliminate the outlier in the
existing data. Some of those tests that need to be done in this matter are listed in Error!
Reference source not found..

4 Statistical significance of loading frequency effect on fatigue life
  capacity
A few papers have explored the effect of frequency and saturation level, and they have made
some statements on the effect of these parameters. Those statements were made based on the
limited number of tests on just one concrete material. Therefore, there was a need to investigate
the effect of the frequency with a larger data set.
4.1 Assumptions
Generally, when somebody wants to explore the effect of a parameter statistically, the other
parameters should remain the same to have a reliable conclusion. Unfortunately, there are a
number of parameters in this data set that vary case by case. For the sake of this project and to be
able to do some statistical analysis on the scale of the course project, all the other parameters are
assumed to be the same in the entire data set. The uniaxial cyclic test data is the only chunk of
the data that has been used for this analysis. The other assumption is the normality of the data
set. The data set is not exactly normally distributed, but it is assumed to be normal. (Figure 16)

 Figure 16 Distribution of fatigue capacity of concrete

4.2 Dividing the data into two big cluster
A general hypothesis is that in low-frequency cyclic loading (less than 1 Hz), concrete
microcracks will have a chance to propagate more effectively and quickly to macrocracks,
leading the process to failure. i.e. in the high frequency of loading (more than 1 Hz) will lead to a

 A-15

high number of cycles of failure with respect to the low ones. The analysis of the results shows
that it is true. Table 3 shows a considerable difference between the data sets A1 and A2,
comparing the mean and standard deviation.

4.3 Conclusion
The results show that the loading frequency has a considerable effect on the fatigue capacity of
the concrete material. However, other parameters need to be investigated statistically to discern
the amounts of effects. In the end, there might be a need to introduce a new model to consider
the combined effect of all the significant parameters and draw a new curve or introduce a new
equation for estimating the fatigue capacity of the concrete material, which has been done in the
following sections.

 A-21

To explore the effect of frequency, the uniaxial concrete fatigue test data has been divided into 5
data sets with five distinct frequencies. This table shows that with the increase in the loading
frequency, the standard deviation of the log number of the cycles to failure would increase along
with the mean. This means that low-frequency loading has a tighter band range of results.
Nevertheless, the higher frequencies have a very wide range of number of cycles to failure.

5 Linear and Polynomial Regression
5.1 Linear regression
One of the usual methods of assessing a data set is to look for any linear regression among the
variables. Here, we are looking for the logarithm of the number of cycles to failure by having
some parameters. Different linear models have been generated using different parameters as
inputs. The coefficient of determination (R2) and Mean Squared Error (MSE) are considered
indicators for comparing these models. To validate models, a 5-fold cross-validation method is
used. In this method, the data set is divided into five equal portions. Then, the model is calibrated
with 4/5 of the data (called train data in this report) and validated by the remaining 1/5 (which is
called test data in this report).
Interestingly, by increasing the number of input parameters, the R2 decreased, and MSE
increased a little! So, having multiple parameters would not help to get fewer errors in the
predictions of the results. Therefore, we can conclude that the maximum and minimum stress
levels influence the logarithm of the number of cycles to failure for plain concrete. Even though
the other parameters have smaller coefficients in the linear regression models, they have some
influence on the final estimation of fatigue life and cannot be ignored. Table 10 shows the model
number, which is related to the number of input parameters, and it also shows the parameters
themselves.

 A-25

 2 Smax, Smin,
 3 Smax, Smin, Frequency

 5 Max Aggregate Size, Strength, Smax, Smin, Frequency
 6 Max Aggregate Size, w/c, Strength, Smax, Smin, Frequency

5.2 Polynomial regression
Polynomial regression is another method that can be used to evaluate the correlation/relationship
among the input parameters and the number of cycles to failure. Polynomial regression, by
considering the combination of input parameters in the models, helped to increase R2 and
decrease the MSE for the models (a little bit). Figure 27 shows the comparison among the newly
generated polynomial regression models and standard models. These models are a little bit
curved and not completely close to a straight line. This curvedness might be somehow more
realistic in the lower Smax levels because there is an agreement between these models and
standard models. The polynomial model with 2 input parameters (model 2-2) shows more
realistic estimates of the log number of cycles to failure for the higher Smax levels as well (very
close to the mean of the data sets).
