6 Introduction to Empirically Developed Models and Artificial
  Neural Network Models
An empirical model in engineering is one that is not developed by fundamental science but rather
one that is developed and calibrated from collected data from experiments and other
observational opportunities. It consists of a mathematical relationship (formula or formulas) that
relates the characteristics (e.g., geometry, material properties, and loading) with what is under
examination (e.g., strength). As part of the process of developing such empirical models, the
impact of a characteristic may be additive, subtractive, multiplicative, exponential, or some
combination thereof. Equation [1] provides an example of such an equation for the shear strength
of a reinforced concrete beam. As illustrated, these expressions can involve the use of many
parameters consisting of material strengths and geometric values. Considerations in developing
such empirical relationships include determining if the user has all of the needed parameter
values, whether their effects are clear and make sense, and whether the equation will be
calculated correctly.

 Eqn. [1]

For complex phenomena, researchers come up with entirely different models that consider
different characteristics and have different calibration values. The reasons that models differ
significantly include (i) that different subsets of experiments are used to develop these models
and (ii) model developers have different biases regarding the effect of different factors. The
accuracy of a model is assessed by R-squared (R2) fit or Coefficients of Variation (COVs) of the
predicted to measured values ratio.
Another type of model that can be used to calculate a performance value such as strength is an
Artificial Neural Network Model. This model is not an equation but a statistical fit that relates a
set of characteristic input parameters to the outcome of an experiment, such as measured
strength; since it is just a statistical fit, it is labeled Artificial. It iteratively weights and biases the
effect of these parameters until there is as close fit as possible with the particular outcome of the
experiment; this is referred to as "model training". This turned ANN Model can then be used to
predict the outcome using test results that were not used to "train" the model. The accuracy of
this ANN Model is also evaluated using R2 and COVs. Since an ANN Model is not constrained,
very high R2 and low COVs can be achieved compared to empirical models that consist of
mathematical relationships. However, if the considered characteristics are insufficient (i.e., not
considering parameters of great influence), or the uncertainty is aleatory (due to randomness), it
is impossible to achieve high R2 and low COVs even with an ANN.
Given the inaccuracy and differences in existing empirical relationships (fatigue models) for
concrete fatigue, as conveyed in Figure 28, it is important to assess if this is because these

 A-28

relationships are just poorly formulated and calibrated or if there are either key characteristics
that are not considered or if there is a high degree of randomness (aleatory uncertainty), or some
combination of the two. This was done by training an ANN Model with some of the available
data and then using this model to predict the fatigue capacity for the test data that was not used to
train the model. If the ANN Model proved to be quite accurate, then improved empirical models
could be developed. If not, then a deeper consideration of test specimen characteristics is needed.

 Figure 28 Test Data and Fatigue Models for the Impact of Scmax on Fatigue Capacity (Scmin = 0.05)

Prior to presenting this assessment for concrete compressive fatigue, it is useful to present the
results for another example where it is particularly clear that the ANN Model provides for a
much better prediction of capacity than empirically developed mathematical relationships. The
example that will be used if that of the shear strength of reinforced concrete beams that do not
contain shear reinforcement [1].

 A-29

Figure 29 presents the characteristics of a reinforced concrete beam that are described by several
parameter values. Note that these greatly exceed the number of parameters in Equation 1. Table
11 further defines some of these geometric and material property values.

 Figure 29 Parameters that Define the Geometry of the Beam and the Beam Cross Section

 Table 11 Further Definition of Parameter Values

 A-30

 Table 12 Further Definition of Parameter Values (Continued)

A comparison of the relationship's accuracy for calculating the U.S.'s one-way shear strength is
now presented. Building Code provisions as of 2014 [2], versus when an ANN is used. The
values on the y-axis in Figures 2 and 3 are the ratios of the (experimentally measured strength,
Vexp) divided by the capacity (Vpred) calculated using the relationship in the 1999 U.S.
Building Code which remained the same through the 2014 code. Each dot represents one test
result. If the y-value is equal to 1, then the measured strength is exactly equal to the "predicted"
strength. If the y-value is equal to 2.0, then the measured strength was twice that "predicted"
(building code is conservative or safe), and if the y-value is equal to 0.5 then the measured
strength is one-half of that "predicted" (building code is unconservative or unsafe). The ratios
shown in the left-side figures are from the U.S. Building Code and the ratios shown on the right-
side images are from when an ANN [3] is used. Figure 30(a) is for when these ratios (on the y-
axis) are plotted against the monotonic compressive strength of the concrete (f1c) on the x-axis.
Figure 30(b) is for when these ratios are plotted against the slenderness of the beam as defined
by the shear span to depth ratio (d/s) which is defined by a/ds in Figure 29. The vertical spread in
the results is an indicator of the accuracy of the "prediction", and as shown, the ANN is much
better than the U.S. Building Code. Since there is no trend in the mean strength ratio for the
building code with either f1c or a/d, then adjusting how this is considered in the code will have
no value. In comparison, the spread of the results in the ANN model is very small for a
phenomenon as complex as the shear strength of reinforced concrete beams. While many

 A-31

strength ratios are less than 1.0 in the ANN Model, recognize that this could be easily addressed
by multiplying the predicted strength by about 0.80. In my comparison, to make the strength
ratios for the U.S. building code greater than 1.0, it would be necessary to multiply the predicted
strengths by about 0.2.

 Figure 30 Comparison of Strength Ratios between U.S. Building Code (no Trend) and ANN

Figure 31 presents the same results as shown in Figure 30, but where this time the strength ratios
are plotted versus (a) the Depth of Beam and (b) Percentage of Longitudinal Reinforcement. This
time, there is a trend in strength ratio between the depth of the beam and the percentage of
longitudinal reinforcement. To account for these and other trends, researchers have proposed
many new equations for how to improve the U.S. building code, as shown in Figure 32.

  Building Code, and this eliminated many of the lowest shear strength ratios (safety concerns)
  as shown in Figure 5 [4 and 5]. When looking at this plot, it is still noteworthy that not all

 conservatism that was deemed to be too large a penalty for other structures.

6.1 Neural Network in Concrete Fatigue Data Sets
As has been discussed previously, design code provisions, be that an equation or an S-N fatigue
curve, are frequently empirical, which means that an equation (or curve) is set so that the design
requirement will mostly result in a conservative estimate of capacity based on the experimental
test data. Therefore, the neural network has been used to predict the logarithm of the number of
cycles to failure for plain concrete using the existing data sets. A large portion of the uniaxial test
data has at least six parameters reported (Max Aggregate Size, water-to-cement ratio, Strength,
Smax, Smin, Frequency of loading). The larger the number of parameters, the better the chance
of having a better predictive NN model. The number of parameters influencing capacity is often
very large, but the number of parameters that can be included in a design formula for practical
reasons is limited. Like the regression models, 5-fold cross-validation is used for validating the
model. A sensitivity analysis of the number of layers, size of layers, type of activation function,
etc., was performed to choose the model with the lowest MSE. If there is a relationship between
collected metadata and a result, then this will be picked up by an Artificial Neural Network
(ANN). Interestingly, the NN was not able to perform better than regression models, and it gave
us higher MSE and lower R2. Unfortunately, an ANN does not lead to a general formula that is
suitable for use in design practice. As mentioned before, the standard model predictions are
mostly conservative. Figure 34 shows the level of variation in the predictions in the higher Smax
level (higher than 0.8), where we have most of the experimental test data and conservativeness
and un-conservativeness of predictions all over different Smax levels using the DNV fatigue
model. Figure 35 shows the same comparison for the NN model generated based on the available
data set. Unfortunately, the variation of the prediction is still high. Based on the literature review
on the concrete beam shear study, a tight band cluster around the prediction capacity ratio of 1 is
expected to be seen. Even though it is tighter, it shows tighter band of variation, but still
variation is high. This shows the incompleteness of the data set. In other words, if we had all the

 A-34

parameters reported for all the data points, there would certainly be a better chance of developing
a more comprehensive model. Moreover, there is a chance that there are other influential
parameters that we are missing from the data set. This will highlight the importance of the
development of a standard or some sort of a check sheet for design certification by testing rather
than having an uncertain predictive model.

 B-2

1 Introduction
1.1 concrete fatigue phenomena
Concrete fatigue is a process directly related to the propagation of microcracks inside the
material [1-3]. Pre-existing defects like pores and shrinkage microcracks, in addition to the
discontinuity of material at the interface between the cement paste and aggregates, act as triggers
for the formation and propagation of cracks [4].
Generally, microcracks have a similar network formation and origin in both static and dynamic
loading. However, cracks are more numerous and can spread more extensively under cyclic
loading in comparison to static loading. On the other hand, microcracks are fewer in static
loading but more developed, which means they are smaller and larger in number. Fatigue-related
microcracks typically initiate and propagate around aggregates and in the cementitious material.
Cracks are parallel to the loading direction under compression and perpendicular under tension.
In both cases, the microcracks show an average orientation perpendicular to the maximum
tension/extension direction in the cementitious/mortar matrix [4].
Fatigue microcracking has an irreversible nature. It contributes to a progressive but slow
deterioration of the inner structure of concrete. Crack progression is quantifiable and recordable,
which allows for an evaluation of the deterioration process and its evolution to failure. The
microcracks produce sounds while they form, and they can be recorded in the laboratory [5-7].
Essentially, acoustic devices can record the failure process in a specimen under fatigue loading.
Another useful method for recording the failure process is using sensors like strain gauges,
extensometers, and linear variable differential transformers (LVDT). Using sensors helps to keep
track of numerous data from strains and deformation, and formulate the crack propagation
process under fatigue loading [8].

 Figure 1 Evolution of the concrete deterioration under fatigue (cyclic) loading

Figure 1 shows the strain () as a function of the number of applied loading cycles (n). There are
three phases in the fatigue deterioration of concrete that are observable:

 B-3

 - Phase I: The initial phase is the crack initiation process. It shows the rapid evolution in
 the deterioration of the concrete. This phase covers about 5-10% in the total number of
 loading cycles, Nf, that are necessary for fatigue failure.
 - Phase II: In the second phase, the rate of deterioration is constant and stabilized. It is the
 main part of the fatigue life of the material. Phase II constitutes 80-90% of the total
 number to cycles of failure.
 - Phase III: The final phase corresponds to the progressive acceleration in the deterioration
 process. It leads to failure.
Another physical effect that can quantify the deterioration process is the dissipation of heat. It
corresponds to the release of energy due to the propagation of the microcracks [4].

1.2 Fatigue strength and number of cycles to failure
The relationship between the imposed load on the material and the number of cycles to failure is
classically presented in the form of a Wöhler curve in a σ-Nf diagram. The stress, σ, is called the
"endurance" or "fatigue strength". By keeping the stress value (mean or maximum stress, stress
amplitude) fixed, the corresponding number of cycles to failure can be found, or vice versa [4].
Three different stages are illustrated on fatigue curves. Each stage is related to the number of
cycles to failure:
 1. Low cycle fatigue: Nf≤103;
 2. Large cycle fatigue: 103≤Nf≤107;
 3. Very large cycle fatigue: 107≤Nf;
Figure 2 shows the three different phases of fatigue phenomena in the material. Phase 2 can be
approximated to a straight line in a semi-logarithmic coordinate system:
 σ = α – β Log Nf (Wöhler’s formula)
 and  are constants that are characteristics of the material and should be derived from
experimental results.

 B-4

 Figure 2 The Wöhler's curve in the σ-Nf diagram [4]

Generally, fatigue tests produce scattered results. For concrete, the number of cycles to failure
for a given loading may vary in the ratio of 1 to 100. Therefore, the probability of occurrence of
failure must be included in a graphical representation of fatigue life (stress vs. number of cycles
to failure), rather than just defining a single averaged value as the fatigue strength [9-11]. The
Gaussian distribution is one of the best representatives of the distribution of fatigue strength of
concrete in each given number of cycles to failure.
The fatigue behavior of concrete material depends on the following parameters [4]:
  Characteristics of the material (composition and strength)
  Type and level of the loading cycle
  Frequency and shape of the cycle
  Environmental conditions

  Figure 3 Behavior of concrete subjected to fatigue under cyclic compression [4] Left: the variation of
 stress and secant modules due to cyclic loading, Right: the change in strain due to cyclic loading

 B-5

Figure 3 shows the behavior of concrete material subjected to cyclic loading. The maximum and
minimum induced loads (peaks and valleys of cyclic loading) are shown on the left. The change
in secant modulus (E0 and ED) during cyclic loading is observable. On the right, the plot shows
the development of strain in material due to cyclic loading. There are two different types of
strain: Minimum strain (min), which is pertinent to minimum stress (or valleys in cyclic loading),
and maximum strain (max), which is pertinent to maximum stress (or peaks in cyclic loading).
Three phases of strain development are observable which were described before.
Both the static and cyclic strength of the concrete depends on the same parameters [12-16]: type
and grading of the aggregates, type of cement, water-to-cement ratio, porosity, method of
casting, and curing conditions during the period of hardening. The other important issue is the
size of the specimen. Thus, an σ-Nf conventional curve is valid for a given concrete based on its
specific age and characteristics. The stress parameter in this formulation can be normalized by
the static strength of the material or the cyclic stress to static strength ratio: S=σ/fc. The stress
ratio can be represented by S, and Wöhler's curve can be modified to the S-N curve. S is
normalized stress by static compressive/tensile/flexural strength of the material, and N is the
number of cycles to failure. With this representation, the fatigue curve only depends on the
loading parameters [4].

2 Existing Fatigue models for concrete
2.1 Standards
2.1.1 Model Codes
The International Federation for Structural Concrete (fib) is a pre-normative organization. 'Pre-
normative' implies pioneering work in codification. This work has now been realized with the fib
model codes. The latest published version of the model code is MC2020. Various versions of the
model codes are presented in the following section to shed light on the concrete fatigue models
they present.
  MC1990
The MC1990 [17] was published in 1993. The MC1990 covered three different categories of
stress state for evaluation of fatigue in concrete, including pure compression, compression-
tension, and pure tension & tension-compression. The formulations are presented in Table 1. As
the MC1990 stated, the relationships are valid for concrete tested under sealed conditions and
large sections of low permeability. The relationships are also valid for concrete that was tested
under dry conditions. The fatigue lives given by these equations correspond to a probability of
failure p=5% in a log-normal distribution for any given maximum stress. If limited data are
available to estimate fatigue strength and number of cycles, the 5% defective of fatigue life
should be evaluated at a confidence level of 75%. It means that the life estimation would be more
conservative since there is less available data. The equations apply for stress level Sc,max and
Sct,max<0.9 (Sc,max and Sct,max are maximum normalized stress in compressive and tensile
loading, respectively), and frequencies greater than 0.1 cycles/min. For higher stress levels and
lower frequencies, i.e., lower cycle fatigue, lower values of log N may be expected in this model.
The model code uses the Sc to represent the normalized compressive strength of material and Sct

 B-6

as the normalized tensile strength of material since it has different formulations for different
stress states.
MC1990 has some limitations and shortcomings. For instance, it does not account for the effect
of confinement and longitudinal reinforcement, the impact of saturation, or the effect of loading
period/frequency. The fatigue strength and life prediction figures based on the maximum and
minimum ratio in the reinforced concrete section in the three different stress states are presented
below. (see Figure 4, Figure 5, and Figure 6)

  MC2010
The MC2010 [18, 19] was published in 2013. The MC2010, like the previous one, covers three
different stress states for the evaluation of concrete fatigue. The stress states include pure
compression, compression-tension, and pure tension & tension-compression. The formulations
are presented in Table 1. The relations are valid for concrete stored in a constant environment of
approximately 20 °C and 65 % relative humidity. The relationships between concrete fatigue life
and strength are based on experiments with ultra-high strength concrete (up to 200 MPa), and
validated for high strength and normal strength concrete as well. They have also been verified for
up to 107 load cycles to failure (log N = 7) based on experiments. For higher load cycles, the
curves are not verified, and they just asymptotically approach the minimum stress level of the
respective curve. The model code expresses that S-N relations can apply to self-compacting
concrete due to material considerations, but there is no experimental evidence to prove the idea.
The S-N curves are mean curves of the number of load cycles to failure due to the large scatter of
the experimental results in concrete material. So, there is a need to apply a reduction factor to the
static strength to take care of safety in design. The equations are applicable for stress levels
Sc,max and Sct,max<0.9, and for frequencies f>0.1 cycles/min. For higher stress levels and
lower frequencies, i.e. lower cycle fatigue, lower values of log N may be expected.
The MC2010, similar to the previous one, has some limitations. For instance, it does not account
for the effect of confinement and longitudinal reinforcement, the impact of saturation, and the
effect of loading period/frequency. Figures of fatigue strength and life prediction based on the
maximum and minimum normalized stress ratio in the reinforced concrete section in the pure
compression stress state are presented below (Figure 7). The two other stress states are the same
as the previous model code.

  Model Code 2020
Model Code 2020 considers the effect of water saturation on concrete (the wet concrete vs
immersed concrete) by multiplying a factor to the pure compression formulation of MC2010.
Characteristic S–N curves for concrete can be used without any restriction for frequencies higher
than 0.1 Hz. For lower frequencies, the fatigue life should be reduced. Also, they have added
fatigue shear forces verification in their formulations and considerations. Since their work has
not yet been published, it cannot be referred to classically.
Like other models, Model Code 2020 does not consider the effect of confinement and
longitudinal reinforcement, the impact of saturation on pure tension and tension-compression
state, and the effect of loading period/frequency. Figures showing the fatigue strength and life
prediction based on the maximum and minimum ratio in the reinforced concrete section in the
pure compression stress state are presented in Figure 19. The two other stress states are the same
as the previous model code.

2.1.1.1 DNVGL-ST-C502
DNV GL is an internationally accredited registrar and classification society headquartered in
Høvik, Norway. They are one of the leading companies in the field of offshore wind. As such,
they have a strong knowledge background and are responsible for certifying the design
documents of other companies. DNV GL recommends numerous standards and codes for every
aspect of offshore wind. DNVGL-ST-C502 [20] is a standard for offshore concrete structures.
The latest version of this code was published in February of 2018. It presents a formulation for
pure compression and pure tension fatigue stresses. It also considers the effect of saturation on
the estimated fatigue life. It proposes Smin=0 in the compression-tension stress state. Since most
codes and researches proposed one formulation for fatigue life prediction in different stress
states, S is the representative of the normalized strength of material (compressive, tensile, or
flexural).
Similar to the model codes, DNVGL-ST-C502 does not account for the effect of confinement
and longitudinal reinforcement or the effect of loading period/frequency. Figures of fatigue
strength and life prediction based on the maximum and minimum ratio in the reinforced concrete
section in the pure compression and compression-tension stress states are presented below
(Figure 8 and Figure 9)

2.1.1.2 Eurocode 2
The European Standard EN 1992-1-1:2011 [21], called EuroCode2, is a code for the design of
concrete structures. There are two verification formulas for concrete under compression and
shear forces. It also has an equation for the estimation of the concrete fatigue life cycle. It has
some limitations, like other codes. For instance, it does not account for the effect of confinement
and longitudinal reinforcement, the effect of loading period/frequency, the effect of saturation,
and so on. Figures of fatigue strength and life prediction based on the maximum and minimum

 B-11

ratio in the reinforced concrete section in the pure compression stress state are presented below
(Figure 10).

 Eurocode 2
 1

2.1.1.3 JSCE.15
The Japanese civil engineering society also has a guideline for designing concrete structures. It
was published in 2007. The concrete fatigue formulation given is applicable for the compression
and flexural compression, tension, and flexural tension state of stresses. It is applicable for
lightweight concrete as well as normal-weight concrete. For high-strength concrete, the code
limits the allowable compressive strength of concrete to 50 MPa. For safety reasons, JSCE-15
has four different safety factors for material property and structural elements in the case of
fatigue. The code does consider the effect of saturation on the estimation of the life of the
concrete. The JSCE.15 [22] has formulations for designing and verifying the structure under
fatigue shear forces. It also has some limitations, like the other codes. For instance, it does not
account for the effect of confinement and longitudinal reinforcement, the effect of loading
period/frequency, the effect of compression-tension stress state, and so on. Figures of fatigue
strength and life prediction based on the maximum and minimum ratio in the reinforced concrete
section in the pure compression stress state are presented (Figure 11).

2.1.1.4 Dutch Code
The NEN-EN 1992-2+C1 and NEN 6723:2009 [23, 24], known as the Dutch code, have two
versions. One was published in 2009, and the other in 2011. Both codes have a formulation to
predict the life of concrete under fatigue cyclic loading. The following curves (Figure 12 and
Figure 13) are derived from those formulations for different maximum and minimum stress
levels in the concrete. Fatigue prediction relationships are presented in Table 1. These curves are
just for the pure compression stress state because the code does not apply to other stress states. It
also does not account for the effect of confinement and longitudinal reinforcement, the effect of
loading period/frequency, the effect of saturation, and so on.

2.2 Papers
There are lots of individual researches that have been conducted in the field of concrete fatigue,
and they have proposed some models for estimation of material strength under fatigue loading.
Most of the proposed models in this section are derived or verified by results of fatigue tests on
some specific concrete materials. They've tried to include all possible effective variables in their
formulations. In the following sections, these S-N models are presented and described.

 B-14

2.2.1 Hsu et al. 1981
Hsu et al. 1981 [25] gathered existing fatigue compression test data up to the time of the study
and proposed a comprehensive relationship among the compressive strength of concrete under
cyclic loading, the number of cycles to failure, stress ratio at the concrete cross-section, and the
frequency of the loading. There are two different categories of fatigue, including low cycle
fatigue (N<103) and high cycle fatigue (103<N<107). Hsu et al. proposed two different
relationships for the fatigue categories based on the database collected. The equations are
presented in Table 1. The following curves are for different maximum and minimum stress levels
at pure compression stress state and loading rate of f=1 Hz. (Figure 14)

                                         Hsu 1981 (f=1 Hz)

2.2.2 Zhang et al. 1996 & modified model (1998)
In 1996, Zhang and his colleagues at the University of Glasgow [26] experimented on plain
concrete beams under flexural cyclic loads and introduced a model to predict the fatigue life of
concrete based on stress ratio and frequency of loading. They considered the effects of different
loading regimes, including sinusoidal, triangular, trapezoidal, and square waves [27]. Zhang et
al. also investigated the sustained loading effect on the fatigue properties of concrete. Based on
the results, the fatigue equation previously proposed by the authors was modified to account for
the sustained loading effect. The modified fatigue equations agree well with previous
experimental results at high-stress ratios. The fatigue model formulas proposed by Zhang et al.
are presented in Table 1. The following curves also show the relationship between the number of
cycles to failure at different stress stages. (Figure 15 and Figure 16)

2.2.3 Kim & Kim 1998
In 1996, Kim and Kim [13] tested plain cylindrical concrete with different stress levels and a
loading frequency of 1 Hz in order to propose a fatigue life prediction model. The proposed
model was based on concrete's maximum stress level and static compressive strength. The
relationship is presented in Table 1. The following curve shows the relationship between the
maximum stress level and the number of cycles to failure. (Figure 17)

2.2.4 Lantsoght et al. 2013
Lantsoght [28, 29] et al. gathered data from different research studies to make a comparison
between collected data and the few existing fatigue models, including MC2010 and Dutch
Codes. They proposed a formulation (Table 1) that is a modified version of Dutch code 2011.
The formulation can be used to predict the life of concrete under fatigue loading. The proposed
formulation is for pure compression stress states. The following plot (Figure 17) is for different
maximum and minimum stress levels in the section of concrete.

 B-17

3 Design requirements evaluation and comparison
In order to compare the different models, over 10 of the fatigue S-N models have been
implemented in a spreadsheet. The relationships of the models in a pure compression stress state
are listed in the following table (Table 1). All equations depend on the maximum stress ratio to
predict the number of cycles to failure under different stress levels/ratios. Some of the equations
are applicable to different loading categories like compression, tension, flexural loading, and
shear force (Table 6). Different codes have safety measures, such as partial safety factors, which
are presented in Table 2 and Table 3. Almost all of the models are suitable for normal and high-
strength concretes. Some codes also consider ultra-high strength and lightweight concretes
(Table 4). Only a few codes have considered saturation in their fatigue evaluation (Table 5). All
of this information has been gathered in a spreadsheet in order to compare the various models.
For the next step, the database explained in task 1 should be merged to the spreadsheet to
evaluate the validity of the S-N models. The curves derived from different S-N models at
different stress states are presented in the following figures (see Figure 19, Figure 20, Figure 21,
Figure 23, and Figure 24). Model Code 2020 and Dutch Code 2011 are the most conservative S-
N models.

 Figure 25 S-N models at pure compression stress state with the minimum stress level of 0.8

Based on the stress demand evaluation in offshore wind turbine structures in task 4, it can be
confirmed that the maximum normalized stress ratio is considerably low (less than 0.6) and the
ratio of minimum stress to maximum stress at the point of evaluation (R) tends to be between
0.7-1. On the other hand, almost all of the available data from the previous research have high
Smax values (greater than 0.6) and very low R-values (lower than 0.5). Therefore, there is a lack
of required data pertinent to the stress demand evaluation. Figure 26 shows that the high
maximum stress values have less number of cycles to the failure. Also, by considering the safety
factors, it is observable that most of the models have a conservative approach for estimating
material life under fatigue loads, which is due to a large scatter of existing data.
As mentioned, the stress demand is different from what was tested before. If we consider the
maximum stress value to be around 0.4-0.5 and the stress ratio to be equal to 0.8, which is
pertinent to evaluating stress demands, there would be a huge difference between estimating the
number of cycles and failure in different models. (see Figure 27). In this specific case, the
estimated N will vary between 107 to 1040. It is very large distribution, and it would affect the
design very intensely. So, there is a need to have new tests to cover the lack of knowledge in the
abovementioned evaluation and propose an accurate model for estimating the fatigue life of the
material.

 B-28

4 Summary
Overall, 13 different concrete fatigue life estimation models (S-N models) have been
implemented in a spreadsheet. The design requirements of these models were extracted from
codes, standards, books, theses, and research papers. Almost all of the codes have the same
methodology and use Hain's models to evaluate fatigue life in design.
Some of the limitations mentioned could have a huge impact on the final results, and it is
recommended that they be evaluated in the upcoming test series. All of the S-N models are for
plain concrete in different stress states, and they do not consider the impact of reinforcement
(both longitudinal and transverse) and confinement on the final number of cycles to failure. In
static loading, both elements significantly impact the final result of strength. Furthermore, the
effect of saturation has not been well-studied.
The stress demand evaluation in offshore wind turbine structures in task 4 showed that the
existing test results are irrelevant to the required stress demands. Also, there is a large gap
between the prediction of the numbers of cycles at failure in the existing models at required
stress demands. Thus, it is crucial to have a test set at needed stress demand to see what models
would better estimate, see the effect of the abovementioned factors, and cover the shortcomings.

 B-29
