 C-2

1 Introduction
Concrete has been used for hundreds of years for marine infrastructure. The use of concrete dates
all the way back to the Roman Empire, when its invaluable material properties were first
discovered. Since then, it has become one of the most widely used construction materials in the
world due to its high strength, durability, and ability to be cast in almost any shape. Concrete
structures have been particularly successful for marine infrastructure and oil and gas platforms.
Concrete has a number of advantages over steel in the offshore environment, namely its
durability and fatigue resistance. It is also robust and can withstand extreme loads such as wave,
wind, and seismic events. Additionally, it has the ability to float, and large projects can be towed
out to sea after construction onshore, eliminating costly offshore construction costs. Another
important advantage to note is that while concrete structures need a large labor force, the projects
give a great deal of business to local contractors, suppliers, and craft workers [1]. This is
particularly true in the case of offshore concrete structures, as they are massive and need a
significant amount of time, manpower, and material to construct. Construction of a massive skirt
foundation for an offshore platform is shown in Figure 1 below.

Figure 1 Onshore construction of a concrete skirt foundation for an offshore platform. A large amount of
 manpower, material, and skilled labor is needed to construct the platform support [2]

While concrete structures have many advantages in the marine environment, there are quite a
few requirements for the structures to be successful and durable in the long term. They must be
high strength, heavily reinforced, and suitable for slipforming. The concrete must also be durable
in the case of freeze-thaw damage, wetting and drying cycles, and chemical attacks from
chlorides, sulfates, and other chemicals. Although the list of requirements is long, countless
studies have shown that concrete structures have performed admirably well in the marine
environment and needed little maintenance over the years. The first concrete oil and gas

 C-3

platform, Ekofisk I, was designed for 30-year service life. It was retired after 35 years with no
significant repairs to strength additions to the structure [3]. Like most concrete platforms, it was
constructed onshore and then towed to its permanent location. The towing of Ekofisk I is shown
in Figure 2 below. Towing the completed or semi-completed structures is much cheaper than
building the structure on-site, and installation can be completed in a matter of days as long as the
weather is good [4].

 Figure 2 The towing of Ekofisk I in 1973. Installed in the North Sea, Ekofisk I was the first offshore
 concrete platform and was used for 35 years with no major repairs or issues (Olsen, 2015)

Since the installation of Ekofisk I, there have been some 50 major offshore concrete platforms
constructed. Most are fixed-bottom gravity-based structures, but there are several floating
structures in use as well [4]. Many of the structures from the last four decades are still in use and
have needed little maintenance [1]. The biggest concern for marine concrete structures is the
corrosion of reinforcement by chlorides, but field investigations have shown that chloride
content is generally quite small on platforms, many of which are in the North Sea. As long as
structures have adequate and dense concrete cover, on the order of 50-60 mm, and low water-to-
cement ratio, reinforcement corrosion can typically be held off far beyond the design life.
Helland et al. (2010) demonstrated the excellent performance of concrete platforms by finding
low chloride contents in the majority of the 180 samples taken from various platforms [3]. Only
a few of the samples taken had low resistance, making them more susceptible to chloride ingress.
The authors contributed the low resistance to poor practices and quality assurance during the
concreting phase. Although offshore concrete has been used successfully for decades,
considerable care still needs to be taken in the design and construction phases to ensure long
service lives.
More recently, concrete has been considered in the offshore environment for structures other
than oil and gas platforms. Floating bridges have been used in both Norway and Washington
state, and floating tunnels are now being considered for use [2]. Additionally, concrete is being
considered for use in the offshore wind industry for both floating and gravity-based structures. A
floating wind turbine concept is shown in Figure 3.

 C-4

 Figure 3 A floating wind turbine designed by Dr.techn.OlavOlsen (Olsen, 2015)

2 Corrosion and Degradation in Marine Concrete
Concrete is one of the strongest and most durable construction materials in the world. It is
relatively easy to make and can be used in many different ways. Although it has many
advantages, namely its durability and versatility, it is also susceptible to cracking and corrosion
by aggressive substances. Marine concretes in particular, face aggressive agents such as salts and
sulfates that can weaken the concrete matrix and contribute to reinforcement corrosion.

2.1 Marine Concrete Exposure
Concrete is a permeable material that continually hydrates through a network of pores. The
hydration leads to a natural strength gain over time, but it also provides a network for deleterious
substances to enter the concrete matrix. As such, marine concrete must be dense and non-porous
in order to prevent harmful substances, such as chlorides, from entering the concrete.
Environmental conditions also influence concrete durability, particularly in the marine
environment. There are four zones of aggressiveness: the atmospheric zone, splash zone, tidal
zone, and submerged zone.
In the atmospheric zone, the concrete is affected by salts in the marine air, but not directly by the
seawater. It is typically characterized by chloride-induced corrosion and carbonation. The splash
zone is affected by wave height and wind, and most damage comes from chlorides. The tidal
zone is located between the minimum and maximum tides, and experiences chloride and
magnesium-induced corrosion, as well as degradation from waves and microorganisms. Both the
splash and tidal zones are subject to wetting and drying cycles. In deep-sea structures, the splash
and tidal zones are somewhat synonymous, as deep-sea structures are not so affected by tides.
The submerged zone is subject to degradation through chloride, magnesium ions, and
microorganisms. The lack of oxygen in the submerged zone restricts corrosion rates to lower

 C-5

levels [5]. Zuquan et al. studied fly ash and slag concrete in the four different exposure zones and
found that chloride content was the highest in submerged concrete due to the constant exposure
to chloride ions. Concretes in the splash and tidal zones showed visible damage, while concrete
in the submerged zone did not show any surface damage [6]. It is a challenge to predict how
concrete will behave in differing conditions because there are many factors that contribute to the
durability of marine concrete. As such, it can be challenging to choose the proper concrete mix
for a given use.

2.2 Concrete Degradation in the Marine Environment
Chloride and magnesium ions present the most threat to marine concrete and its reinforcement,
and cracking can exacerbate the issues they create. Chloride-induced corrosion occurs when the
chloride content of concrete, usually measured by the weight of cement, reaches a certain level
called the corrosion threshold. It is typically taken to be around 0.4%, but there are conflicting
reports on exactly what the corrosion threshold is for different types of concrete [7]. It is
inevitable that chlorides will penetrate the concrete, so prolonging the service life of concrete
structures is a matter of preventing and slowing chloride ingress in the long run. A corroded
marine concrete support is shown in Figure 4.

 Figure 4 Reinforcement corrosion in a marine concrete structure. The image shows excessive spalling
 and characteristic rust stains from reinforcement corrosion [8]

Once enough chloride ions penetrate the concrete, the passivity of the steel is destroyed, and the
presence of oxygen corrodes it, leading to cracking and spalling of the concrete cover [9].
Chloride attack is especially harmful in concrete located in tidal and splash zones, and typically
not as prevalent in submerged structures due to the lack of oxygen [5]. Submerged concrete is
more susceptible to chemical attack than reinforcement corrosion. On average, seawater contains

 C-6

35 g/l of dissolved salts, although the exact number varies by location. Chloride ions are the most
common, but seawater is also full of harmful sulfates that attack concrete [7]. Magnesium attack,
typically from magnesium sulfates, directly attacks the concrete substrate rather than the steel.
Calcium hydroxide is turned into magnesium hydroxide, which reduces the strength of the
concrete and creates internal stresses that can lead to cracking and spalling [10]. Most studies
and literature reviews have focused on the effects of chloride-induced corrosion rather than
magnesium or other sulphate attacks on marine concrete.
Once a marine concrete structure is damaged by corrosion, it is both challenging and expensive
to repair. It is estimated that any diving operation has 38 times the manpower and 100 times the
cost of a similar operation above water [9]. The structures can be hard to inspect, particularly
submerged ones. Furthermore, cracks and spalls can be hidden by marine growth or sediment. As
such, it is important to design strong and durable marine concretes in order to avoid the high
costs associated with repair and inspection. The best defense to reinforcement corrosion and
chemical attack is to use a low permeability concrete, typically achieved by using a low water-to-
cement ratio, adequate concrete cover, and supplementary cementitious materials. Chemical
admixtures, concrete and reinforcement coatings, and cathodic protection can be used as
additional protection for marine concrete.

2.3 The Influence of Cracking
Although strong in compression, concrete is brittle in nature. It is weak in tension, and therefore,
susceptible to cracking on the tension face. Steel reinforcement minimizes the emergence of
cracks, but cracking is ultimately inevitable. The size of cracks is dependent on factors such as
the concrete constituents, rebar spacing, and curing. Microcracks already exist within the
concrete matrix, and they can be made worse by external stresses, environmental conditions,
chemical reactions, or thermal gradients [11]. Cracking advances corrosion by allowing the
ingress of more harmful chemicals into the concrete matrix, which eventually causes more
cracking in the future.
One way to reduce cracking at the early age of a structure is to use types of cement with low heat
generation during curing. Types of cement that generate a high amount of heat can induce
thermal stresses on the concrete. Cementitious additives such as fly ash and blast furnace slag
can help reduce heat generation during curing. Superplasticizers can also reduce heat generation
during curing [7]. Drying shrinkage also causes cracking at an early age, but moist curing can
help minimize shrinkage. Today, there is a lot of interest in trying to find types of cement or
mixes with low heat generation during curing. Research is also being done on different
admixtures or nano-additives that can reduce cracks or even promote self-healing [1].
Minimizing or avoiding cracks in concrete, particularly marine concrete, would significantly
prolong the service life of a structure.
For now, there are several standards and codes that set maximum crack width limitations for
marine concrete. One of the foremost bodies involved in offshore concrete, DNV, sets the
maximum crack limits at 0.2 mm for all environmental exposures [12]. Alexander (2016)
mentions that the maximum crack width is often taken to be 0.3 mm [7]. Additionally, there are
numerous ways to calculate crack widths. According to Widianto et al. (2019), most crack width
provisions are semi-empirical and based on small-scale beam type tests [13]. They are not

 C-7

representative of large offshore structures, because they do not have the layers of transverse
reinforcement that are common in those structures. ACI codes limit crack width by limiting
reinforcement stress and rebar spacing [14]. Overall, there is no code that outlines how to
calculate crack widths in thick, heavily reinforced concrete like the concrete used in offshore
structures. In 2017, Borosnyoi and Balazs wrote a comprehensive review of the various models
and equations used to calculate crack widths. There is no globally accepted crack width
formulation, so the list was quite extensive. Crack widths are based on a complicated mix of
factors that make them a challenge to predict. Nonetheless, it is important to consider crack
widths in the design of marine structures because cracking can lead to issues with durability and
serviceability in the future [15].

3 Durability of Marine Concrete
The durability of concrete is influenced by the environment and its inherent material properties.
Concrete mixes can have vastly different properties depending on mix proportions, the types of
ingredients, and curing. In terms of marine concrete, more dense and less porous mixes are
preferred because they prevent chloride ingress. Some of the most simple and effective ways to
increase the durability of concrete involve basic mix design variables.

3.1 Durability and Basic Concrete Mix Design
Designing a concrete mix can be a complicated procedure, particularly when the concrete needs
to have high strength and high performance. There have been countless studies on marine
concrete in the past. The research has led to many technological advances that have made
concrete stronger and more durable. These breakthroughs have paved the way for larger and
more innovative concrete structures, but some of the most effective ways to increase concrete
durability involve basic mix design principles such as water-to-cement ratio and concrete cover.

3.2 Water to Cement Ratio
It is well known that lowering the water-to-cement ratio, or w/c, lowers the porosity of the
concrete and therefore increases its durability. The w/c affects the pore structure of the concrete
and slows the diffusion of aggressive agents into the concrete. High cement content and low
water content also lessen the amount of freezable water in concrete, making it less susceptible to
freeze-thaw damage [4]. Low w/c also increases the strength of concrete [16]. A study by Song,
Lee, and Ann looked at several factors influencing chloride transport in concrete, including w/c.
The authors found that an increase in w/c resulted in an increased likelihood of chloride ingress
[17]. Similarly, Ahn and Reddy also looked at the effects of w/c. They determined that concretes
with higher w/c ratios had a greater tendency to corrode than mixes with lower w/c ratios [18].
The results of the studies show that the higher the cement content in the concrete, the less
permeable it is.

There are several codes and standards that give recommendations for the design of offshore
concrete, including maximum w/c ratio. In fact, fib Bulletin no. 50 (2009), states that a low w/c
ratio is essential for high-performance concretes used on offshore concrete platforms [19]. ACI

 C-8

357R-84, which details design and construction requirements for fixed offshore concrete
structures, suggests that the maximum w/c ratio for submerged concrete is 0.45, and the
maximum ratio for concrete in the atmospheric and splash zone is 0.40 [20]. The DNV GL
standard for offshore concrete structures, DNV-ST-C502, recommends that the w/c ratio is not
greater than 0.40 in the splash zone, and not greater than 0.45 elsewhere. The standard also states
that a low w/c ratio is essential for the durability of high-performance concrete [12]. The British
standard, BS 6349, provides a maximum w/c of 0.35 for structures in the splash zone with a
design life of 100 years, and a maximum of 0.45 for structures with a 50-year design life [7].

3.3 Concrete Cover
Another design modification that can increase the durability of marine concrete is to increase the
cover depth. Providing adequate cover depth is perhaps the most simple and effective method for
protecting marine concrete. Larger cover depth gives more protection to the steel by keeping it
further away from harmful substances. This method only works to a certain extent, however,
because the cover depth influences the geometry and dimensions of a structure. Minimum
standards for cover depth should always be followed in the case of marine concrete because of
the added durability. A case study by Alexander and Mackechnie found that among a group of
over 20 marine concrete structures in South Africa, most of them had low cover and did not
follow the minimum standards. As a result, many of the structures showed severe spalling and
likely had a reduced service life [21]. Another case study by Polder and Rooji (2005) came to a
similar conclusion [22]. Among concrete structures along the coast of the North Sea, many of the
older structures had low cover depths. It led to higher instances of corrosion in the reinforcement
[22]. Both studies illustrate the need for proper cover in marine concrete in order to increase
service life and durability.

The above-mentioned standards also give recommendations on minimum concrete cover. ACI
357R-84 suggests that the submerged zone and atmospheric zone not subject to salt spray have at
least 50 mm of cover over reinforcing steel, and that the splash and atmospheric zone subject to
salt spray have at least 65 mm of cover [20]. DNV-ST-C502 suggests a minimum cover depth
based on the different exposure classes as well. A cover depth of 60 mm is suggested for the
atmospheric zone and submerged zones, and 70 mm is suggested for the splash zone [12]. The
British standard BS 6349 suggests a cover depth of 65 mm for the splash zone [7]. Although
cover depth affects the geometry and load-bearing capacity of a concrete structure, it is important
to consider the minimum cover depths and resulting impacts when designing offshore concrete
structures.

3.4 Aggregate
Aggregate choice is another important consideration for concrete mix design. Most offshore
concrete structures use high-performance, high-strength concrete, which needs suitably strong
and dense aggregate. In the past, it was common to use aggregate directly from the pit. Now it is
more common to use an aggregate that has been processed through crushing, fractioning, or
washing. Additionally, it is typical nowadays to use aggregate with a well-graded sieve curve
with small tolerances. This has led to increases in the strength and durability of high-

 C-9

performance concrete [1]. Aggregates used in offshore concrete structures should also be frost-
resistant and not susceptible to alkali-silicate reactions. An alkali-silicate reaction occurs when
aggregate contains forms of silica that react with the alkaline pore solution of the concrete [7]. It
results in an expansive gel that damages the concrete, but it can be avoided with proper
consideration.
There are two types of aggregate typically used in offshore construction: normal density
aggregate (NDA) and lightweight aggregate (LWA). Normal density aggregate is more
commonly used than lightweight aggregate because LWA is not usually dense enough to be used
in high-strength concrete [1]. On the other hand, LWA is better for floating structures.
Lightweight concretes can have 20-35% less density than normal weight concretes. There have
been some floating platforms, such as the Heidrun platform in the North Sea. The floating
platform Heidun is shown below in Figure 5.

 Figure 5 Floating platform Heidrun in the North Sea. All of the coarse aggregate consists of expanded
 clay1

Although LWA has a significant advantage in terms of weight, most offshore platforms are
gravity-based structures, and need significant weight in their base. LWA is not suitable for
gravity-based applications. However, Olsen (2015) suggests that it may be beneficial to use
NDA concrete in the lower parts of an offshore structure, and LWA concrete in the upper parts
[2]. That would create a significant advantage in terms of weight, but it could also create some
complications during construction. Research is currently being done on different types of
aggregate for marine concrete. The eventual goal is to have the strongest possible concrete with
the least amount of weight, and much of that depends on the aggregate.
While the water-to-cement ratio, concrete cover, and aggregate choice are important factors in
the design of marine concrete structures, they do not necessarily guarantee protection on their

1
 https://www.offshore-energy.biz/gallery-two-decades-of-oil-from-heidrun-norway/

 C-10

own. In order to create the most durable concrete, a low w/c ratio, proper cover, and suitable
aggregate must be combined with other protections such as supplementary cementitious
materials, admixtures, and coatings.

3.5 Supplementary Cementitious Materials
It is well known in the concrete industry that adding supplementary cementitious materials, or
SCMs, can increase the strength and durability of concrete. Cementitious materials include
pozzolans such as fly ash, blast furnace slag, and silica fume. It has been shown that SCMs make
the cement matrix more dense by decreasing the amount of capillary pores [17]. A fib bulletin on
offshore concrete structures states that supplementary cementitious additives are essential for the
durable, high-strength concrete needed for the structures [4]. SCMs are usually used at low levels
(5-25% of total binder weight), but it is becoming increasingly common to use as much as 40-
60% of total binder weight in order to make cement stronger, more durable, and more sustainable
[23]. There have been innumerable studies over the years on the effects of different types and
amounts of SCMs in marine concrete mixes.
Although the majority of studies came to the conclusion that adding SCMs to concrete increases
durability and often strength, many researchers had conflicting conclusions on the optimal
amount of SCM to use in a mix. Blast furnace slag, which comes from a byproduct of the ferrous
and nonferrous metal industry, is one of the most commonly used and researched additives. It is
well agreed that blast furnace slag reduces permeability and increases chloride resistance, but
different amounts of slag in the mixes sometimes had a negative effect on the properties of the
concrete. Vessalas et al. (2017) found that concrete with 40% slag reduced compressive strength
compared to a control, but the overall addition of blast furnace slag decreased water permeability
significantly [24]. A similar study by Seleem et al. (2010) compared the behavior of blast
furnace slag, silica fume, and metakaolin in marine concrete. The researchers determined that
blast furnace slag was the least efficient of the three in stopping chloride ingress, but the slag
addition still decreased permeability and increased strength compared to the control [25].
Tumidajski and Chan (1996) found that concrete containing 65% slag and 3% silica fume had
the most durability to magnesium brine when compared to other mixes containing silica fume
and fly ash. The study came to the conclusion that concrete containing slag is the most suitable
for resisting magnesium attack due to low calcium hydroxide contents [10]. It has also been
shown to be more resistant to combined chloride and sulfate attacks than normal OPC concrete
[26]. Although there appears to be little agreement on the optimal amount of blast furnace slag to
use in marine concrete mixes, it is an effective additive for marine concrete as long as it is
properly proportioned.
Blast furnace slag also appears to be a commonly used additive in offshore concrete structures. A
case study on six concrete structures along the coast of the North Sea reported that blast furnace
slag was used in all of the structures [22]. Similarly, a case study on marine concrete harbor
structures in South Africa found that structures with blast furnace slag and fly ash had improved
durability and chloride resistance [21].
Silica fume is another widely studied cementitious additive. A byproduct of the silicon metal and
ferrosilicon alloy industry, silica fume is incredibly fine and has been shown to increase the
strength of concrete [1, 13]. Silica fume has been used in the offshore concrete industry since

 C-11

1985, and it has become increasingly common in mix-designs. It was first used in small amounts,
around 2% of binder weight, but over the years, it has increased to 4-8% of binder weight. In
Norway, if other pozzolans are not used, the minimum silica fume content is 6% [13].
Several studies have focused on the effects of silica fume in marine environments, and it is
generally agreed that silica fume can be used in smaller percentages of the total binder weight. In
2010, Seleem et al. compared the chloride resistance of blast furnace slag, silica fume, and
metakaolin as concrete additives. Silica fume was found to have the highest compressive strength
and was the most efficient in stopping chloride ingress [25]. Shekarchi, Rafiee, and Layssi
(2009) studied the performance of silica fume concrete in the Persian Gulf after 3, 9, and 36
months of exposure. The mix containing 7.5% silica fume by weight of cement was the most
efficient in terms of strength and permeability. Mixes with higher silica fume contents did little
to further decrease the permeability of the concrete [27]. In a similar study to Seleem et al.,
Farahani et al. (2018) compared the performance of silica fume, metakaolin, and zeolite
concretes in a marine splash environment. The mix containing 10% silica fume had one of the
best performances in terms of chloride diffusion when compared to metakaolin and zeolite. Silica
fume was recommended for use in marine reinforced concrete structures due to its durability and
resistance to chloride ions [28].
Fly ash is another common additive for concrete. Similar to the other cementitious additives, it is
well agreed that fly ash decreases permeability and increases resistance to chloride ions, making
it a productive additive for marine concrete. In a 1991 study, Thomas tested the performance of
fly ash concrete in tidal conditions. The concrete mixes containing fly ash were less permeable to
chloride ions after two years of tidal exposure. Cracking was only seen in low-strength and
normal OPC concrete, demonstrating that fly ash adds strength and durability [29]. A different
study by Thomas a few years later came to the somewhat paradoxical conclusion that the
addition of fly ash decreased the chloride content needed to initiate corrosion, but still afforded
the steel better protection than OPC concrete. The behavior was most likely due to the increased
chloride binding capacity of fly ash concrete [30]. On the other hand, when testing the
combination of permeability reducing admixtures with different cementitious materials, Vessalas
et al. came to the conclusion that a mix with 25% fly ash by weight of cement decreased
compressive strength compared to a control. It was also found that fly ash had little effect on
water permeability [24]. Similar to the other SCMs, there are mixed reviews on the effectiveness
of fly ash, and the efficiency of the additive comes down to mixing it in proper proportions.

Ultimately, fly ash, slag, and silica fume are all beneficial to marine concrete because they
reduce permeability and allow for smaller cement contents. The durability of concrete with
SCMs outperforms Ordinary Portland Cement (OPC) concrete in almost every instance. The
corrosion rate of OPC, fly ash, and slag concretes are compared below in Figure 6, and it shows
that even in cracked concrete, SCMs lengthen the service life of marine concrete by lowering
corrosion.

 C-12

 Figure 6 Corrosion rate is OPC, fly ash, and slag concrete. The corrosion rate for OPC concrete is
 significantly higher in uncracked and cracked specimens [7].

Overall, supplementary cementitious additives are vital for improving the durability and
resistance of marine concrete. Moffat et al. (2019) states that plain OPC concrete cannot
withstand marine exposure for 50 to 100-year service lives, regardless of the w/c ratio and
concrete grade. SCMs are a vital part of a durable mix design, although care must be taken when
choosing the proper amount of SCM in a mix [23].

3.6 Cathodic Protection Systems
One of the more commonly used and successful protection systems for concrete are cathodic
protection. Metals have a natural tendency to corrode when they interact with the environment.
Corrosion consists of 4 parts: an anode, a cathode, a metallic path, and an electrolyte. The
mechanisms of corrosion are shown below in Figure 7.

 Figure 7 Mechanisms of corrosion in reinforced concrete [31]

If any of the four elements are not present, metal cannot corrode [8]. Cathodic protection systems
create alternative paths for electric currents that protect the steel from corrosion. There are two
types of cathodic protection: sacrificial anode (SACP) and impressed current (ICCP).

 C-13

3.6.1 Sacrificial Anode Cathodic Protection
Sacrificial anode systems use galvanic anodes that corrode sacrificially to provide electrical
current to the steel. The galvanic anodes are less noble than the steel they protect, creating a
difference in electrochemical potential that causes current flow from the external anode to the
reinforcement [31]. A typical SACP system is illustrated in Figure 8.

 Figure 8 A typical SACP system [31]

Zinc, aluminum, and magnesium are some of the metals that can be used in ribbons, meshes, or
coatings on the concrete surface. Because the anodes are sacrificial, the system cannot be
controlled, and it has a limited service life. On the other hand, SACP systems are self-regulating
and easy to install [8]. SACP systems have been successful in steel structures, but the high
resistivity of concrete creates a challenge. Galvanic anodes have low currents, and they cannot
always provide enough protection for reinforced concrete. On the other hand, the low voltage can
be better for prestressed structures, as there is less risk of embrittlement of the steel [31]. Byrne
et al. state SACP is best used for structures with shorter service lives or small repairs because the
limited service life of the anodes raises the possibility of a need for more protection to be
installed in the future [31]. While SACP can provide corrosion protection for structures,
depending on the environment and structural material, it is not always the best choice.

3.6.2 Impressed Current Cathodic Protection
Impressed current cathodic protection (ICCP) systems differ from sacrificial anodes because they
use a permanent external power source to deliver a protective current. The systems involve
complex wiring and maintenance, but have long service lives. They must be regularly adjusted so
the steel is not overprotected by the current [8]. Too much current can lead to embrittlement of
the steel. A typical ICCP system is shown in Figure 9.

 C-14

 Figure 9 A typical ICCP system with an external power source and monitoring system [31].

ICCP is used more often than SACP for reinforced concrete because it is better for structures
with long service lives, as concrete structures normally have. There is also more industry
experience and understanding of ICCP systems for concrete structures [31]. Florida DOT has
used ICCP systems to great success in both protection systems and structural rehabilitation.
Florida has a very corrosive climate for marine structures, so cathodic protection has been used
for decades. From their experience, ICCP systems are highly dependable and need little
maintenance. Titanium mesh systems worked particularly well, and were flexible enough to fit
most geometries [32]. Other ICCP anodes include magnetite, carbonaceous materials, high
silicon iron, lead alloys, and titanium. All are good conductors, can tolerate high currents, and
have a low rate of corrosion [31]. While it is generally agreed that ICCP provides adequate
protection to reinforced concrete, it does have a few drawbacks, namely its need for constant
power and monitoring. The higher currents can lead to embrittlement of the steel if not properly
adjusted. Bryne et al. (2016) suggested that renewable energy sources such as solar energy
should be used for ICCP systems, making them more sustainable in remote locations [31].

Cathodic protections are a reliable and historically successful method of protecting concrete
structures. Impressed current systems are often used as a means of protecting concrete
infrastructure such as bridges and other marine structures in shallow environments. Cathodic
protection is well understood and should be taken into consideration when designing marine
concrete.

3.7 Admixtures and Coatings for Marine Concrete
Another way to increase the durability of marine concrete is the use of admixtures and surface
coatings. There are several types of each, and numerous commercially available versions to
choose from. Superplasticizers are commonly used in marine concrete in order to lower the w/c
ratio and make concrete more workable.

 C-15

3.7.1 Permeability Reducing Admixtures
Permeability reducing admixtures protect concrete by making it less susceptible to the ingress of
harmful substances. The two main types of permeability reducing admixtures, or PRAs, are
hydrophobic and crystalline. Crystalline PRAs react with water to increase the density of the
concrete matrix by creating more calcium-silicate-hydrate (C-S-H) and blocking pores with
crystalline deposits. Hydrophobic PRAs contain water-repellents that form a thin hydrophobic
coating on pores in the concrete matrix to keep water from entering [24]. Both admixtures have
been used to varying degrees of success in studies on marine concrete.
In 2010, Dao et al. compared the performance of hydrophobic and crystalline PRAs in simulated
coastal environments. Samples were exposed for 28 days, one year, and two years. They came to
the conclusion that crystalline PRAs had little to no effect on reducing permeability, but
hydrophobic PRAs increased durability considerably [33]. Vessalas et al. also compared
crystalline and hydrophobic PRAs in a 2017 study. Researchers looked at different w/c ratios and
cementitious additives as well. In mixes with a w/c of 0.4, both hydrophobic and crystalline
PRAs increased strength compared to mixes without PRAs. The PRAs were more efficient in
mixes with a w/c of 0.6 than in mixes with a w/c of 0.4. Ultimately, researchers concluded that
the efficiency of PRAs in reducing permeability was more reliant on the w/c ratio rather than the
amount of PRA in the mix [24]. An interesting study by Borg et al. studied the effect of crack
width with crystalline PRAs. The crystalline PRAs increased the autogenous healing capacity of
concrete due to the formation of mineral deposits. Immersion in seawater actually helped the
concrete specimens heal, and small cracks were completely healed due to the combined action of
the crystalline PRA and salt deposits from seawater [34]. The studies showed that crystalline and
hydrophobic PRAs can increase strength and reduce the permeability of concrete, but they
depend on other characteristics such as w/c, cementitious additives, and cracking. On their own,
PRAs cannot adequately improve the durability of concrete.

3.7.2 Corrosion Inhibiting Admixtures
The other type of admixture sometimes used in marine concrete are corrosion inhibitors. The
most commonly used corrosion inhibitor is a calcium nitrite inhibitor, which prevents chloride
induced reinforcement corrosion by reacting with iron ions [35]. There are also organic-based
corrosion inhibitors, although they are less commonly used. Organic corrosion inhibitors absorb
onto the steel and suppress the cathodic and anodic reactions on the metal [35]. Calcium nitrite
inhibitors are more commonly studied than organic, but Kondratova et al. compared organic and
calcium nitrite corrosion inhibitors on cracked and uncracked slabs, and found that organic
inhibitors worked better than calcium nitrite. Both decreased permeability compared to a control,
but organic inhibitors had the lowest permeability overall. Both admixtures were inefficient in
cracked slabs [35].
A year later, Montes et al. (2004) studied the influence of calcium nitrite inhibitors in concretes
with varying w/c ratios and fly ash contents. Results suggested that a low w/c ratio with the
addition of fly ash and the corrosion inhibitor is effective in reducing cracks. The corrosion
inhibitor alone did not always protect the steel, and it was found to be ineffective in cracked
concrete. The corrosion inhibitor did little to protect steel in concrete that did not have fly ash
and had a w/c ratio of 0.45 [16].

 C-16

In an overview of corrosion protection in marine concrete, McDonald agreed that the
performance of corrosion inhibitors is poor in cracked concrete. He also states that some
inhibitors are water soluble and may leach from the concrete during long-term marine exposure
[36]. The inferior performance of corrosion-inhibiting admixtures in cracked concrete is a big
drawback, as cracking is almost always present in concrete due to its brittle nature. Although
corrosion-inhibiting admixtures can provide extra protection to steel reinforcement, their
efficiency is dependent on the environment and other aspects of mix design.

3.7.3 Marine Concrete Surface Coatings
Another method for preventing corrosion in marine concrete is the use of water-repellent or pore-
blocking surface coatings. Coatings are often used in combination with other protection systems
such as cathodic protections or sealers. The durability of coatings is directly affected by the film
thickness, number of layers, and chemical and abrasion resistance [37]. There are three types of
surface treatments: pore blockers, sealers, and pore liners. They are illustrated below in Figure
10.

 Figure 10 The three types of surface treatments, sealers (a), pore blockers (b), and pore liners (C) [38]

There are numerous commercially available coatings of all kinds for the marine concrete
industry, and several studies have been completed on the effectiveness of different external
coatings. Dai et al. (2010) studied different water-repellent and pore-blocking coatings in a dry-
wet marine cycle for one year. Pore blockers were found to be ineffective in preventing chloride
ingress, while silane-based sealers were the most effective in uncracked concrete. The
effectiveness of the coatings in cracked concrete depended on how deep the coating penetrated
into the concrete. In most cases, the coatings were not effective if they were applied before
cracking [39]. A similar study by Helene and Medeiros (2009) studied several surface treatments
used alone and in pairs. They used a series of laboratory tests to determine the permeability and
resistivity of the concrete after surface treatment. All systems were effective in preventing water
and chloride ingress, especially when more than one was used. The most effective systems used
a silane-based water repellent in combination with an acrylic coating. The authors ultimately

 C-17

determined that surface protection systems can extend the surface life of a concrete structure by
7.8 times, although that number would change under different environmental conditions [38].
Overall, concrete coatings can protect marine concrete from chloride-induced corrosion, but their
ineffectiveness in cracked concrete makes them inefficient when used as a single protection
system. Coatings are best used in combination with other protection systems in order to
minimize water and chloride ingress to marine concrete.

4 Reinforcement in Marine Concrete
Although mix design plays a large part in determining the durability of concrete, the type of
reinforcement also influences the service life of concrete. The use of steel reinforcement in
concrete provides tensile strength and reduces cracking. Unfortunately, steel has a natural
tendency to corrode, particularly in harsh environments exposed to salts or chlorides. Although
high-quality concrete with adequate cover will help protect it, it is not always sufficient. It is
important to consider the use of corrosion-resistant reinforcement as well as normal steel
reinforcement when designing marine concrete structures.
Black steel, or carbon steel, is the most common type of reinforcement. It has a high tensile
strength, and a similar coefficient of thermal expansion to concrete, which reduces stresses at the
steel-concrete interface [40]. It also has a natural tendency to corrode. It is often not suitable for
use in concrete structures with long service lives, particularly if the structure is exposed to de-
icing salts or a marine environment. Fortunately, there are a number of corrosion-resistant
options for reinforcement.
Epoxy-coated reinforcement, or ECR, is the most common corrosion-resistant reinforcement. It
was first used in 1973 in the United States, and it is now used all over the world in structures
where steel corrosion is a concern [40]. The epoxy coating is applied during the manufacturing
process, creating a barrier between the steel and harmful substances. Poursaee (2016) conducted
an extensive literature review of studies on epoxy-coated steel from 1980 to 2014. The studies
generally showed that ECR outperforms carbon steel in terms of corrosion, but its durability was
dependent on the manufacturing process and quality control. Damage to the coating or low
thicknesses significantly affected its long-term performance. Although still popular, some
agencies have discontinued the use of ECR due to inadequate performance. Both the Florida and
Virginia DOTs have moved towards different types of corrosion-resistant reinforcing [40].
Another type of corrosion-resistant reinforcement is galvanized steel. The rebar is coated with
zinc that is metallurgically bonded to the steel. The coating is harder than the steel beneath,
protecting it not only from corrosion but abrasions as well [40]. Galvanized steel can resist
corrosion in chloride concentrations up to five times that of black steel. It also slows the rate of
corrosion by maintaining passivation at lower pH levels [41]. Furthermore, zinc corrosion
products have less volume than iron corrosion products, which causes less damage to the
concrete in the event of corrosion [40]. The corrosion-resistant properties of galvanized rebar
make it a good choice for concrete in marine environments.
Stainless steel reinforcement is another corrosion-resistant option for concrete. Stainless steels
are iron-based alloys with a minimum chromium content of 11%. Some advantages include good
ductility, good weldability, homogeneity of microstructure, and the cut ends do not need
protection [40]. Stainless steel has high strength and long-term resistance to chloride attack, and

 C-18

it is used in structures with long service lives. Stainless steel has primarily been used in marine
structures or structures exposed to de-icing salt, similar to other types of corrosion-resistant
reinforcement [42]. Structures utilizing stainless steel reinforcement have been quite successful
in terms of reduced corrosion and increased service life. The biggest drawback, however, is the
high upfront cost of the steel [40]. As such, it is typically used in combination with black steel, in
areas of a structure that are more exposed to corrosion damage [42]. Stainless steel is a good
option for concrete structures in highly corrosive environments, although the upfront cost is
perhaps the most influential factor when considering it for use.
While steel is the most common type of reinforcement for concrete, nonferrous reinforcements
have emerged in recent years as a viable option, both as fibers and bars. Fiber-reinforced
polymers (FRP) are a nonferrous option for reinforcing bars in concrete. They can also come in
grids, sheets, cables, and plates, although bars are the most common. FRPs have a high strength-
to-weight ratio, do not rust, and expand at a similar rate to concrete [43]. FRPs usually consist of
steel, glass, or aramid fibers encased in a resin matrix. They are corrosion-resistant, lightweight,
and have excellent fatigue properties, except for glass FRPs. They are, however, expensive and
have a low modulus of elasticity and failure strain [44]. Glass FRPs, or GFRPs, are the most
common fiber-reinforced plastic. They are low cost and high strength, but their material
properties vary significantly based on the type of glass they are made from. Some glass types are
stronger or more corrosion-resistant than others, which is an important factor when choosing the
proper FRP. Scott (1999) gives an overview of the different types of FRPs. Carbon FRPs have
the highest tensile modulus, but a relatively low maximum strain. Aramid FRPs have a higher
failure strain than carbon, and it weighs about one-seventh of the weight of steel wires. Scott
states that FRPs are well suited to use in the marine environment because of their excellent
corrosion resistance properties [44].
Fiber reinforcement is another option for marine concrete. The fibers are dispersed randomly in
the concrete matrix, adding tensile strength, limiting crack growth, and allowing stresses to be
transmitted across cracks [44]. The performance of fiber reinforced concrete (FRC) depends on
the fiber material, geometry, distribution, orientation, and concentration [45]. In order for fibers
to perform adequately in concrete, they should have a high tensile strength, a modulus of
elasticity similar to or higher than concrete, and a similar Poisson’s ratio to concrete in order to
reduce lateral stress [44]. A cracked FRC is shown below in Figure 11. Fiber-reinforced concrete
has shown great performance in field applications, and depending on the type of fiber, it can also
be corrosion-resistant.
Reinforcing fibers can be made from glass, steel, organic, or synthetic materials. Scott (1999)
gives an overview of the different types of fibers and their properties. Glass fibers are limited by
exposure to alkalis, which corrodes the glass. They also lose strength over time, which is unideal
for structures with long service lives. Carbon fibers are resistant to high temperatures and
chemical attacks, but they are expensive. Organic fibers have an average tensile strength, low
rigidity, and a high ductile strain. They cannot completely replace steel bar reinforcement [44].

 C-19

 Figure 11 A cracked concrete pipe with synthetic fiber reinforcement. The pipe was under a large load
 and significant deflection, but the fibers resisted collapse [46]

A newer development in the field of fiber-reinforced concrete is the use of basalt fiber-reinforced
polymer, or BFRP. BFRP comes in both fibers and bars. It is made from melted basalt stone.
When compared to steel, BFRPs have a higher initial tensile strength, about 25% of the density,
more flexibility, and they are not susceptible to corrosion. On the other hand, they have a lower
modulus of elasticity than steel, and they must be carefully handled during construction [12]. In a
study on the durability of BFRPs in the marine environment, Lu et al. (2020) found that the bars
were not affected by chlorides, but were susceptible to the alkalinity of the environment. Highly
alkaline environments caused corrosion in the BFRP resin matrix. The study demonstrated that
the alkalinity of the environment should be considered when adding BFRPs to concrete [47].
Overall, there are many types of corrosion-resistant reinforcement to choose from. Chloride-
induced corrosion is the biggest threat to marine concrete, and the use of corrosion-resistant
reinforcement could prolong the service life and reduce the maintenance costs of large marine
structures. Although many of the reinforcements discussed above have high upfront costs, their
long-term benefits should still be considered.

5 Current Design Practices
As it stands, there are numerous offshore concrete oil and gas platforms, and comparatively less
concrete offshore wind structures. Although there have been some reviews and case studies on
existing offshore structures, there is fairly little information available on the mix design and
properties of the concrete. This section gives a brief overview of marine concrete construction
and a review of case studies on the performance of such structures.

5.1 A Brief History of Offshore Concrete
The first offshore concrete platforms were built in the 1970s and 80s in the North Sea. Many are
gravity-based, but several are floating. The platforms themselves are built onshore and then
towed out to sea. Once on site, the installation of the structures can be completed in a matter of

 C-20

days, as long as the weather is good. The process eliminates the need for costly offshore
construction. Furthermore, the structures are very robust, and have successfully withstood
intense loading, accidental ship impacts, and dropped equipment with little damage [4]. The
North Sea is an incredibly hostile environment in terms of wave and wind loads. Figure 12
shows the harsh and stormy environment that can buffet the platforms. Even with the
unfavorable conditions, the platforms have performed very well and needed little maintenance
[2].

 Figure 12 A concrete platform buffeted by wind and waves in the North Sea [2]

The first concrete platform was Ekofisk I in 1973, built off the coast of Norway for the storage of
crude oil. Since then, there have been over 30 major concrete platforms constructed. The design
strength of Ekofisk I was 45 MPa, and the water-to-cement ratio was 0.45 [3]. The water-to-
cement ratio of Ekofisk I is higher than most offshore platforms, as modern standards have set
the maximum water-to-binder ratio as 0.40 in most cases. The structures that followed Ekofisk I
had water-to-binder ratios of 0.40 or lower in order to have high strength and low chloride
ingress. Concrete mix design and technology have made significant improvements since Ekofisk
I in 1973. Advances in cement binders, aggregates, and chemical admixtures have all contributed
to the kinds of high-strength, high-performance concrete that is used in platforms and marine
concrete today. Newer platforms have strengths of 80 MPa or higher, while the first platforms
had strengths of around 45 MPa [20]. The average cement content of marine concrete has
decreased while average compressive strength has increased. Figure 13 shows the concrete grade
of offshore structures in the North Sea from 1974 to 2016. Testing methods and standards have
also improved accordingly, making the marine concrete design more standardized and successful
in terms of long and reliable service lives.

 C-21

 Figure 13 Compressive strength of 15 concrete structures from 1974 to 2016 [20]

A number of concrete platforms have also been installed in Brazil. Fernandes et al. (2008) gives
a brief overview of the platform design. Similar to the North Sea platforms, the structures were
built onshore and then towed to their offshore location. The concrete had an average compressive
strength of 50 MPa and a design strength of 35 MPa. Cementitious additives such as fly ash,
silica fume, and slag were used to increase strength and durability. Some structures used
metakaolin and even rice husk ash to increase strength. Typically, the binder content ranged from
380 to 500 / , and the water-to-cement ratio was reduced to 0.3. There was no information
about protection systems used on the structures, but they exhibited excellent performances in
terms of durability.
Advanced concrete mix design coupled with durable and strong reinforcement has made offshore
structures robust and durable, leading to long service lives. It is important to learn from marine
concrete mix designs in the past as well as modern mix designs in order to create the best
solution for offshore concrete structures in the future.

5.2 Case Studies on the Performance of Marine Concrete
There have been many great case studies on the performance of marine concrete structures. For
the most part, they have performed admirably, especially on offshore platforms. Most of the
deep-sea structures in the North Sea are still in use, with an average age of 25 years, and they
have needed relatively little maintenance [20]. Concrete structures that are constantly exposed to
tides and marine spray, like harbor structures, have had slightly less success. The need for repair
in marine concrete structures has mostly arisen from a lack of compliance with minimum cover
depth requirements, although poor construction and curing can lead to deterioration as well.

In 1997, Alexander and Mackechnie conducted a case study on harbor structures in South Africa.
It was found that many exhibited severe deteriorations, even on structures less than 20 years old.

 C-22

The main cause of the deterioration was due to poor compacting and curing practices and a lack
of compliance with minimum cover depths. The study also found that patch repairing of
structures did little to increase the service life, as the repairs often failed in a few years [21].
Polder and Rooij (2005) conducted a similar field investigation on six concrete structures in the
North Sea aged between 18 and 41 years. All the structures were shallow water harbor structures
containing blast furnace slag, but the cement content, water-to-cement ratio, and curing regimen
were not well documented. They visually assessed the concrete and used a model to predict
chloride profiles in the concrete. Only one showed visible corrosion damage, but all had high
levels of chlorides. The chloride profiles varied significantly, which the authors attributed to the
different degrees of exposure. For example, areas that were exposed to seawater splash but were
sheltered from rain had high surface chloride contents. While mix-design plays an important part
in improving the durability of marine concrete, so do the environment and construction practices.
Although the environment of harbor structures is vastly different from the deep sea, there is still
much that can be learned from the failures and successes of other types of marine concrete [22].
There have been relatively few case studies on offshore concrete platforms due to the challenge
of access. In 2010, Helland et al. conducted a study of chloride profiles from ten structures in the
North Sea, with service lives ranging from 2 to 26 years. 180 samples were taken, and each
sample was used to determine the chloride content of the concrete and an estimated design life
based on the chloride profiles. The authors stated that while the location of the samples was
supposed to be random, they were often taken near areas with visual defects. As such, it is
probable that the performance of the structures was slightly underestimated in terms of chloride
resistance [3]. The first North Sea concrete platform, Ekofisk I, was analyzed after 19 years as a
part of the study. Ten cores were taken, and the estimated service life of the different samples
ranged from around 10 to 120 years. The platform was designed for a 30-year service life, and
was ultimately retired after 35 years with no major defects or repairs. Another platform, Statfjord
A, had little to no chloride ingress. The authors attributed the excellent performance to the epoxy
coating on the concrete shafts in the splash zone. Amazingly, the coating was still intact 32 years
after being applied. Overall, many of the cores analyzed had estimated service lives of 100 years
or more, even though many of the platforms were not designed for such use. On the other hand,
there were a number of cores that had almost no resistance and had short, estimated service lives,
particularly in the Brent B platform. The authors guessed that the potential of the
underperforming samples was destroyed during the concreting process. The authors guessed that
damage occurred because the cover to reinforcement was less than the specification, the
compaction of the concrete was poor, or the concrete was poorly cast. The authors ultimately
came to the conclusion that the execution of concrete construction on site is just as crucial to
durability as the mix design. If the concrete quality is not monitored, even a highly durable mix
with 60 mm of cover will not have a long service life. The case study by Helland et al. is perhaps
the most comprehensive studies on offshore concrete platforms, and it demonstrates the superb
performance of concrete in the marine environment [3].

In 2013, da Costa et al. investigated the chloride profiles in a 25-year-old offshore concrete
platform in Brazil. Concrete samples were taken from the platform at different heights above the
water. Chloride content varied depending on the height above the water and directional
orientation. Some sides of the platform saw more wave loading, and therefore had more
 C-23

deterioration. As for the mix design, it was estimated that the w/c ratio was 0.44, but there was
no other mention of mix characteristics. The main purpose of the study was to model the
mechanisms of chloride ingress in concrete, but it ultimately demonstrated that chloride ingress
is strongly influenced by environmental conditions and the intensity of wetting and drying cycles
in exposed marine concrete [48].
There have also been several reviews of offshore concrete design practices over years, beginning
in 1975. Two years after the first concrete platform was installed in the North Sea, Moksnes
published an overview of the concrete technologies used in the design of the early North Sea
platforms Beryl A and Brent B. Both platforms were installed in 1975 after the Ekofisk tank. The
basic mix design for the platforms consisted of 400 to 450 / of cement and had a w/c ratio
ranging from 0.40 to 0.45. The mix contained a sodium lignosulfonate superplasticizer and a
vinsol resin air entrained in the splash zone to retard the setting time, improve workability, and
entrain air. The compressive strength of the different structural parts ranged from 53 to 57 MPa.
There is no mention of any protection systems in the paper, although it does go into great detail
about the construction and quality control aspects of the platform construction.
A few years later, in 1982, DNV GL published a conference paper that discussed design
practices and inspections of offshore concrete structures installed between 1973 and 1978 in the
North Sea. The structures appeared to be in excellent condition, with no harmful cracking or
corrosion. There was slight corrosion on the steel plates in the splash zone, but not enough to
impact the structure in a harmful way. Even in cases of unexpected loads or accidental impacts,
the structures performed very well and maintained little damage. The authors concluded by
stating that offshore concrete structures could likely be inspected less often in the future due to
their exceptional performance [9]. Another review by Ronneberg, discusses the general mix
design and characteristics of platforms in the North Sea. The author states that silica fume is
commonly used in the platforms due to the ease of procurement from metal production in
Norway. It is usually added in small amounts. The addition of just one to three percent of silica
fume by cement weight improved the pumpability and stability of concrete and added strength.
The strength addition from silica fume is not considered in the design because the concrete
already has such a high strength (55 to 65 MPa). Ronneberg also confirms that plasticizers are
regularly used, typically lignosulfonates or naphthalenes [49].
Overall, there is little mention of protection systems in any of the case studies and design
reviews, and relatively little information available on actual mix design. The articles and case
studies generally agree that the performance and durability of marine concrete structures are
exceptional, although characteristics of the concrete studies varied considerably from study to
study. Fib Bulletin no. 50 summarizes it best, stating that high-strength concrete for offshore
structures must have a low water-to-binder ratio, adequate concrete cover, pozzolanic additives,
high quality aggregates, and chemical admixtures such as superplasticizers and air entrainers
[19]. Additionally, Fossa and Widianto (2019) state that offshore concrete must be high strength,
heavily reinforced, able to float, resistant to freeze-thaw and chemical attack damage, and
suitable for slipforming. There are quite a few variables to consider for marine concrete design,
but as long as proper consideration is given to the mix design and quality control, offshore
concrete structures will have exceptionally long and successful service lives [20].

 C-24

6 Mix Designs for Use in this Project
At least three mix-designs will be used in this research program. These are a normal OPC
concrete, a high strength concrete, and a lightweight concrete. The basic mixes are discussed in
the following section.
The first mix design will be a normal, ordinary Portland cement mix. It will be similar to the mix
design used by Viswanath in his study on concrete fatigue in axial compression. The mix will be
used as a baseline, and it will complement the fatigue testing already completed by Viswanath.
The basic proponents of the mix will include a water-to-cement ratio of 0.54. This will be
lowered slightly, as most offshore concrete structures have a maximum water-to-cement ratio of
0.40. The ratio of cement to fine aggregate to coarse aggregate is 1:3:2 by weight. Portland
cement and limestone coarse aggregate will be used. The mix will also be modified to include a
cementitious additive, because most marine concrete mixes use SCMs to improve strength and
durability. The SCM will most likely be silica fume or blast furnace slag, as those seem to be the
most commonly used additives in marine concrete [50].
The second mix design will be a high-strength concrete, similar to the mix used by Tomann &
Oneschkow (2019) in a study on moisture content and the fatigue performance of high-strength
concrete. The mix had a water-to-cement ratio of 0.35 and contained no reactive additives other
than Portland cement. The cement itself is a high-strength Portland cement, type CEM I 42.5.
The concrete contained basalt aggregate up to ⅝ mm. The mix also used a polycarboxylate ether
plasticizer as well as a stabilizer. High-strength, high-performance concrete could be beneficial
for marine structures, particularly those with long desired service lives and severe loading [51].
In the oil and gas offshore industry, the average compressive strength of the platforms has
continued to grow upward, towards strengths in excess of 80 MPa [20]. It is likely that high-
strength high-performance concrete will be common in offshore constructions in the future.
The third mix design will be a lightweight concrete, similar to the mix design used for the
construction of the MPU Heavy Lifter, designed by Helland et al. [3]. The MPU Heavy Lifter is
a large floating concrete vessel designed to remove retired offshore installations. It is a multi-use
vessel, and can also act as a dock, support structure, or a floating production or storage unit. The
mix design contains entirely lightweight aggregate from expanded clay. The water-to-binder
ratio is 0.38, and the air content is 5%. The designers for the MPU Heavy Lifter came up with
two mixes, a hot weather and a cold weather mix. The only difference between the two is the
type of cement. The hot weather mix uses CEM III/B 42.5 N, a blast furnace slag cement with
low hydration heat and high sulfate resistance. The cold weather mix uses CEM I 52.5 N, a
normal Portland cement. The hot weather mix also contains silica fume and fly ash, at 9.5% and
22.8% of total binder weight, respectively. As Fossa and Widianto (2019) state, the advancement
of high-strength concrete with lightweight aggregate will lead to more cost-effective offshore
concrete structures in the future. Studying the fatigue performance of lightweight concrete will
only benefit the lightweight concrete industry in the future, particularly for marine installations
[20].

 C-25
