 D-4

1 Introduction to Loadings and Calculation Methods
According to the standard IEC 61400-3, the loads that must be considered in the design of offshore
wind turbines are as follows [1]:
 (i) Gravitational and inertial loads: These are the loads due to gravity of all components
 of the system, vibration and rotation of the moving components, and ground motions,
 which can result in static or dynamic loads on an offshore wind turbine.
 (ii) Aerodynamic loads: These loads result from the interaction of the airflow with the
 stationary and moving components of wind turbines. Aerodynamic loads mainly
 depend on the average wind speed and turbulence across the rotor plane, the rotational
 speed of the rotor, the density of the air, and the aerodynamic geometry of the wind
 turbine components and their interactive effects.
 (iii) Hydrodynamic loads: These loads are due to the interaction of water flow with the
 support structure of an offshore wind turbine. The hydrodynamic loads mainly depend
 on the kinematics of the water flow, the density of the water, the depth of the water,
 the shape of the support structure and the potential interactive effects.
 (iv) Actuation loads: These loads result from the operation and control of wind turbines.
 The main actuation loads include torque control from a generator/inverter, yaw and
 pitch actuator loads, and mechanical braking loads.
 (v) Other loads: Other loading scenarios include sea ice loads, wake loads, vessel impact
 loads, vortex-induced vibrations, seismic actions, etc.
Figure 1-(a) presents the types of loadings and loading frequencies.

 (a) Loadings and loading frequency (b) Complexity of wind field

 D-5

 Figure 1 Loadings that need to be supported by an offshore wind turbine support structure [2]

The turbulence from wind has two primary characteristics. One is the complex local variation that
is shown in Figure 1-(b). The other is the long-period variation in average wind speed across the
capture area of the rotor; this typically has a return period of 1-2 minutes, as shown in Figure 2.

 Figure 2 Mudline bending moments due to dominant wind and wave loadings [2]

 Figure 3 Wind turbine control setting

The local variation does not have significant effects on loading demands on support structures.
The long-period variation in average wind speeds (shown as a 100-second interval Figure 2) occurs
about 5 million over the operational period of turbine operation over 25 years. The magnitude of
these variations in force demands on the turbine would be less than this because the control of
blade pitch, nacelle yaw, and generator torque settings (see Figure 3) would be used to reduce
these variations in thrust load and power production, which are closely correlated. The period of
wave loadings typically ranges from 5-15 seconds depending on water depth, wind speed, and

 D-6

fetch. Figure 2 shows a wave with a 10-second period superimposed onto the 100-second long-
period variation in average wind speed. There would be about 50 million wave load cycles over a
25-year period, and the magnitude of these loads also depends on water depth, wind speed, and
fetch. The fatigue capacity utilization from waves ranges from 10-50% of fatigue capacity
utilization from all sources.

When the frequency of loading (f) is close to the natural frequency of the support structure (fo),
there is a dynamic amplification of these loading due to resonance; see Figure 4-(b). To limit such
forces, a soft-stiff design approach is used in which fo is smaller than the blade passing frequency
(3P at low RPM), and larger than the mass and aerodynamic imbalance associated with a 360-
degree rotation of the turbine (1P at high RPM). See Figure 4-(a).

 (a) Soft-Stiff design philosophy (b) Effect of frequency on loadings
 Figure 4 Consideration and impact of dynamics on force demands [2]

Fatigue stress demands can only be properly determined by using a fully probabilistic dynamic
time-history analyses that consider all geometries, material properties (including the seabed),
complexity of the wind and wave loadings, and all time-dependent behavior. The use of
Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD), or wave tank experiments, can also be necessary to
properly determine wave loadings.
The design of a support structure for an offshore wind turbine is commonly divided into three
phases:
 i. A preliminary phase in which there is limited metocean data, and only the approximate size
 of the wind turbine is known (e.g. 8 MW, 10 MW). This phase is introduced in section 2
 of this report, and then is fully described in section 7 (Appendix D-1).
 ii. An intermediate phase in which more complete metocean data is available such that a sea
 state table can be generated, and where an Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM) such
 as GE, Siemens-Gamesa, or MHI Vestas has provided a Markov matrix of wind turbine
 loads. This is introduced in section 3 of this report and then more fully described in section
        8 (Appendix D-2).
 iii. A final phase in which dynamic-time history analyses are conducted by the OEM, and
 where the designer of the support structure has provided the stiffness, strength, masses, and
 other characteristics of the support structure for this analysis. This is the step when the

 D-7

 design of the structure, particularly for Fatigue Limit State (FLS), is optimized and it can
 involve a few iterations between the designer of the foundation and the OEM. This is
 introduced in section 4 of this report and the more fully described in section 9 (Appendices
       D-3) and section 10 (D-4).
Section 5 of this report presents how time series analyses are being used in this project to assess
fatigue stress demands, and the selection of stress ranges that will be used in the preliminary testing
program.

2 Preliminary Design
In the preliminary design phase, the designer will use basic information on wind speeds and waves,
rules of thumb, and many assumptions in order to determine a geometry of the support structure
using “hand-calculations”. This process is shown in Figure 5. These calculations can be
implemented on paper, but are more conveniently done in spreadsheets, MATLAB programs,
Mathcad, and other tools in which the designer is fully responsible for inputting every equation
that is used in the process [3].

 • Specify geometry and material parameters for turbine and substructure
 • Specify turbine operation and soil parameters used to find natural frequency
 User • Specify metocean parameters used to calculate environmental loading conditions
 Inputs

 • Calc_Structural: Calculate stiffness parameters and natural frequency of the system
 Structural
 Calcs

 • Calc_Wind: Calculate wind-driven loads and moments
 Loading • Calc_Wave: Calculate wave-driven loads and moments
 Calcs

 • Calc_Geo: Calculate moments and deflections along the pile
 • Calc_Fatigue: Estimate fatigue life using modified E-1 environmental loading
 Checks & • Outputs: Compare calculated loads with ULS; evaluate frequency spectra
 Outputs

 • Sensitivity: Evaluate model sensitivity to discrete values of a model parameter
 Refine • Optimization: Minimize structure mass by altering pile diameter and thickness
 Design

 Figure 5 Flow chart of spreadsheet model processes

The geometry is selected to satisfy Ultimate Limit State (ULS) requirements in which
combinations of significant and extreme wind and wave loadings are considered, such as shown
in Table 1. In this table, the following abbreviations are used: NTM (Normal Turbulence Model),

 D-8

ETM (Extreme Turbulence Model), EOG (Extreme Operating Gust), ESS (Extreme Sea State),
EWH (Extreme Wave Height), UR (Rated Wind Speed), and UOUT (Cutout Wind Speed).
 Table 1 Critical combinations of wind and wave loadings [4]

The applied lateral loads from wind (Fwind) on the rotor, and then the bending moments on the
support structure (Mwind), are calculated using the equations given in Figure 6for the cases stated
in Table 1. As shown in the red box of Figure 6, the wind force (or thrust) is proportional to the
density of the air (ρa), the area encompassed by the rotor (Ar), a thrust coefficient (CT), and the
square of the wind velocity (U2). This thrust coefficient is affected by control setting (i.e. pitch,
yaw, generator torque), The maximum wind velocity is equal to the mean wind speed plus that due
to long-period average turbulence effect that was shown in Figure 2. The bending moment is equal
to the lateral force times the distance from this force to the level of the evaluation for which the
stress range is being evaluated. In the red box, this distance is shown as the water depth plus the
distance of the nacelle to the water surface (S + z).

 D-9

 Figure 6 Relationships for calculating forces and bending moments Resulting from wind

The forces and bending moments created by waves can be calculated by linear wave theory using
Morison’s equations which are shown in Figure 7. The forces over the height of the support
structure (dF) are a function of height (Hm) of waves, their period (Hs), the geometry of the
structure, and the drag and inertial coefficients (Cm and Cd).

 D-10

 Figure 7 Relationships for calculating forces and bending moments by Linear Wave Theory

The pattern of total moments (M) as a function of time from wind (Mwind) and waves (Mwaves) are
then added together as shown in Figure 2. The stresses from the moment at any point in time can
be taken as equal to the bending moment divided by the Section Modulus (Sm); Sm is a geometry
property of the cross-section and is equal to 2πr2t for a hollow cylinder of radius r and thickness t.
The required geometry of a section is usually determined to satisfy the relationship below in which
σdesign_strength is the maximum stress capacity of the material used in design. For steel, this
commonly around 300 MPa (45,000 psi), and for concrete in compression this varies from about
30 – 100 MPa (4,500 – 14,500 psi).
σdesign_strength > (Load Factor) x (Mmax/Sm) where the load factor varies from 1 to 1.35 by IEC
standards
The FLS assessment is done as a check. The most common assumption for the fatigue analysis is
that the fatigue damage accumulates linearly (i.e., the damage from the nth cycle is not dependent
on any of the previous cycles). This approach is given by the Palmgren-Miner’s rule [5]; see
Equation 1, and where DC is characteristic cumulative fatigue damage, k is number of stress blocks,
ni is number of stress cycles in the stress block i, and Ni is number of stress cycles to failure at
constant stress range of ∆σi. For steel structures, in which the structure may be taken as linear
elastic, it is possible to assume a particular distribution of moments and stresses from which to
determine damage equivalent loads for conducting a FLS assessment. Section 7 (Appendix D-1)
presents an example on how this approach can be implemented.

 D-11

 = ≤1 Equation 1

As presented in the report on section 8 (Appendix D-2), the FLS assessment for concrete structures
is more difficult because concrete is not a linear elastic material because of the development of
internal cracking and because damage caused by the higher stress levels in the initial stages
negatively influences the additional damage caused by subsequent lower stress levels. There has
the effect that there is not a linear log-log relationship between the imposed stress ranges and
number of cycles of loadings that can be resisted as was the case for steel. Because of this, it is not
possible at this point in the understanding of fatigue performance of concrete structures to make a
FLS assessment using the preliminary design method presented in this section. The reasons for
this are discussed in the next section.

3 Design Markov Matrix and Sea State Table
The next significant stage in the maturity of the design process is when a specific turbine is selected
for use in the design process such that the Original Equipment (Turbine) Manufacturer (OEM) is
able to provide a Markov Matrix for wind loadings from the turbine. This matrix consists of the
number of cycles of loadings for many combinations of Mean Moments and Moment Ranges.
Figure 8 presents a made-up example of part of a Markov Matrix in the top part of this figure in
which the Mean Moments ranges are defined in the left column, the Moment Ranges are in the
second row, and the numbers in green are the number of cycles of occurrence for each combination.
The OEM would have determined these values using the type of dynamic time history analyses
that are described in section 4, and where the sectional force values are given at the base of the
tower as shown on the right side of Figure 8. The information in lower table in Figure 8 illustrates
how stresses can be determined from these moments.

 D-12

 Figure 8 Markov Matrix example and determination of associated stress ranges

For wave loadings, this is when a Sea State Table is derived from sufficiently detailed metocean
data such that the number of occurrences for different Extreme Sea States is provided. An example
of such a sea state table is shown in Figure 4-9 in which the number of occurrences for different
significant wave heights and periods are shown for a short time window. The process for
determining the full Markov wave matrix, and then how to combine the wind and wave matrices
for making a full FLS assessment, is presented in section 8 (Appendix D-2). This Appendix uses
the following steps and inputs.
 1. Chose an elevation to establish loads for
 2. Establish turbine fatigue loads at this elevation
 3. Establish wave fatigue loads at this elevation
 4. Combine turbine and wave fatigue loads
The method uses the following data as input:
  Turbine loads: Markov matrices at the level of interest, as illustrate in Figure 8,
 with wind rose data
  Wave loads: Hs-Tp scatter diagram, as illustrated in Figure 9, with using wave rose
 data

 D-13

 Figure 9 Described Sea State (Hs-Tp scatter diagram)

4 Time-Series Analyses
Multiple loading scenarios act on offshore wind turbines in which their characteristics significantly
vary through the time, therefore a multi-physics modeling approach and dynamic time series
analyses are required to capture the response of the system to various loading conditions.
Consistent with the multi-physics loading phenomena acting on offshore wind turbines, fully
coupled aero-hydro-elastic time domain simulations are required for the load calculations of their
support structures. Several computational programs have been developed by different research
institutes or companies involved in the wind engineering industry to perform such time-demanding
simulations. OpenFAST is one of these platforms which is a modularized multi-physics modeling
tool which has been developed by NREL to perform time domain analyses on the horizontal axis
and vertical axis, on-land and offshore, wind turbines. Figure 10 schematically represents the
OpenFAST modularization framework [6].

 D-14

 Figure 10 OpenFAST modularization framework for multi-physics engineering modelling [7]

As it can be seen in the Figure 10, the OpenFAST platform has been created based on several
modules in which each one simulates the effect of different loading scenarios coupled with the
response of system obtained from the other modules within the OpenFAST modularization
framework. The main modules of OpenFAST with short explanation of their input information
and output data are as below:
 (i) ElastoDyn, BeamDyn and SubDyn: These modules calculate the structural dynamic
 loads on tower and rotor-nacelle-assembly (RNA), blades, and offshore support
 structure respectively.
 (ii) AeroDyn and InflowWind: These modules use the inflow wind characteristics in the
 wind site (such as wind speed, wind shear profile, wind turbulence properties, etc.)
 and the geometry of the blades, RNA, and tower to calculate the aerodynamic loads
 on wind turbine.
 (iii) HydroDyn and MoorDyn: These modules use the water flow characteristics
 estimated based on the measured metocean data in an offshore wind site (such as water
 density, significant wave height, wave peak spectral period, current profile, marine
 growth, etc.) and shape of offshore support structures to estimate the hydrodynamic
 loads acting on fixed and floating support structures respectively.
 (iv) ServoDyn: This module uses the control system input variables (such as blade pitch
 rate, maximum blade pitch maneuver, nacelle yaw control mode, high-speed shaft
 brake properties, nacelle tuned mass damper. etc.) to calculate the control and
 electrical-drive dynamics and actuation loads on wind turbines.
In order to estimate the fatigue life of offshore wind turbine support structure, at least six 10-
minute or one 1-hour time-history simulations are required for each wind speed bin according to
the standard IEC 61400-3. A full 1-hour time domain simulation will provide the total loads in
different components of a support structure. These loads can then be used to estimate the stress
time-histories at critical locations. Figure 11 demonstrates a stress time-history at a critical spot

 D-15

(at mudline) of a monopile support structure as an instance. The first 30 seconds of the simulation
was discarded to alleviate the potential impact of the transient state of wind turbine operation on
the results of the fatigue assessment.

 Figure 11 Stress time-history at a critical location at mudline level of a monopile substructure

A cycle counting algorithm, such as the Rain Flow algorithm, can then be applied to the estimated
stress time-histories at critical locations of a support structure to obtain the ranges of stress cycles
and the number of cycles for each stress range [8]. The total number of fatigue cycles for each
stress range over the design lifetime of an offshore wind turbine (typically 25 years) can be
extrapolated from the calculated number of cycles over the duration of time domain analysis. A S-
N curve provided by common standards or recommended practices, such as DNVGL-RP-C203 for
steel structures [9] and DNVGL-ST-C502 [10] for concrete structures, can then be used to obtain
the allowable maximum number of cycles for each stress range and the Palmgren Miner’s rule is
applied to estimate the fatigue damage indexes for the critical spots on the support structure. A
detailed example for application of the time series analyses for fatigue life estimation of offshore
wind turbine support structures is provided in section 7 (Appendix D-1).

5 Use of Time Series Analysis with 15 MW Reference Turbine for
  Determining Stress Demands
To estimate stress demands in offshore wind turbine support structures, the 15 MW reference wind
turbine is used. This turbine was developed in an IEA Wind Task 37 project [11], and is based on
a monopile support structure with geometry configurations and material properties as defined in

 D-16

the Technical Report of the IEA Wind TCP Task 37 which was published in March of 2020. A
schematic representation of the 15 MW reference wind turbine is presented in Figure 12 and its
main properties along with characteristics of the monopile support structure are summarized in
Table 2.
 Table 2 Key parameters for IEA Wind 15 MW wind turbine as compared to DTU 10 MW wind turbine
 [11]

 D-17

The hub height for this OWT is 150 meters above the MSL, and the RNA (Rotor-Nacelle
Assembly) with a rotor diameter of 240 meters is supported by 129.5 meters linearly tapered
tubular steel tower. The monopile which was used for this study is a modified version of the
original steel monopile which is presented in Table 2. For this Task, a tubular concrete cylinder
with 45 m length and constant section properties was used to estimate the stress demands in a
concrete support structure. 30 meters of the monopile is embedded in the water from a presumed
rigid foundation at the seabed up to the sea surface, and the remaining 15 meters covers the distance
between MSL and the transition piece (TP). The variation of the key control system parameters,
generator power and rotor thrust, and aerodynamic performance coefficients with inflow wind
speed at the hub height of the wind turbine are shown in Figure 13.

 Figure 12 The IEA Wind 15 MW reference wind turbine [11]

 D-18

 Figure 13 Performance of control system of the 15 MW reference wind turbine [11]

In order to estimate the stress demands, including mean stresses, stress ranges, and the number of
cycles of each stress range with each mean stress, in the support structure of the 15 MW reference
wind turbine, full 1-hour time domain simulations were performed to provide the time-histories of
total acting loads on the support structure which will result in estimation of stress time-histories in
the critical locations. The time domain simulations are performed for a variety of wind speeds (i.e.,
wind speeds between cut-in and cut-out speed of wind turbine) in which the wind turbine is in the
normal operation condition. Figure 14 presents the histograms of the fore-aft moments at the
mudline level of monopile which were obtained from time series analyses using the OpenFAST
program at three wind speeds of 6, 12, and 18 m/s which are below the rated wind speed, close to
the rated wind speed, and above the rated wind speed of the reference wind turbine respectively.
It should be noted that the number of cycles presented in this figure are obtained from the 1-hour
simulations, therefore they should be multiplied by a factor of 24 (hours/day) × 365 (days/year) ×
25 (years) in order to estimate the fatigue damages during the design life of the wind turbine.

 (b)

 (c)
Figure 14 Histograms of fore-aft moment for wind speeds of: (a) 6 m/s, (b) 12 m/s, and (c) 18 m/s

 D-21

6.1 Introduction
This spreadsheet model is intended to aid in preliminary design of a wind turbine monopile
foundation. Many of the formulas originate from the following primary references:
  Bhattacharya, Subhamoy. Design of Foundations for Offshore Wind Turbines. Wiley,
 2019. [12]
  Arany, Laszlo, et al. “Design of Monopiles for Offshore Wind Turbines in 10 Steps.” Soil
 Dynamics and Earthquake Engineering, vol. 92, Jan. 2017, pp. 126–52. DOI:
        10.1016/j.soildyn.2016.09.024. [2]
The spreadsheet was developed as a course project for Tufts CEE-293: Offshore Wind Structures,
taught by Dr. Dan Kuchma over the Spring 2020 semester. The spreadsheet is meant to be a
learning tool. By developing this model in Excel, the formulas and mathematical relationships are
accessible to a broad range of users. Our intention is to provide a functional, well-documented tool
that future students can build upon within a class or research context. This tool is not
comprehensive of all design considerations, and we hope others will carry it forward and continue
to build out additional functionality in future versions.
6.2 Overview of Tool
The progression of information within the model is presented as a flow chart in Figure 15.
 • Specify geometry and material parameters for turbine and substructure

 Figure 15 Flow Chart of Spreadsheet Model Processes

 D-22

 1. User Inputs: User input is provided exclusively within the “User_Inputs” tab. Here, the
 user can change parameters related to the tower and substructure dimensions and material
 properties, metocean parameters, and soil conditions.
 2. Structural Calcs: Once the “User_Inputs” tab is complete, the model automatically
 calculates intermediate parameters in the “Calc_Structural” that are required to find the
 loading and structural natural frequency. The natural frequency is useful for determining
 dynamic amplification factors (DAFs) and for checking the natural frequency against the
 1P and 3P frequencies to avoid structural resonance.
 3. Loading Calcs: The environmental loadings from wind and waves are calculated in the
 “Calc_Wind” and “Calc_Wave” tabs for eight separate cases (U-1 through U-4 and W-1
 through W-4) and five combined cases (E-1 through E-5).
 4. Checks & Outputs: The “Calc_Geo” tab finds deflection and moments along the
 embedded pile length. Environmental loadings are used within “Calc_Fatigue” tab to
 estimate the fatigue life using S-N curves. The “Outputs” tab summarizes environmental
 loadings, fatigue life estimations for different weld classes, and calculated frequency
 ranges for the system. Ultimate limit state (ULS) checks are included to help with design.
 5. Refine Design: The “User_Inputs” and “Sensitivity” tabs provided automated features to
 help the user better understand the model and make appropriate design selections. Pile
 design optimization capabilities are embedded within the “User_Inputs” tab to minimize
 the overall mass of the substructure design.
6.3 User Inputs
User input is provided exclusively within the “User_Inputs” tab (shown in Figure 16). Here, the
user can change parameters related to the tower and substructure dimensions and material
properties, metocean parameters, and soil conditions. Within this tab, the user is introduced to a
color-coding scheme used throughout the spreadsheet:
  Yellow cells: These are cells where the user can and should enter values
  Grey cells: These are cells where formulas are built in or automated output is
 provided. Avoid overwriting these cells unless you have a compelling reason to do so.
 Spreadsheet functionality may be impacted.
  Green cells: These are cells where particularly important calculated values (model
 output) are provided. Model outputs are flagged in a different color for the user’s benefit.
 Many are used in subsequent calculations elsewhere in the model.
  Red cell outline: This formatting flags model parameters that affect the wave number
 calculation. If you change any of these parameters, click the button to update wave loads
 after doing so.
The “User_Inputs” tab is also the launch point for a few built-in automated features to aid in the
design process. Those features are discussed in the Automated Design Tools section of this
guide.

 D-23

 Figure 16 Screenshot of “User_Inputs” tab (2020-05-07 version)

6.3.1 Turbine Structural Parameters
The turbine substructure parameters are the main way the user will interact with the spreadsheet
tool. The metocean data and soil parameters are site-specific and should come from external data
sources—they should not change as part of the design process. The turbine structural parameters
are where the user has a chance to control the structural responses.
Some turbine structural parameters are more malleable than others. For example, the dimensions
relating to the tower are typically given to the substructure designer by the original equipment
manufacturer (OEM). The main relationships that the user can and should edit are related to the
pile and the transition piece. We suggest more research be done to understand how transition piece
design is dependent on pile design, and vice versa.

6.3.2 Metocean Data (aka Wind and Waves)
The “User_Inputs” tab collects the minimum information needed to characterize wind and wave
conditions into the cases set forth in the two primary references used to support this project
(Bhattacharya, 2019; Arany et al., 2017). Refer to the Environmental Load Cases section for a list
of the cases, and refer to the primary sources for the supporting theory behind each case.

 D-24

Wind inputs include the rated and cut-out wind speed, which are characteristics of turbine
operation, and the average wind speed, which is a characteristic of the project site. The reference
turbine intensity is used in calculation of wind speed standard deviation for different turbulence
models. The Weibull distribution parameters are used to estimate the 1-year and 50-year return
interval extreme wind speeds. If the user wishes to bypass the Weibull approach, they can do so
by directly entering the extreme wind speeds into the Calculations sections of the “Calc_Wind”
tab.
Wave inputs include water depth, coefficients, and 50-year recurrence interval wave
characteristics. Information about significant and maximum wave heights and periods can usually
be obtained for particular metocean buoys at the websites of scientific agencies. The 1-year
recurrence interval wave characteristics are estimated based on the 50-year information. If the user
wishes to do so, they can directly enter information about 1-year recurrence interval wave
characteristics into the Calculations sections of the “Calc_Wave” tab.
After entering wave inputs, click the button to update wave loads. This will solve an implicit
formula for wave number and re-calculate the load outputs accordingly. All cells with a dark red
outline around their respective variable name affect the wave number calculation. If you change
any of those inputs, be sure to click the button to update wave loads after doing so.

6.3.3 Soil Parameters
Offshore geologic and geotechnical data are fundamental to representative analyses for offshore
structures. This spreadsheet provides guidance on how to carryout preliminary analyses of offshore
wind turbines (OWTs) in predetermined geotechnical conditions. The exact methodology for
implementing these geologic and geotechnical criteria is show in in Figure 17. The implementation
of these parameters provides necessary inputs for the computation of the natural frequency of the
OWT system—as well as a preliminary analysis to defining load cases and in-situ conditions.

The geometry of the monopile supported OWT are delineated in the “User_Inputs” tab. These
include fundamental parameters including pile diameter, length, thickness, density, and yield
strength. To characterize the soil conditions of the pile, the geotechnical and geologic relations
were established to be a function of: ( , ), Where A is a coefficient and is the submerged
unit weight of the bearing strata. These parameters were delineated for the spreadsheet because
they can provide a reasonable first-estimation of expected behavior. More representative data
could be utilized, given that it is available, to further characterize soil conditions at higher
resolution including: Cone-Penetration Tests results, shear-wave profiles, laboratory testing, etc.
We assume a singular layer that is to be characterized by A and .
To maintain ease of use and simplicity within this current version of the spreadsheet, the soil at
the turbine location will be considered to be SAND, or Normally Consolidated (NC) clay—as they
exhibit similar behavior. The analysis of over-consolidated (OC) clays, or bearing strata, are
outside the scope of this version of the spreadsheet. The ground model and monopile idealization
that is defined within this version of the spreadsheet is shown in Figure 18. This model assumes a
soil that assumes a linear increase of soil-modulus with depth.

 D-26

 Figure 18 Generic soil profile for linearly increasing modulus profile

This assumption is fundamental to the analysis of the monopile to lateral loading. This ground
profile allows for the utilization of the non-dimensional solutions for vertical piles subjected to
lateral loads that were delineated in Matlock and Reese (1960) [13]. These relationships allow for
the estimation of deflection, slope, moment, and more. These are exhibited in the Calc_Geo
section.

6.4 Outputs
Model outputs are summarized within the “Outputs” tab (shown in Figure 19). The “Outputs” tab
summarizes environmental loadings, fatigue life estimations for different weld classes, and
calculated frequency ranges for the system. Ultimate limit state (ULS) checks are included to help
with design.

 D-27

 Figure 19 Screenshot of “Outputs” tab (2020-05-07 version)

6.4.1 Environmental Load Cases
The spreadsheet uses the same load cases that are proposed in the 10-step paper from Arany et al.
(2017) [2], reprinted below in Table 3. The “Outputs” tab of the spreadsheet reports mudline forces
and moments for each design case. Associated mudline stresses are also reported, along with the
corresponding ULS ratio. If the ULS ratio exceeds 1.0 for any case, conditional formatting will
trigger that cell to turn red.
Table 3: Representative Design Environmental Scenarios as Load Cases Chosen for Foundation Design

6.4.2 Frequency Diagram
The frequency diagram is provided in the “Outputs” tab to show the user where the natural
frequency of the structure falls relative to the wind and wave spectrums and the 1P and 3P bands.
The plotted 1P and 3P frequency bands include a 10% buffer for safety.

 D-28

6.4.3 Fatigue Life
The fatigue life presented in the “Outputs” tab is given in years and represents the fatigue life after
the DFF from the “User_Inputs” tab is applied. Currently the mudline is treated as a region that is
not able to be inspected, so the DFF is set to 3. This means that the original fatigue life calculated
from the Palmgren Miners rule is then divided by three. If the user can justify an inspection regime
for the mudline, the DFF could be reduced to 2.

6.4.4 Foundation Deflection
From the framework delineated in the “Calc_Geo” tab, the current version of the spreadsheet
computes distributions of moments and deflections along the length of our monopile of interest.
We evaluate that the distributions of deflection and moments resemble those that are expected for
the generic p-y case in Figure 20.
Within the scope of the spreadsheet, we recall that complete fixity is assumed at the mudline. This
allows us to proceed with using the work delineated in Matlock and Reese (1967)—this framework
can be seen in the Calc_Geo section [13]. The computed distributions can be seen in Figure 21.
The response of the monopile will change as a function of loadings that are generated by the
“Calc_Wind” and “Calc_Wave” tabs, as well as with the geotechnical parameters that are
embedded in the “User_Inputs” tab.

 Figure 20 p-y concept for laterally loaded pile for soil with linearly increasing soil modulus [13]

 D-29

 Figure 21: Deflection and moment distribution along pile subjected to laterally loading

6.5 Automated Design Tools
The spreadsheet includes two automated design tools to help the user better understand the model
and make appropriate design selections.

6.5.1 Sensitivity
Sensitivity analysis capabilities are embedded within the “User_Inputs” tab and output to the
“Sensitivity” tab. The user can select any parameter from the drop-down menu shown in Figure
22. The drop-down menu is populated using Excel’s data validation feature, which links to a list
of named cells in Column U:X of the “Outputs” tab. The list includes input parameters and
calculated values, and it should be augmented as new features and named cells are added to future
versions of the spreadsheet. Running a sensitivity analysis will not overwrite input values or
formulas within the target cells.

 Figure 22 Screenshot of sensitivity analysis feature within the “User_Inputs” tab (2020-05-07 version)

The user can test the model sensitivity to only one variable at a time. Three discrete must be
provided. Once the user pushes the button to Generate Sensitivity Chart, VBA code will be
initiated that runs the model and pastes output to the “Sensitivity” tab, as shown in Figure 23. The
code can be viewed using the keyboard shortcut Alt+F11. Avoid making manual edits to the
“Sensitivity” tab, as it will likely compromise the automated features built into the buttons. Each
time the user runs a sensitivity analysis, the topmost tables and charts will be updated with new

 D-30

model results. Any number of analyses can be saved using the Save Current Analysis button, which
makes a static copy below the top-most analysis. The Delete Saved Results button will delete
everything below the top-most analysis.

 Figure 23 Screenshot of “Sensitivity” tab (2020-05-07 version)

Each tested value is color coded as blue, orange, or grey within the tables and charts. The bar chart
shows the stresses that are present at the mudline for different environmental loading scenarios,
enabling easy visual comparison. The line chart shows frequency ranges, with the dots representing
the structural natural frequency for each evaluated scenario. The table results use conditional
formatting to highlight considerations around ultimate limit state and fatigue life of the structure.

6.5.2 Optimization
Pile design optimization capabilities are embedded within the “User_Inputs” tab to minimize the
overall mass of the substructure design. Reducing mass is a big focus of the industry because it
reduces material costs and demands on heavy-lift installation vessels.
This feature takes advantage of Excel’s built-in Solver capabilities. Once the user pushes the button
to Update Pile to Minimize Mass, VBA code will be initiated calling Solver’s “GRG Nonlinear
Multistart” algorithm. The code can be viewed using the keyboard shortcut Alt+F11. The
optimization routine changes pile diameter and pile thickness subject to three constraints:
 • The ratio of diameter to thickness must fall within a given range for reasons of stability.
 • The structural natural frequency must also fall within a given range to avoid dynamic
 resonance.
 • Lastly, the structure must meet ultimate limit state requirements for all loading conditions.
The user can adjust the target ranges for the optimization constraints by altering values or formulas
in the Min and Max columns (Range P11:Q13) under Pile Optimization Routine. The routine will
overwrite D_P and t_Pile values in the “User_Inputs” tab, but it will paste a record of the changes
under the columns labeled OrigVal and Optimized.

 D-31

 Figure 24 Screenshot of optimization feature within the “User_Inputs” tab (2020-05-07 version)

6.6 Special Calculation Tabs
The spreadsheet model calculations are performed within the dedicated tabs, “Calc_Structural,”
“Calc_Wind,” “Calc_Wave,” “Calc_Fatigue,” and “Calc_Geo.” Each tab is formatted similarly.
The input cells that are used in that tab’s calculation are linked at the top of the page for reference,
and the calculated variables are recorded in grey cells. Descriptions of each variable and a note
about where the calculation came from are included for the user’s benefit.

6.6.1 Calc_Structural
The “Calc_Structural” tab is focused on calculating the natural frequency of the structure, which
is a critical aspect of any soft-stiff substructure design. The goal of the soft-stiff design is for the
natural frequency of the structure to fall between the 1P and 3P loading frequencies. In addition to
calculating the natural frequency of the structure, the 1P and 3P frequency band are calculated
based on the maximum and minimum rotation speed of the turbine. A diagram showing where the
natural frequency fits compared to the 1P, 3P, wind, and wave loadings is produced under the
“Outputs” tab and can be seen in the bottom right of Figure 23.
The natural frequency calculation comes out of the 10-Step paper from Arany (2017). The main
theory behind this calculation is that first the natural frequency is calculated assuming a fixed base
frequency (f_FB) for the tower. However, the true frequency of the structure is less than f_FB due
to soil-structure interactions and the substructure stiffness. These reductions in the natural
frequency are represented by the coefficients C_L, C_R, and C_S. These three coefficients are all
calculated using closed-form equations from Arany et al. (2017) [2].

 D-32

Editorial Note: The output of the spreadsheet currently represents the answer that the CEE 0293
class converged on for assignment 4. The answer for the natural frequency of the spreadsheet is
significantly lower than the result in the 10-step paper.

6.6.2 Calc_Wind
The calculations in the “Calc_Wind” tab follow the methodology set forth by Arany et al. (2017)
[2] and Bhattacharya (2019) [12]. The wind thrust force is calculated as the sum of the mean thrust
force and the turbulent thrust force.

 Equation 2

Where ρa is the density of air, AR is the swept area of the rotor, and CT is the OWT thrust
coefficient.
The thrust coefficient is calculated differently for wind speeds up to the rated wind speed than it
is for wind speeds beyond the rated wind speed.

 Figure 25: Thrust Coefficient Curve Approximation [2]

The turbulent component of thrust force is found using different methodologies for each wind
loading case:
 • [U-1] Normal Turbulence Model (NTM) at Rated Wind Speed
 • [U-2] Extreme Turbulence Model (ETM) at Rated Wind Speed
 • [U-3] Extreme Operating Gust (EOG) at Rated Wind Speed
 • [U-4] Extreme Operating Gust (EOG) at the Cut-Out Wind Speed
The turbulence models (case U-1 and case U-2) are based on wind speed standard deviation
calculations that rely on the site’s reference turbulence intensity at wind speeds of 15 m/s. The
EOG cases are based on the site’s 1-year extreme wind speed. Of the four wind loading cases, U-

 D-33

3 produces the highest loads. Case U-3 assumes that when the OWT is operating at the rated wind
speed, the blades have not been pitched and the turbine is subject to the full wind load. The reality
(as shown in Figure 25) is that pitching begins before the rated wind speed is reached. For case U-
4, the OWT blades have already started to pitch and shed load.

 Figure 26 Screenshot of “Calc_Wind” tab (2020-05-07 version)

6.6.3 Calc_Wave
The calculations in the “Calc_Wind” tab follow the methodology set forth by Arany et al. (2017)
[2] and Bhattacharya (2019) [12]. Linear (Airy) wave theory is applied for simplicity, and the
design wave load is taken as the sum of the max drag and inertia loads. In reality, this is a
conservative assumption because the drag and inertia loads do not peak simultaneously.
The wave loading cases use different combinations of statistical recurrence interval (1-year and
50-year) and wave characteristics (significant wave height and maximum wave height):
 • [W-1] 1-year Extreme Sea State (ESS)
 • [W-2] 1-year Extreme Wave Height (EWH)

 D-34

 • [W-3] 50-year Extreme Sea State (ESS)
 • [W-4] 50-year Extreme Wave Height (EWH)

The ESS wave cases rely on information about the significant wave height at different recurrence
intervals. The significant wave height is the average height of the highest one-third of waves in a
wave spectrum. The EWH wave cases rely on information about the absolute maximum wave
height observed at different recurrence intervals. Associated with each characteristic wave is a
corresponding wave period, which is the time it takes for one wave to pass by, measured from
trough to trough or crest to crest.

 Figure 27 Definition of wave terminology [12]

 D-35

 Figure 28 Screenshot of “Calc_Wave” tab (2020-05-07 version)

6.6.4 Calc_Fatigue
Any fatigue analysis is going to be based on the Palmgren-Miner’s rule seen in Equation 3 from
DNV GL c203. The accumulated fatigue damage D is seen as a ratio of the number of cycles at a
specific stress range nI over the number of stress cycles until failure at the specific stress range Ni.
For the denominator of this equation Ni is calculated using the S-N curves from DNV GL c203.
An image from DNV GL c203 that represents S-N curves for cathodic protection conditions can
be seen in Figure 29. These curves are represented by Equation 4 from DNV GL c203 [9].

 Equation 3: Palmgren-Miner Rule DNV GL c203

 D-36

 Figure 29: DNV GL c203 S-N curves for Cathodic Protection [9]

 Equation 4: Equation defining S-N curves

These values that define the S-N curves in Equation 4 are defined in tables in DNV GL c203. They
are stored in the “Fatigue Curves FC” and “Fatigue Curves CP” tabs for the free corrosion and
cathodic protection conditions respectively. These two tabs are where all the failure points along
the curve are calculated. For example, if the E1 loading stress is 40 MPa this tab is where 40 MPa
is plugged into Equation 4 to find the number of cycles to failure. This number of cycles represents
the denominator in Equation 3. An image of the “Fatigue Curves CP” tab can be seen in Figure
30. The values in this tab should not be edited unless the user wants to use their own S-N curves.

 D-37

 Figure 30 Variables defining S-N curves taken from DNV GL c203

Figure 31 shows the main fatigue sheet inputs and calculations. The most important thing to note
here is how the cyclic loading is estimated from the ULS. For ULS the only issue is the magnitude
of the stress. Fatigue loading is more complicated because it has to do with loading cycles not just
the peak magnitude. In theory a loading condition could have a high peak magnitude but not have
a significant cyclic component because the loading is constant.
To estimate the cyclic component two different methods have been taken. The 10-step paper
simply takes the max and minimum loading conditions from each of the 5 loading conditions and
defines the cyclic range as the difference. The problem with this is that this method combines wind
and wave loading. These two types of loading occur at very different frequencies. Wind loading
occurs at around 0.01 Hz and wave loading occurs around 0.1 Hz. As a result, when these loadings
are combined it is not clear what equivalent frequency should be used. This is important because
without a frequency for the actual loading it is impossible to estimate the numerator of Equation
3. This means that using the 10-step paper’s method it is only possible to find the denominator of
Equation 3 and an actual fatigue life is not estimated.
The second method was an attempt from the author to estimate a fatigue life. This method separates
the max and min wind loads and the max and min wave loads from the “operational case” E1.
They are split up because the frequency of the individual loading regimes can be estimated. These
cyclic loadings are called sigma_U1_cyclic and sigma_W-1_cyclic in Figure 31 from the
spreadsheet.

 D-38

 Figure 31 Important Loading Variables from Fatigue Tab

The main problem with the second method is that even though I can get an estimate for the number
of wind loads and the number of wave loads, the accumulated damage then has to be combined to
get a total fatigue life. There is an equation in the DNV GL c203 standard that is designed for this
purpose. The equation along with some figures from DNV GL c203 used to explain its purpose
can be seen in Equation 5. This equation is combines two accumulated damages at different
frequencies into one damage value.

 Equation 5: Load Combination from DNV GL c203

 D-39

 Finally, Table 4 and Table 5 show the tables that contain the fatigue results. These tables estimate
 a fatigue life using the second method described previously. The results from these tables are what
 is imported into the outputs tab.

 Editorial Note: The loadings for this are based on the E1 “operational case” from the 10-step
 paper. However, this loading condition is EXTREMELY conservative. For example, the cyclic
 wind case assumes the rated wind speed for the whole lifespan of the turbine. Additionally, the
 cyclic wave case assumes a significant wave height of 5.28 m for the 20-year lifespan of the
 structure. Both assumptions lead to very high cyclic loading conditions. Applying these loadings
 across the 20-year life of the monopile is very conservative. A better version of fatigue will try
 and use design load case 1.2 in the IEC 61400 design document.

 when exposed to various loadings brought upon by the various load cases (U-1, U-2, etc.). This
 tab takes in account the various structural characteristics of the OWT, the forces and moments

 D-40

originating from the load cases, and the foundational stiffness parameters that originate from the
“Calc_Structural” tab—these can be seen in Table 6:

 Inputs 50-year EWH M_wave_M_50
 Inputs Normal operational conditions M_E1
 Inputs Extreme wave load M_E2
 Inputs Extreme wind load M_E3
 Inputs Extreme gust at U_out and 50-year EWH M_E4
 Inputs Wind-wave misalignment M_E5
 Calcs Stiffness parameter - foundation lateral K_L
 Calcs Stiffness parameter - foundation cross-coupling K_LR
 Calcs Stiffness parameter - foundation rotational K_R
 Calcs Material modulus - Pile E_P
 Calcs Bending stiffness EI_P
 Calcs Fixity parameter lambda_f
 Calcs Soil subgrade reaction (for sand and NC clay) n_h_kN
 Calcs Ratio bending stiffness to subgrade reaction T

The main computation that takes place within this tab follows the work that is delineated in
Matlock and Reese (1967) [13]. We recall that we are dealing with a ground profile that has a
linearly increasing modulus, and wish to proceed with analyzing the behavior of the monopile
when subject to the lateral loading that is inherent of the various load cases. This ground profile
allows for the utilization of the non-dimensional solutions for vertical piles subjected to lateral
loads and we see the following relationships embedded within the tab deal with pile deflection,
slope, moment, shear, and inherent soil reaction. This can be seen in Figure 32. These formulations
originate from analyzing a beam on an elastic foundation.

 D-41

 Figure 32 Non-Dimensional Solutions for Vertical Piles subject to lateral Loading

Given these formulations provided by Matlock and Reese, the “Calc_Geo” tab applies these for
each load cases that originate from the “Calc_Wind” and “Calc_Wave” tabs. These loads cases
provide estimates for the resultant horizontal loads and moments at that the foundation would be
exposed to. We see that the formulations for analyzing the pile subject to lateral loading depend
on: Pile Bending stiffness (EI_p), Soil coefficient (A), Relative stiffness (T), Horizontal load (P),
Moment (M), and Soil-subgrade (eta_h).
Each load case is analyzed for a certain fixed condition: Completely Fixed, Completely Free, and
Partially Fixed. These conditions are delineated in the structural analysis phase of the process—
meaning that the monopile will be evaluated under a certain fixity. For this version of the
spreadsheet, we are assuming a fixed condition at the mudline—allowing for the idealization of
the monopile of the OWT that is seen in Figure 33.

 Figure 33 Pile Idealization with description of super position for deflection as a function of (P,M)

 D-42

This analysis is founded in the concepts brought forth by the p-y solutions for laterally loaded
piles. These concepts aim to describe the modulus of subgrade reaction in the case where a soil
medium is represented by various closely-spaced, independent, elastic springs. The various types
of springs aim to capture the deformation characteristics of the soil—providing a deflection (y) to
a force (p). This phenomenon can be seen in Figure 34, and provides a sufficient basis to proceed
with our analysis.

 Figure 34 Winkler Concept for lateral loading on pile system

The outputs generated from these analyses for our monopile subjected to lateral loadings are
distributions of deflections and moments for each fixity case. We are able to analyze the moments
and deflections along the length of the pile. This is essential because we want to be able to
determine whether or not we are going to exceed the limits delineated within ULS, FLS, and ULS
criteria.
From the framework delineated in the “Calc_Geo” tab, we are then able to see the distributions of
moments and deflections along the length of our monopile of interest. We evaluate that the
distributions of deflection and moments resemble those that are expected for the generic p-y case
in Figure 35.

 D-43

 Figure 35 p-y concept for laterally loaded pile for soil with linearly increasing soil modulus

7 APPENDIX D-2 Calculation of FLS Stress Demands from Markov
  Wind Matrix and Sea State Table
7.1 Introduction and Scope
This Memo describes a method for early-phase estimation of combined fatigue loads from wind
and wave acting on the foundation of a wind turbine generator.
The method aims at a slightly conservative approach to be used before time series analyses
establish the detailed combined loads.
The calculation has four steps:
 1. Chose an elevation to establish loads for
 2. Establish turbine fatigue loads at this elevation
 3. Establish wave fatigue loads at this elevation
 4. Combine turbine and wave fatigue loads
The method uses the following data as input:
Turbine loads: Overturning moment Markov matrices at the interface to the tower and
wind rose data
Wave loads: Hs-Tp scatter diagram and wave rose data

7.2 Choose Elevation
The elevation at which we wish to establish fatigue loads is picked. An example where this
section is chosen at a distance z1 below the interface to the tower is shown in Figure 36.

 Figure 36 Example elevation of interest

 D-45

7.3 Wave Fatigue Loads
The wave fatigue calculation uses the scatter diagram for significant wave height versus spectral
peak period as input. The scatter diagram gives the probability distribution of combinations of
significant wave height and spectral peak period. This is given in the form of number of sea-
states – typically each with duration 3 hours- over a set duration.
The basic layout of scatter diagram is shown below.

 Figure 37. Hs-Tp scatter diagram layout

The method for calculating wave fatigue input is as follows:
 1. Calculate number of waves in each sea state based on the scatter diagram
 2. Calculate the distribution of individual wave heights for each entry in the scatter diagram
 3. Calculate distribution of wave responses for each entry in the scatter diagram and roup
 responses into bins with corresponding load intensity
 4. Adjust the results for wave directionality
Each of these steps are described in further detail in the sections below.

7.3.1 Number of waves
We first calculate the duration spent in each condition in the scatter diagram. The scatter
diagram can be based on a different total duration than the lifetime of the structure to be
investigated. We therefore scale the results with the life time of our structure as shown in the
example below.

In our example we have:
  Lifetime of structure 25 years
  Total number of sea states in scatter diagram 100,000
  Number of sea states with spectral peak period 8-9 seconds and at the same time Hs 2.0
       to 2.5 m: 2,300

 D-46

 Figure 38. Scatter diagram entry example
 ,
Based on this we calculate the duration in these conditions as ∗ 25 ∗ 365 ∗ 24 ∗ 3600 =
 ,
1.81 × 10 seconds.
The number of individual waves within these conditions are then calculated based on the zero
up-crossing period taken as 0.71 times the spectral peak period.
 . ×
For the example we get = 3.00 × 10 waves.
 .

This is done for all entries in the scatter diagram.

7.3.2 Distribution of Individual Waves
The distribution of individual wave heights is calculated for each entry in the scatter diagram.
The individual wave heights in a sea state with given significant wave height, Hs are assumed to
follow a Rayleigh distribution.

7.3.3 Distribution of Wave Responses
Wave and current loads give little mean static load, and all wave load cycles are therefore
assumed to have zero net static load.
The wave loads can be calculated by dividing the exposed part above the elevation of interest in
a number of sections, then calculate the load on each section and finally sum the effect of all
sections.

 Figure 39. Sections

 D-48

The wave loads consist of drag and inertia loads that can be calculated using Morison’s equation:

                             =       | |    ∆                  Equation 7

 = ∆ Equation 8

Where
Fdi = Drag force on section number i
Fmi = Inertia force on section number i
Cd = Drag coefficient, typical value 0.8 to 1.05
Cm = Inertia coefficient, typical value 2.0 if the wave length large compared to the diameter of
the section
= Water density
u= Horizontal water particle velocity from wave and current
a= Horizontal water particle acceleration
Di = Diameter of section number i
z = Height of section
Wave particle velocity and acceleration can be calculated using linear (Airy) theory or
alternatively nonlinear theory such as stream function theory or Stoke’s 5th order theory.
Drag and inertia forces are 90 degrees out of phase. Hence it is very conservative to simply add
the terms. A better approach is to add the root of the squares summed.

 = + Equation 9

Using inertia factor 2.0 exaggerates the mass forces for short waves. A better approach if the
diameter of the sections is not small compared to the wavelength is to use Mc Camy Fuchs
Theory to calculate the inertia force.
The overturning moment at the elevation of interest is then calculates as the sum of the loads on
all the sections.
 =∑ Equation 10

Where
Mwqs = Quasi static wave load overturning moment
Fi = Force on section number i
ai = arm of section number i above the elevation to establish loads for
Large turbines typically have a first natural frequency of about 0.2 Hz (period 5 seconds). This is
in a range where there is some wave excitation, and a dynamic amplification of wave loads is
seen.
A simplified approach is used to account for this by adding a dynamic amplification factor. Only
the first natural frequency is considered as the higher natural frequencies are typically above the

 D-49

region of significant wave action. The dynamic amplification is calculated using a modified
formula for a single degree of freedom system. This simplified calculation gives very high
dynamic amplification factor near the natural frequency. In actual sea states the amplification
would be broken up by loads at other frequencies. Furthermore, the waves attack a part of the
structure where the deflection in the first eigenmode is modest. An assumed cap of 3.0 is
therefore put on the calculated dynamic amplification factor.
                         =       (1) ∙    ∙                       Equation 11

 1
 = ≤ 3.0

 1− + 2

Where
Mwd = Dynamic response to wave of height H
Mwqs(1) = Quasi static moment response to unit wave height
H = Wave height
DAF = Dynamic amplification factor
 = Circular frequency
e = Circular frequency corresponding to first natural frequency
= Damping ratio taken as 2%
The wave overturning response to a unit wave height with different wave periods can be
established by performing this calculation with different waves as input. Care should be
exercised if non-linear theory is used as these do not converge for waves that are too steep.
Furthermore, using a steep wave as input with non-linear wave theory would over-estimate the
loads for fatigue purposes.
In our example, we calculate the range in overturning moment for a wave with unit wave height
and period 8.5 seconds (note that this is the range of load as the complete wave passes, not the
load amplitude). Let us assume that we find that this overturning moment is 5 MNm.
We then calculate the dynamic amplification factor also based on period 8.5 seconds and the first
natural frequency of the complete system – let us say 0.2 Hz - and find DAF = 1.5.
The distribution of load cycles for this entry in the scatter diagram is then found by multiplying
the ranges in wave height in Table 8 with the response for unit wave height and the DAF. That is
multiplying with 5*1.5 = 7.5 in our example. Doing this we get:

The total number of wave responses from all entries in the scatter diagram is found by repeating
the steps above for each entry.
Grouping the responses into bins with corresponding load intensity gives the omnidirectional
wave fatigue loads. This is the fatigue loads if all waves had the same heading direction.

7.3.4 Adjustment for Wave Directionality
Assuming that all waves act in the same direction is conservative. A more accurate result is
obtained by adjusting the results for directionality based on the all-year wave rose.

 Figure 40. All Year Wave Rose Example

The concentric circles give percentages, and the sectors show how many percent of the total time
with waves in the different directions.
The effect of different wave headings is calculated assuming a linear relationship between loads
and stress. The stress level caused by load acting at an angle  goes with the cosine of the angle.
Thus, equivalent moments can be calculated as
                            =         ( −     )                Equation 12

Where
M = Moment from wave with heading angle 
 = Fatigue load direction to be investigated
Me = Equivalent moment for investigating fatigue from the load at angle 
The effect of wave directionality is estimated by grouping the omnidirectional number of wave
cycles into percentages according to the wave rose and then multiplying the load intensity with

 D-52

the cosine of the angle between the wave propagation direction and the fatigue load direction to
be investigated.

Wave fatigue loads in different directions is then found by grouping the results for different
values of 0 into suitable bins of load intensity with corresponding number of cycles.

7.4 Turbine Fatigue Loads
Turbine loads at the interface to the tower is used as basis for calculating turbine fatigue loads at elevations
further down.

 Figure 41. Turbine loads

The moment at the elevation of interest is calculates as:
 = + Equation 13

Where
M = Moment at connection between the steel and concrete parts
MT = Moment at tower interface
z1 = Vertical distance from connection between the steel and concrete parts to tower
interface
QT = Shear force at tower interface
It is noted that the load bins in the shear load Markov matrix may typically not be easily matched
to specific bins in the bending moment Markov matrix. An equivalent quasi static shear force is
therefore used to pair each moment load range with an appropriate shear force:

 = Equation 14

Where
z2 = Vertical distance from tower interface to hub height

 D-53

The Markov matrices give the number of load cycles gathered in bins depending on load range
and mean static load. The Markov matrices are typically generic cases only including turbine
loads established by the turbine suppliers. The basic layout of the matrices is shown below.

 Figure 42. Markov matrix basic layout.

All entries in one row of the matrix have the same mean static load. Similarly, all entries in one
column of the matrix have the same load range intensity.

7.4.1 Fatigue of Steel Structures
The mean static load is usually disregarded for fatigue checks of steel structures. Hence, for steel
the matrix can be condensed into a vector by summing all entries in each column.

 Figure 43. Markov matrix simplification for steel fatigue checks

7.4.2 Fatigue of Concrete Structures
The mean static load is of importance for fatigue checks of concrete structures, but it is still
desirable to simplify the matrix to facilitate the combination of turbine and wind fatigue loads.
This is done in two steps

 D-54

 1. Summing entries with positive mean load into one vector and entries with negative mean
 load into another vector
 2. Pairing each entry in the two vectors with the weighted average mean static load. The
 weighted average is calculated based on the distribution of load cycles with different
 static loads.
 = ∑ Equation 15

Where
Mi = Mean static load for row i
ni = Number of cycles in row i column j
n = Total number of positive or negative cycles in column j
Note that this gives a different mean load for each entry in the vectors as the distribution of load
cycles for each entry varies.

 Figure 44. Markov matrix simplification for concrete fatigue checks step 1

 D-55

 Figure 45. Markov matrix simplification for concrete fatigue checks step 2

Care should be exercised to make sure the following with respect to the Markov matrices:
  Are the matrices based on the same lifetime as our structure? If not, then all number of
 cycles should be scaled to represent the correct lifetime
  Are the matrices uni-directional – that is in a coordinate system following the turbine as it
 yaws or in a coordinate system fixed relative to direction north? An adjustment for
 directionality based on the wind rose is made if the coordinate system follows the turbine.
 The method is like that described for waves in 7.3.4. If the coordinate system is fixed to
 direction north, care should be taken to establish it is based on the wind data for the
 correct location.

7.5 Combination of Turbine and Wave Fatigue Loads
Performing the steps in chapters 7.3 and 7.4 establishes wave and turbine loads in different
directions. The final step is to combine these loads. An innovative method for doing this is
presented in this chapter.
The total number of turbine load cycles is typically significantly larger than the total number of
wave load cycles. The situation is illustrated in Figure 46.

 D-56

 Figure 46. Combined Load From Turbine and Waves

An example where there is a ratio about 5 times more turbine load cycles than wave load cycles
is used as the basis for explaining the method. The procedure would be the same also with other
ratios.
The combination process is done in two steps:
 Step 1 combination: Each wave load cycle is combined with a turbine load cycle
 Step 2 combination: Each of the remaining turbine load cycles is combined with part of
 a wave load cycle
Taking the wave loads as basis, the most conservative way to combine it with turbine loads is
assume that both will always have extreme values matching the other. That is to maximize the
ranges between red markers in Figure 47.

 Figure 47. Global Maxima and Minima

There are two assumptions here that contribute to conservatism:
  Always assuming the largest loads from the one combine with the largest loads from the
 other
  Always assuming that these loads are in phase.

 D-57

Looking at Figure 47 we see that – even with these conservative assumptions regarding the
phasing of loads – only some of the turbine load maxima coincide with a wave load maxima. For
our example with 5 times as many turbine load cycles as wave load cycles we find that every
fifth turbine load maximum may coincide with a wave load maximum. Likewise, only every fifth
turbine load minimum may coincide with a wave load minimum. The remaining 4/5 of turbine
load maxima and minima necessarily occur when waves are in some interim state between
maximum and minimum.
The first step in combining wave and turbine loads is therefore to match every 5th WTG cycle
with a corresponding wave cycle. This is done starting with WTG cycles with the highest loads
and matching 1/5 of them with the highest wave loads. This is repeated until all wave cycles
have been combined with turbine cycles.
This approach accounts for the global slower wave load variation together with turbine loads, but
it does not account for the local maxima and minima in between (blue markers in Figure 48).

 Figure 48. Local Maxima and Minima

In our example there are on average four of these local load ranges for each wave load range and
each range spans one fifth of the wave period. The load variation in each of these local load
ranges is the turbine load range plus part of the wave load range.
The larger the ratio of cycles, the smaller part of the wave period is covered by each turbine load
cycle and the smaller part of the wave load range within each turbine cycle. Values for different
ratios are given in the table below.
 Table 11. Part of wave load range

 Ratio of total turbine  Factor for part
 load cycles to total wave  of wave load
 load cycles range
 2 0.71
 3 0.50
 4 0.38
 5 0.31
 6 0.26
 7 0.22
 8 0.20
 9 0.17

 D-58

 load cycles to total wave  of wave load

 10 0.16

7.5.1 Fatigue of Steel Parts
For fatigue of steel parts we have one vector giving the turbine fatigue loads, ref section 7.4.1
and one vector giving the wave fatigue loads, ref section 7.3. We call these vectors for wave
loads and for turbine loads.

Step 1 combination:
 1. Calculate the ratio between turbine load cycles and wave load cycles. In our example this
 ratio is 5
 2. Divide the turbine load cycles in vector by the ratio from step 1. We call the result
 vector . We now have two vectors . and with the same total number of
 load cycles in them. These two are then combined according to steps 3 to 6 below
 3. Start with the largest wave load cycle and combine it with the largest turbine load cycle
 4. Combine the next largest wave load cycle with next largest turbine load cycle
 5. Combine the 3rd largest wave load cycle with the 3rd largest turbine load cycle
 6. Continue until all load cycles are exhausted
Step 2 Combination:
 1. Calculate the portion of turbine load cycles not already combined with wave load cycles.
 In our example, this ratio is 4/5 (80%)
 2. Multiply the turbine load cycles in vector by the ratio from step 1. We call the result
 vector
 3. Multiply the wave load cycles in vector with the numerator of the ratio in step 1 and
 the load intensities with a factor according to Table 11. In our example the numerator is 4
 and the factor is 0.31. We call the result vector
 4. We now have two vectors . and with the same total number of load cycles
 in them. These two are then combined according to steps 5 to 8 below
 5. Start with the largest turbine load cycle and combine it with the largest wave load cycle
 6. Combine the next largest turbine load cycle with next largest wave load cycle
 7. Combine the 3rd largest wave turbine cycle with the 3rd largest wave load cycle
 8. Continue until all load cycles are exhausted

7.5.2 Fatigue of Concrete Parts
For fatigue of concrete parts we have two vectors giving the turbine fatigue loads, ref section
7.4.2 and one vector giving the wave fatigue loads, ref section 7.3. We call these vectors for
wave loads; and for turbine loads.

 D-59

  contains load cycles with positive mean static turbine load
  contains load cycles with negative mean static turbine load

Combination of loads is in this case done as described for steel parts above, but the number of
wave load cycles is also split in two vectors. One to combine with and the other to
combine with . This is done as follows

 1. Calculate the ratio of total cycles in to the combined number of cycles in
 and
 2. Multiply the number of load cycles in with the ratio from step 1. We call the result

 3. Combine vectors and according to steps 1 and 2 in section 7.5.1.
 4. Calculate the ratio of total cycles in to the combined number of cycles in
 and
 5. Multiply the number of load cycles in with the ratio from step 4. We call the result

 6. Combine and according to steps 1 and 2 in section 7.5.1.

 D-60

8 Appendix D-3: An Example Fatigue Assessment of Monopile
  Support Structures of Offshore Wind Turbines Using Time Series
  Analyses
8.1 Introduction
This Appendix provides an example for fatigue damage assessment of monopile support
structures for offshore wind turbines. A potential wind site close to a National Oceanic and
Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) buoy in the Northeastern US waters was considered for
this example. The main metocean parameters, including wind speed, wind direction, significant
wave height, and wave peak spectral period were estimated using near to 40 years of hourly data
recorded by the NOAA buoy. The 5 MW reference wind turbine of the National Renewable
Energy Laboratory (NREL) was used for this case study. The support structure was considered to
be the same monopile which was introduced in the Offshore Code Comparison Collaboration
(OC3) project. Effects of various design conditions and assumptions, such as uni-directional and
multi-directional wind and wave, residual and mean stresses, tensile and compressive stresses in
critical spots, fatigue in base material and weldments, corrosive environment, and corrosion
protection on the fatigue life of monopiles are investigated in this example.

8.2 Reference wind turbine
A schematic representation of common OWT support structures is presented in Figure 49 [17]. A
monopile support structure was selected in this study for the following two reasons: (i)
approximately 80% of OWTs have been installed on the monopile support structures; and (ii) the
process of estimating fatigue life is relatively simple for the monopiles due to their axisymmetric
properties.

 Figure 49 Offshore wind turbine support structures introduced in Offshore Code Comparison
 Collaboration projects [17]

 D-61

The main properties of the NREL 5-MW wind turbine on the OC3 monopile are summarized in
Table 12 [18]. The hub height for this OWT is 90 meters above the MSL, and the RNA (Rotor-
Nacelle Assembly) is supported by a 77.6 m linearly tapered tubular steel tower. The monopile
has been designed as a tubular steel cylinder with 30 m length with constant section properties.
Twenty meters of the monopile is embedded in the water from a presumed rigid foundation at the
seabed up to the sea surface, and the remaining ten meters covers the distance between MSL and
the transition piece (TP). Although the real water depth at the SE Nantucket site is 74.7 m, the
design water depth in this study was assumed to be 20 m to match with the dimension of the
OC3 monopile.
 Table 12 Main characteristics of the NREL 5 MW WT and OC3 monopile support structure

 Item Value
 Wind regime IEC Class 1B
 Rotor diameter (m) 126
 Hub diameter (m) 3
 Hub height (m) 90
 Cut-in wind speed (m/s) 3
 Rated wind speed (m/s) 11.4
 Cut-out wind speed (m/s) 25
 Rated rotor speed (rpm) 12.1
                    RNA mass (Kg)                          350,000
                    Tower mass (Kg)                        347,500
 Transition piece height (m) 10
 Monopile embedment depth (m) 30
 Monopile diameter (m) 6
 Monopile thickness (mm) 60
8.3 Metocean Parameter Estimation

8.3.1 Case Study Wind Site
A potential wind site in the Northeastern U.S. waters, which is close to the National Oceanic and
Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) buoy 44008, was considered in this study which is shown
in Figure 50. The NOAA buoy provides reliable information about the environmental conditions
at this wind site. The motivations for selecting this wind site are: (i) it is close to the
Massachusetts and Rhode Island wind energy areas as shown in Figure 50; and (ii) it is one of
the 23 NOAA buoy stations which were investigated by NREL as potential offshore wind sites
for the U.S. waters; therefore, there exists some estimated metocean data for this site which was
used for verification of the estimated metocean parameters in this study.

 D-62

 SE Nantucket
 Buoy Station

 Figure 50 Massachusetts and Rhode Island wind energy areas and SE Nantucket station [19]

8.3.2 Wind Data
The metocean data from 1982 to 2018 (except year 2014) were recorded as 1-hour mean values
at the SE Nantucket wind site. A total of 272,534 data points was available for each metocean
parameter; however, after cleaning and processing of the data, 237,148 data points of wind speed
and wind direction were used in this study. This process was focused on removing all the data
points which did not include one of the wind or wave parameters to be able to estimate the

8.3.3 Wave Parameters
According to the IEC 61400-3 standard [1], significant wave height, Hs, and wave peak spectral
period, Tp, are the two main wave parameters which need to be estimated in order to perform a
fatigue limit state design of OWT support structures. These two parameters are included in the
measured data set at SE Nantucket station. In this study, the significant wave height and wave
 D-65

  peak spectral period were directly estimated from the SE Nantucket data set as a function of
  mean wind speed. In Figure 54, the estimated wave parameters are presented for different wind
  speeds and compared to the proposed quantities by the NREL study for the US East Coast.

  The Pierson-Moskowitz spectrum was employed to generate the irregular wave kinematics based
  on the IEC standard recommendations for fatigue analysis of offshore wind turbines. The
  second-order wave spreading effect was included in the simulations while the breaking waves
  effect was not considered in this study. Different random representations were used to generate
  the irregular wave signals corresponding to the different mean wind speeds.

  8.3.4 Further Assumptions
  Certain environmental conditions and metocean parameters cannot be estimated due to the lack
  of data for the case study site. For instance, sub-surface current, marine growth, and sea icing
  effects were not considered in this example. Soil-structure interaction was also not investigated
  due to a lack of data; however, its impact on the fatigue life of offshore support structures could
  be significant [20].

  8.4 Time Domain Simulations

  8.4.1 Generating Wind Field
  In order to design the OWT support structures for fatigue limit state in the normal operational
  condition, a full range of inflow wind speed (i.e., mean wind speed between cut-in and cut-out
  wind speeds in which the wind turbine generates energy) must be considered. The IEC Standard
  61400-1 recommends the minimum intervals of 2 m/s to cover this wind speed range [4].

 D-66

Therefore, 11 wind fields with mean wind speed of 4, 6, 8, …, 22, 24 m/s were generated for
each fatigue analysis.
The TurbSim program was used to generate full three-dimensional turbulent wind fields using
turbulence spectral parameters estimated based on Kaimal turbulence model [21, 22]. Similar to
the approach used to generate the irregular wave signals, 11 different random representations
were used to simulate the turbulent wind fields. Time-histories of the generated turbulent wind at
hub height for varying average wind speeds between cut-in and cut-out wind speeds, and the
corresponding time-histories of the wave elevations, are presented in Figure 55 and Figure 56
respectively.

 (a)

 (b)

Figure 55 Initial 5 minutes of generated turbulent wind at hub height by TurbSim: (a) average wind speed
 between cut-in and rated wind speed, (b) average wind speed between rated and cut-out wind speed

 D-67

 Figure 56 Initial 3 minutes of generated wave time-histories for different wind speeds

8.4.2 Coupled Aero-Hydro-Servo-Elastic Analyses
Numerical simulation of offshore wind turbines requires a multi-physics modeling approach due
the various loading scenarios which are acting on these structures. Fully coupled aero-hydro-
elastic time domain simulations were conducted in this study using the OpenFAST platform [6].
OpenFAST is a modularized multi-physics modeling tool which has developed by NREL to
perform time domain analyses of the horizontal axis and vertical axis, on-land and offshore, wind
turbines.
At least six 10-minute or one 1-hour time-history simulations are required for each wind speed
bin according to the IEC standard. For this study, the length of all time-domain simulations was
3630 seconds, where the first 30 sec of each simulation was discarded to alleviate the potential
impact of the transient state of wind turbine operation on the results of the fatigue analyses.
Figure 57 and Figure 58 present the time-histories of rotor speed and blade pitch, respectively,
for varying average wind speeds between cut-in and cut-out wind speeds. The influence of the
wind turbine pitch control system can be observed in these figures as the rotor speed remains
around the rated speed (12.1 rpm) while the inflow wind speed exceeds the rated wind speed
which is 11.4 m/s.

 D-68

 (a)

 (b)

 average wind speed between rated and cut-out wind speed

 D-69
