 Figure 58 Time-history of blade pitch: (a) average wind speed between cut-in and rated wind speed, (b)
 average wind speed between rated and cut-out wind speed

The time-histories of reaction forces and bending moments were obtained at mudline level of the
monopile support structure, which is considered to carry the maximum bending moments caused
by the wind and hydrodynamic loadings. The time-histories of the fore-aft and side-to-side
moments for varying inflow wind speeds are depicted in Figure 59 and Figure 60 respectively.

Eight locations around the perimeter of the monopile at the mudline level were considered as the
critical spots for the fatigue assessment. These critical spots were evenly distributed over the
perimeter of the monopile (i.e., at interval of 450), and were located along the main eight wind
directions to facilitate the study of the wind directionality effects on the fatigue life of monopiles.
The time-histories of normal stress at the critical locations were calculated based on the
estimated reaction forces and bending moments; then, these stress time-histories were used to
predict the fatigue damage of the monopile.

 D-72

8.4.3 Rainflow Cycle Counting
The Rainflow (RF) cycle counting method was adopted to estimate the total number of stress
cycles corresponding to stress range bins, and to create stress range distribution through rainflow
matrices [8]. A common Rainflow cycle counting algorithm is shown in Figure 61.

 Figure 61 A common Rainflow algorithm for cycle counting of time-history signals [3]

The 1-hour stress time-histories at critical locations were used to create the rainflow matrixes and
calculate the number of cycles of different stress cycle ranges for each wind speed bin at each
location. Considering the design life of 20 years for the OWT support structures, the total
number of stress cycles that a critical location of a support structure would experience during its
lifetime can be estimated by extrapolating the results from 1-hour simulations to the lifetime of
20 years. Figure 62 presents an example of rainflow matrix which has been extracted from a 1-
hour stress time-history at a critical spot of the monopile for the mean wind speed of 12 m/s (i.e.,
wind speed bin of 11 to 13 m/s).

 D-73

 (a)

 (b)

Figure 62 Rainflow matrix histogram for an hour stress time-history of a critical spot at mudline level of
monopile: (a) number of stress cycles for full stress ranges, (b) number of stress cycles for stress ranges
 up to 10 MPa

8.5 Fatigue Damage Assessment
According to the International Electrotechnical Commission standard IEC 61400-3 [1], the
directionality of the wind, as well as the directionality of the induced waves, have a significant
impact on the loads acting on OWT support structures. Therefore, applying a uni-directional or a
multi-directional assumption for the inflow winds and waves has an influence on the estimated
fatigue life of support structures. In normal operation design situation for OWTs, the uni-
directional assumption is recommended by the IEC standard (see Appendix D-4) for the ultimate
limit state design (i.e., DLCs 1.1, 1.3, 1.4, 1.5, 1.6a, and 1.6b) to account for the worst-case
scenario; however, the multi-directional wind and wave must be taken into account for the
fatigue limit state design (i.e., DLC 1.2).

8.5.1 Characteristic Fatigue Damage
The most common assumption for the fatigue analysis is that the fatigue damage accumulates
linearly (i.e., the damage from the nth cycle is not dependent on any of the previous cycles) [5].
This assumption is known as the Palmgren-Miner’s rule which is presented in Equation 18.

 D-74

 = ≤1 Equation 18

where DC is characteristic cumulative fatigue damage, k is number of stress blocks, ni is number
of stress cycles in the stress block I, and Ni is number of stress cycles to failure at constant stress
range of ∆σi. The S-N curves provided in the DNVGL-RP-C203 recommended practice were
used to obtain the number of stress cycles to failure and evaluate the fatigue damage [9]. In this
recommended practice, the design S-N curve is defined as below:
 log = log − log ∆ Equation 19

where is the predicted number of cycles to failure for stress range ∆ , ∆ is stress range with
unit MPa, is the negative inverse slope of S-N curve, and log is the intercept of log axis
by S-N curve which can be calculated as:
 log = log − 2 Equation 20

where log is the intercept of mean S-N curve with the log axis and represents the
standard deviation of log .
In order to develop the S-N curves for different structural specifications, the DNVGL-RP-C203
provides the required parameters in corresponding tables for different environmental conditions
including air, seawater with cathodic protection, and seawater with free corrosion condition. The
S-N curve parameters for seawater with cathodic protection and corrosive seawater condition are
listed in Table 13 and Table 14 respectively. The generated S-N curves for seawater with
cathodic protection are subsequently demonstrated in Figure 63.
  Table 13 S-N curves for different structural classifications recommended by the DNVGL-RP-C203 for
 seawater condition with cathodic protection [9]

Two proposed categories of the S-N curves in the DNVGL recommended practice were used to
estimate the fatigue damage: B1 category for the base material and D category for the
weldments. Since the critical spots of the monopile for fatigue damage are located at the mudline
level, both categories of the S-N curves were considered in seawater with cathodic protection as
well as free corrosion condition to monitor the corrosion fatigue effect [9].

 D-76

8.5.2 Effect of Mean Stress on Fatigue Damage
When the base material is not significantly impacted by residual stresses due to welding or cold
forming, the stress range obtained from the rainflow method can be reduced if a part of the stress
cycle is in compression according to [23]. The permission to reduce the stress range for
compression stress is due to fact that the compressive stress help close the existing cracks,
whereas the tensile stress facilitates the crack opening mechanism and therefore increases the
crack growth rate. The reduction factor can be calculated by Equation 21.
                                      + 0.6| |                      Equation 21
 =
                                        +| |
where σt is maximum tension stress where tension is defined as positive and σc is maximum
compression stress where compression is defined as negative. It should be noted that even during
compressive stress cycles if the stress exceeds the yield stress locally, the base material will
experience residual stresses, hence the stress reduction factor should not be applied in this
condition. Furthermore, stress reduction should not be used for cold formed tubulars since in the
cold forming process, the tubular sections experience yielding in the circumferential and the
longitudinal directions of the sections which results in significant residual stresses in both
directions in the base material. Cut-outs in the base material are the most common instances in
which the stress reduction factor can be applied.
Similarly, when the welded structural details have low residual stresses (e.g., due to post weld
heat treatment), the reduction factor calculated by Equation A.8 can be used for these weldments:
                                      + 0.8| |                     Equation 22
 =
                                        +| |
It should be noted that low residual stresses are not commonly documented in welded
connections, therefore, applying the stress reduction factors for the welded structural components
is not recommended in most practical situations; however, the stress reduction factor is applied
for welded components in this study to investigate its effect on the estimated fatigue damage of
monopile support structure. The stress reduction factors for non-welded and welded structural
components are schematically presented in Figure 64 and Figure 65 respectively.

 Figure 64 Stress reduction factor for non-welded structural components

 D-77

 Figure 65 Stress reduction factor for welded structural components

8.5.3 Plate Thickness Effect on Fatigue Damage
Lack of relevant fatigue test data for real structures results in need to various adjustments and
modification factors in application of existing S-N curves suggested by different standards and
recommended practices. Most of the fatigue test data are obtained from experiments conducted
on small-scale test specimen. There are certain shortcomings in application of these fatigue data
for large structural components used in the real-world complex structures which can be classified
in the following three categories:
 i. The small-scale test specimens do not contain the same level of residual stresses as large-
 scale structural components.
 ii. The fatigue test data are commonly obtained from experiments with different R-ratio
 values (i.e., the minimum peak stress divided by the maximum peak stress) comparing to
 what large specimens experience in real structures.
 iii. The small-scale test specimens are often fabricated more perfectly leading to less defects
 comparing to large-scale structural components.
According to the DNVGL-RP-C203 recommended practice, the effect of discrepancies between
the scale of test specimens and real structural components (i.e., size effect) depends on several
different parameters including:
 i. Thickness of plate: higher plate thickness at the fatigue crack initiation regions results in
 more severe notch and consequently higher stress ranges in the large-scale structural
 components.
 ii. Connection length: higher connection length causes more flow of stress into the
 connection which results in more severe notch stress.
 iii. Volume effect: higher structural volume can correspond with higher surface defects and
 other potential imperfections which can result in earlier fatigue crack initiation in large-
 scale specimens of real structures.
The thickness of plate effect is accounted for the welded structural components by a modification
of the stress range using the following equation:

 D-78

 log = log − log ∆ Equation 23

where is the thickness through which a crack will most likely grow, is reference thickness
which is equal to 16 mm for tubular joints and 25 mm for other types of welded connections, and
  represents the thickness exponent which is provided for different weld specifications in the
corresponding table of the recommended practice.

8.5.4 Design Fatigue Factor
The estimated characteristic fatigue damage is multiplied by design fatigue factor (DFF) to
represent the design fatigue damage:
 = × Equation 24

Different standards and guidelines recommend varying design fatigue factors which can be
considered as high as 10. The DFF varies based on: (i) location of critical spots on support
structure (i.e., whether fatigue damage is estimated for a critical spot in the atmospheric zone, or
in the splash zone, or in the submerged zone); and (ii) possibility and frequency of future
inspections. In general, the more difficult to access the critical spots and the less frequency of the
planned inspections of the critical spots, the higher the design fatigue factor should be. Since in
this study the critical spots were considered at the mudline level, the DFF was assumed to be 3.0
according to [24].

8.6 Summary and Discussion of Results
The estimated fatigue damages with and without considering the effects of multi-directional
wind and mean stress are presented in Figure 66 to Figure 69. The estimated fatigue damages are
presented for all eight potential critical locations over the perimeter of the monopile at the
mudline (e.g., N refers to the north side of the monopile at the mudline level), although for the
case of uni-directional wind the estimated damages in the dominant direction are of more
importance. The fatigue damages were estimated for both non-welded and welded components
considering both cathodic protection and free corrosion conditions. It is observed that the
assumption of uni-directional wind highly overestimated the fatigue damage on the southwest
and northeast sides of the monopile which is consistent with the dominant wind direction of the
site. On the other hand, accounting for multi-directional wind distribute the fatigue damage over
the perimeter of the monopile. Considering the effect of mean stress furthermore decrease the
estimated fatigue damages since it reduces the stress ranges on the tension sides of the monopile.
It should be noted that all the estimated fatigue damages are design fatigue damages based on
Equation 24 using a design fatigue factor of 3.0.

 DISCUSSION
 Number of cycles to failure
 Comparison with existing models-The failure number of
 cycles Nf, was obtained from each of the experiments and
 presented in raw form as a plot in Fig. 2, with the maximum
 stress level Smax, plotted against log10 Nf.
 In addition to this plot, it can be beneficial to compare
 the Nf results with existing fatigue-life predictive models, as
 shown in Fig. 8. While there are several fatigue-life predic-
 tive equations available in the literature, the experimental
 results were specifically compared against the model by
 Zhang et al.10,11 (Eq. (2)), which was used by the authors
 in the decision-making process while designing this test
 series. By comparing experimental results to the predictions
 (dotted lines in Fig. 8), it appears as if the predictive model is
 generally quite conservative in comparison to the results of
 experiments conducted as part of this test series. The results
 suggested that having a lower value of maximum stress level
 Smax, for one-cycle of fatigue loading (that is, at log10 Nf =
 0), in conjunction with a lower slope for the model, would
 result in better prediction of the test results. It was therefore
 investigated if there were any fatigue-life models that inher-
 ently incorporated these two modifications, by comparing
 the experimental results against other predictive models.
 The fatigue-life model provided by Model Code (2010)15
Fig. 3—Variation of peak and valley strains versus number incorporates these modifications – of a lower Smax at log10 Nf
of fatigue loading cycles; representative results from two = 0, and of a lower slope – and thus it was anticipated that this
tests shown in figure. model could better predict concrete fatigue life. Therefore,
 a comparison of the experimental results with this model

of R). However, comparison of the experimental results with have an influence on concrete fatigue life, Nf, is the loading
this model indicates increasing conservativeness at higher frequency. While some authors state that Nf increases with

small sample size of test results, though, it is recommended evident when there is at least a ten-fold change in frequency
that a more exhaustive verification be undertaken before magnitude.9 Some authors state that the effect is only visible
generalizing and using this last observation as a conclusion. in the low-cycle fatigue domain,12 whereas still other models
Until such a thorough comparison has been completed, it is mention that frequency effects are negligible if the frequency
 is between 1 and 15 Hz.14,25
 Three (Smax, R) combinations were each tested at two
 different frequencies; experimental results for these combi-
 nations are presented in Fig. 9. In each case, the average
 of the log of the failure number of cycles, [avg.{log10 Nf}],
 and therefore average Nf, was slightly higher for higher
 frequencies. However, this increase in average Nf for higher
 frequencies was relatively modest to be quantified, general-
 ized, or implemented in designs and analyses. Additionally,
 the increase in average Nf was well within the inherent vari-
 ability of the fatigue test results, and the sample size was too
 small to justify such a generalization. Therefore, it is deliber-
 ated that it may be a reasonable design and analysis practice
 to simply neglect frequency effects, if the frequencies are
 within 1-15 Hz, and if fatigue effects are being considered
 in the high-cycle fatigue domain only. If the frequency is
Fig. 5—Comparison of secant modulus of elasticity values, outside this range, or if low-cycle fatigue is being consid-
Esec, at start and end of testing, across all tests conducted ered, then additional attention may need to be given to
as part of Basic Fatigue Test Series Esecst and Esecend repre- loading frequency effects.
sent secant stiffness of first and last complete fatigue cycle,
respectively. (Note: 1 ksi = 6.89 MPa).

 It should be noted that in the plots of Fig. 11, all tests in the parameter, say, peak strain gradients, while the same general
basic fatigue test series have been included, without regard trend was observed, the equations themselves were not iden-
to any of the three primary parameters considered (namely, tical. Additionally, it was investigated whether this differ-
 ence in the equations was related to different values of mean
 cylinder compressive strength (which varied from roughly 6
 ksi (41 MPa) to around 13.5 ksi (93 MPa)), but a consistent
 variation versus concrete strength was not observed. There-
 fore, it is hypothesized that a definite linear trend between
 the log of gradients and log10 Nf exists in each fatigue study,
 but they are not identical across all fatigue studies.
 Furthermore, with regard to the regression equation,
 it should be noted that obtaining a particular value of the
 gradient (for either strains or stiffness) then enables estima-
 tion of the corresponding Nf. However, the converse of this
 is not true, as the same (or similar) gradient values could
 be obtained for different (Smax, R, f) combinations. In other
 words, the gradient should strictly be used as the indepen-
 dent variable, and the expected Nf as the dependent variable
 in the regression line equations, which is the reason that
 log10 Nf was plotted along the Y-axis in Fig. 11.
 While the merits of this relationship have previously been

Concrete compressive strain behavior and magnitudes under uniaxial
fatigue loading
Somashekar Viswanath a,⇑, James M. LaFave a, Daniel A. Kuchma b

h i g h l i g h t s g r a p h i c a l a b s t r a c t

 Understanding of concrete axial strain
  evolution and behavior are further
  improved.
 A method was developed to estimate
  peak strain at failure during fatigue
  analysis.
 Estimate of peak strain at the end of
  the stable strain accumulation phase
  is provided.
 Dependency of peak strain evolutions
  on peak stress levels are
  demonstrated.
 Dependency of peak strain evolution
  on failure number of cycles is
  demonstrated.

a r t i c l e i n f o a b s t r a c t

Article history: Recent advances in concrete technology and construction have promoted an increasing utilization of con-
Received 11 December 2020 crete in fatigue-critical structures, such as wind turbine towers. While research into concrete fatigue is
Received in revised form 24 April 2021 not a new topic and has been going on for several decades, a large majority of this research has primarily
Accepted 21 May 2021
 been focused on the determination of concrete fatigue capacity. However, understanding and advancing
Available online 4 June 2021
 concrete fatigue behavior (e.g., fatigue stress vs. strain response) has received limited attention, even
 though this is also crucial, especially considering the inherently nonlinear behavior of concrete. To
Keywords:
 address this knowledge gap and further advance our understanding of concrete fatigue behavior, an
Concrete
Fatigue
 experimental investigation consisting of uniaxial compressive fatigue testing of concrete cylinder speci-
Stress–strain response mens until failure was undertaken, and axial strains were recorded throughout the duration of fatigue
Peak strain at failure loading. The behavior and evolution of concrete axial strains, including the rate of accumulation of
Secant stiffness strains, through the fatigue loading process were extensively analyzed and are reported. Additionally,
Concrete fatigue performance the influence of key parameters, such as peak stress levels and number of cycles to failure, on peak strain
Concrete damage evolution were investigated and are described. Magnitudes of peak axial strain at the end of the stable
Wind turbine towers strain accumulation phase, as well as at failure, are also reported, and recommendations are made for
Wind turbine foundations
 estimating strain magnitudes at these critical junctures when conducting fatigue analysis and design.
 The results will help advance the understanding of concrete axial strain behavior under compressive fati-
 gue loading. The results also provide guidelines for estimating the magnitudes of concrete strains under
 fatigue loads, which in turn will be beneficial to designers, as it allows them to also consider structural
 performance during the analysis and design of fatigue critical concrete structures.

 However, to investigate this further, strain gradient evolution
 plots were also prepared focusing on just the end of first phase,
 entire middle phase, and beginning of last phase of strain evolution
 (~0.1  n  ~0.9), which are shown in the bottom row of Fig. 2.
 From these plots, it can be observed that the strain evolution rate
 continues to gradually decrease (from the very high value at the
 start of fatigue loading) through the fatigue loading process, until
 about 50% of the fatigue loading has been completed (n  0.5),
 beyond which the strain evolution rate begins to gradually
 increase over the course of the remainder of the middle phase,
 with a steep increase in gradients upon the start of the third phase.
 This observed behavior of strain gradients in the second phase,
 with a nonlinear rate of change in strains, does not align with
 the strain evolution theory documented in the literature (as sum-
 marized previously in section 3.1.1), where the second phase is
 said to be characterized by a ‘‘constant” strain evolution rate. How-
 ever, the magnitude of strain gradients in the middle phase is sub-
 stantially smaller than the peak magnitudes in the first and third
 phases, which may have been the cause of an assumption of con-

Appendix G – “High Cycle Fatigue
Behavior of Reinforced Concrete”
 fib Oslo Conference

 10 pages

 HIGH CYCLE FATIGUE BEHAVIOR OF REINFORCED CONCRETE
 Daniel A. Kuchma1, Mohsen Minaeijavid2, Somashekar Viswanath3, James M. LaFave4
 1. Professor, Dept. of Civil and Environmental Engineering, Tufts University, Medford, MA, USA
 2. PhD student, Dept. of Civil and Environmental Engineering, Tufts University, Medford, MA,
 USA
 3. PhD student, Dept. of Civil and Environmental Engineering, University of Illinois at Urbana-
 Champaign, Urbana, IL, USA
 4. Professor, Dept. of Civil and Environmental Engineering, University of Illinois at Urbana-
 Champaign, Urbana, IL, USA

Abstract
Towers and foundations that support wind turbines must be able to sustain high-cycle fatigue stress
demands that are caused by waves, winds, and turbine operations. These demands often have high mean
stress levels due to prestressing and/or mean wind speed, they can range into the millions of cycles of
occurrence, and they often control design. In current practice, the fatigue design life of these structures is
determined using “S-N curves” and Palmgren-Miner’s linear damage accumulation model. While this
may be suitable for most steel structures, it is not suitable for most concrete structures.
With funding from the U.S. Department of Energy, an experimental testing program is examining the
impact of steel reinforcement, tensile stress, strain variation, and the order of stressing on compressive
fatigue capacity. This is one of the first significant experimental testing programs to study the impact of
steel reinforcement on the fatigue performance of concrete structures. The results from completed tests
have identified shortcomings in existing fatigue models, and they are being used to suggest improved
means to characterize damage and estimate fatigue life. This research project is also examining fatigue
stress demands so to identify the primary sources of fatigue capacity utilization (demand/capacity for each
stress state) and determine how they can be mitigated by turbine control settings. This includes the
consideration of time dependent behavior including changes in the stiffness characteristics of structural
concrete.
This paper presents key observations and conclusions from the completed experiments, and a discussion
of challenges in both fatigue testing and the development of suitable models and design requirements.

INTRODUCTION
This project’s primary goal is to advance innovation in offshore wind support structures (i.e. towers and
foundations) made from concrete through an experimental study that quantifies the effect of fatigue on the
strength, stiffness, and durability of marine concretes, and then to use this data to advance models and
standards. Currently, the impact of fatigue on structural concrete in standards is typically treated the same
for all concretes regardless of the type of concrete material, and neglects the benefits of fibers, bar
reinforcements, and other effects. The large scatter in test data has led to models that are extremely
conservative for many designs, and this is a barrier to the competitiveness of concrete design solutions.
The new data, models, and standards that this project aims to help deliver should give designers and
developers the tools they need to drive innovation, reduce costs, and produce more resilient concrete
support structures for wind turbines.

 1

ASSESSING FATIGUE DESIGN LIFE FOR STEEL AND CONCRETE STRUCTURES
The fatigue capacity of concrete structures is characterized in a similar way as it is for steel structures
even though some of the assumptions inherent to this approach are not appropriate for concrete. This is
now discussed. Figure 1 shows the so-called S-N relationship for steel structures in which a stress range is
shown on the vertical axis, the number of cycles of fatigue capacity is shown on the horizontal axis, and
both scales are in log base 10. To assess the number of cycles of fatigue capacity for a particular stress
range (e.g. 100 MPa), one would go to the point on the vertical axis corresponding to this stress range,
and then move along a horizonal line to the right (e.g. blue line for 100 MPa range) until insectingfs the
“curve” corresponding to the particular base metal or weld type that is being evaluated. As an example,
for a D-Class weld, you would then move down the blue line until you reach the horizontal axis for which
you would find a fatigue capacity of about 550,000 cycles. If you only imposed this stress range for
100,000 cycles, then you would have used up 0.18 (100,000/550,000) or 18% of the available fatigue
capacity. To assess the total capacity utilization due to a series of different stress ranges, you would
simply add these up by Palmgren-Miner’s rule given in the equation shown in the Figure 1. An example
for this is presented in the table contained within Figure 1 for which the loading was 50 MPa for 3e+06
cycles, 100 MPa for 100,000 cycles, and 200 MPa for 20,000. Providing that the sum is less than 1.0 (or a
more conservative total as some cases require), then the design would be considered satisfactory.

Figure 1 Plain concrete fatigue test result; left: stress-strain curve of monotonic test; right: strain
evolution plot of concrete under fatigue loading [1]

 2

Figure 2 shows a typical fatigue model for
concrete in compression. This differs somewhat
from the S-N curve for steel in that the vertical
axis value is the fraction that the maximum
compressive stress is of the monotonic
compressive strength (Smax), and the particular
curve to use is defined by the fraction that the
minimum compressive stress is of the
monotonic strength (Smin). Palmgren-Miners’
linear damage accumulation model is used to
assess the impact of multiple stress ranges.
 Figure 2 Concrete Compressive Fatigue Model in fib 2010 Model Code [1]

There are several shortcomings of this approach, and these will be described after the following
description of the typical compressive fatigue behavior of a plain concrete cylinder.

COMPRESSIVE FATIGUE BEHAVIOR
Figure 3 presents the results of the typical compressive stress-strain response observed in a fatigue test.

Figure 3 Plain concrete fatigue test result; left: stress-strain curve of monotonic test; right: strain
evolution plot of concrete under fatigue loading [2, 3]

 3

The left side of Figure 3 presents the compressive stress-strain response of concrete in compression. The
solid black line (parabolic in shape) is the monotonic stress-strain response of the concrete. This figure is
rotated 90 degrees counterclockwise from how it is normally presented in the literature. In Figure 3, the
stress is on the horizontal axis in ksi and MPa, and the compressive strain is on the vertical axis. The
monotonic compressive strength is denoted as fcmono. Combinations of stress-strain values are shown for
15 cycles at different points of a test in which several thousand loading cycles were imposed. The red
circles identify the value of compressive straining at 80% of the monotonic compressive strength (Smax =
fcmax/fcmono = 0.80), the pink squares show the value of the compressive straining at 7.5% of the
monotonic strength (Smin = fcmin/fcmono = 0.075), the blue lines connecting these show the effective
stiffness between these points for selected cycles, and the few green lines present the shape of the
unloading branch during a cycle. As shown, the “peak strains” (those at Smax = 0.8) increase with cyclic
loading from 0.0013 to 0.0043, while the corresponding “valley strains” (those at Smin) increase from
0.0004 to 0.0023. This inelastic behavior is due to the formation of cracks that do not fully close with
unloading, and these cracks progress and new cracks form with an increasing number of loading cycles.
The plot in the right side of Figure 3 presents the evolution (cycle-dependent growth) of these “peak” and
“valley” strains (vertical axis) as a fraction of the number of loading cycles to failure (Ni/Nf). This strain
evolution is usually considered to be divided into three phases. In “Phase 1”, there is a substantial growth
in peak and valley strains within a modest fraction of Ni/Nf. In “Phase 2”, there is very close to a linear
increase in straining with Ni/Nf. In “Phase 3”, there is a much more rapid increase in straining,
particularly the peak strain, until failure occurs. The black dashed horizontal lines connect the level of
peak strain in this plot to the level of straining in the plot on the left side of the figure. The pictures show
where that surface damage started to occur and that extended relatively quickly through Phase 3.

FACTORS COMPLICATING THE COMPRESSIVE FATIGUE RESPONSE OF CONCRETE
While Figure 2 presented a reasonably simple model for concrete compressive fatigue capacity, and
Figure 3 presented the commonly observed type of compressive fatigue response in a test on an
unreinforced concrete cylinder, there are many factors that complicate the development and reliability of
this model and the response to fatigue loadings. Several of these are now described.
Effect of Loading Rate on Monotonic Strength: The monotonic strength of concrete is significantly
affected by loading rate (psi or MPa per second). A two order-of-magnitude difference in loading rate has
been observed to result in a 15% change in both capacity and stiffness, with the more quickly loaded
concrete being both stronger and stiffer. Most fatigue tests have been run at a two order-of-magnitude
difference or more than the specified rate of loading to be used in standardized compressive tests. Thus,
the loading ratios of Smax and Smin of fcmono that we use in fatigue testing likely overestimate what is
actually being applied by 10% or more.
Effect of Fatigue Waveform on Capacity: It has been observed that when the same loading frequency is
used, that the use of triangular wave form will result in a fatigue capacity that is twice as long as a
sinusoidal wave form, and twelve times greater than when a square wave form is used. This is likely
because capacity is reduced when there is more time for cracks to propagate under high stress. This may
also be the reason for why loading rate has a large effect on strength as expressed in the previous point.

 4

Scatter of Test Data and Impact of Variability in Monotonic Cylinder Strength: In general, there is a very
large scatter in fatigue capacity (number of cycles to failure for a particular stress range), and this will
commonly range by one to three orders of magnitude. An aspect of this variability is due to the variability
of the monotonic compressive strength of concrete test cylinder that were cast from the same concrete
batch, and which is commonly more than 5%. Thereby, when one is thinking that they are applying a
stress of 0.80 fcmono, they could actually be applying a stress of 0.76 of fcmono. Using the fatigue model
presented in Figure 2, this variation would suggest greater than a 5-fold influence on fatigue capacity.
When selecting a fatigue model that tries to lie beneath the lower values of the scatter, a much lower
capacity is suggested than would exist if the specified strength is provided in the real structure.
Impact of Aggregate Type and Degree of Saturation: The fatigue capacity has been observed to be less
when larger size aggregates are used, and this is considered to be due to the impact of larger stress
concentrations and planes of cracking that develop along the outside face of aggregates. It has also been
observed that fatigue capacity decreases with increasing degrees of saturation.
Impact of Order of Stressing in Variable-Amplitude Tests (VATs): This is discussed in the next section.
Impact of Longitudinal Reinforcement and Associated International Competition: This is discussed later.

EFFECT OF VARIABLE AMPLITUDE LOADING
With the use of Palmgren-Miner’s linear damage accumulation model, only the magnitude of the stresses
and number of cycles matter, and not the order of the stressing. This is unlikely to be true for concrete
because having higher stress levels early in the loading history would lead to a greater level of cracking
(damage) that make subsequent lower-level stresses more impactful than if otherwise. This was studied
using Variable Amplitude Tests (VATs) (i.e. when more than one combination of stress levels (Smax,
Smin) had been imposed). Figure 4 presents the results from a representative pair of 3-stage VATs. Figure
4(a) presents the results when stress range loadings were imposed in order of decreasing stresses (Smax =
0.80, 0.75, and 0.70), and Figure 4(b) presents the results when the order of increasing stresses (Smax =
0.70, 0.75, 0.80). In both cases, the lower end of the stress ranges was 0.075. The number of cycles at
each stress range are shown. Using the fib Model Code [1] and Palmgren-Miner’s rule, the fatigue
capacity ratio (test capacity/code estimate) when the higher Smax was imposed first was 2.07, and the
fatigue capacity ratio when the lower Smax was imposed first was 67.18. Thus, the impact of sequencing
was found to affect the fatigue capacity by a factor of 32.4 (67.18/2.07). A similar pattern was observed
throughout the VATs that were completed, and they confirm the hypothesis that the order of stressing
matters and that the Palmgren-Miner’s linear damage accumulation rule does not hold for concrete.

 (a) When higher stress are imposed first (b) When lower stresses are imposed first
 Figure 4 Strain evolution of plain concrete under variable amplitude cyclic test

 5

EFFECT OF LONGITUDINAL REINFORCEMENT ON FATIGUE CAPACITY
Figure 5 presents the monotonic and fatigue response of a concrete cylinder when Smax = 0.80, and Smin
= 0.075; this is a larger version of what was shown in Figure 3 and in the typical orientation. As
previously indicated, the peak and valley strains increase with the number of cycles of loading. Figure
5(a) also shows the stresses that would be expected in the longitudinal reinforcement at strains of 0.0010,
0.0015, and 0.0020 using an Es = 29000 ksi (200000 MPa).

 (a) (b)
Figure 5 a) Monotonic response of reinforced concrete (Stress-Strain curve) and loading-unloading lines
of fatigue test; b) reinforcement cage used for casting reinforced concrete cylindrical specimens (4”x8”)

The load share between the reinforcing steel and the concrete would be expected to vary because of
concrete softening and damage accumulation. Early in the fatigue loading history, the stress in the
concrete when the strain is equal to 0.0010 may be 3 ksi (21 MPa) as shown as point (1), whereas by
midway through the loading history, the strain in the concrete for this level strain would be about 0.6 ksi
(4 MPa) as shown as point (2). The effect of this on fatigue stress demands for a reinforced concrete
structure subjected to a constant variation in forces is a decrease in concrete stresses and increase in steel
stresses as the fatigue loading progressed. This effect is not considered in design practice or quantified in
fatigue models. A significant number of fatigue tests are being conducted on reinforced concrete cylinders
to investigate and quantify this effect. The results from tests to date have been very informative. To draw
attention to this matter, an international competition is being held to predict the fatigue behavior of
reinforced concrete cylinders.

INTERNATIONAL COMPETITION
An international competition is being held that will invite participants to predict the fatigue response
characteristics of plain and reinforced concrete cylinders with the full results being available to report at

 6

the fib 2022 Symposium in Oslo. The provided information to participant is the monotonic stress-strain
characteristics of the materials, the concrete composition, and results from Constant Amplitude Tests
(CATs) for Smax/Smin ratios of 0.80/0.05 and 0.70/0.05. Full strain evolution plots that are similar to
those presented in Figure 3 were provided. The testing, context questions, and timeline is now presented.
Type 1: Constant Amplitude Tests (CATs) on Reinforcement Concrete Cylinders that contain 4
longitudinal #3 bars (diameter = 3/8 inch or 9.5 mm) under loading that cause stresses of Smax/Smin of
0.80/0.05

Type 2: CATs on Saturated Plain Concrete at Smax/Smin of 0.80/0.05

Type 3: Variable Amplitude Tests (VATs) on Plain Concrete at a Smax/Smin levels of 0.80/0.05 and
0.70/0.05 with one sequence of decreasing Smax values (0.80 followed by 0.70) and the companion VAT
of increasing Smax value (0.70 followed by 0.80). For each case, two different stage 1 peak strain values
will be used, one at a lower peak strain (near the end of strain evolution phase 1 at ≈ 0.0018) and the other
at a higher peak strain (near the end of strain evolution phase 2 at ≈ 0.0027).
Because of the natural scatter that exists in fatigue testing, up to 10 tests of each type will be conducted
such so that typical response can be used for the evaluation of predictions.
Those participating in the competition are asked to predict the following:

  For CATs on Plain and Reinforced Concrete: number of cycles to failure (Nf), strain at failure
 (εf), the portion through the loading history for surface damage is noticeable (Ndn/Nf) or
 extensive (Nde/Nf).
  For CATs on Reinforced Concrete Only: Load share between concrete at steel at Smax and Smin
 loadings at the following cycles (10 cycles into testing; N/Nf = 0.10, 0.50, 0.9; last full cycle
 before failure).
  For the VATs on Plain Concrete: the (measured capacity ratio evaluated by Palmgren-Miner’s
 Rule using the fib Model Code 2010 fatigue model for low/high loading sequence) divided by
 (measured capacity ratio evaluated by Palmgren-Miner’s Rule using the fib Model Code 2010 [1]
 fatigue model for high/low loading sequence) as was done for results presented in Figure 4 for the
 case of transitioning at both the lower peak strain of 0.0018 and the higher peak strain of 0.0027.
The testing will be completed before the fib 2022 Symposium in Oslo.
CHARACTERISTICS OF FATIGUE TEST DATA
A part of this research project, an experimental data repository has been populated by adding fatigue
experimental data from more than 30 papers (for instance [3-9]), and it consists of more than 2400 data
points related to fatigue experiments on plain concrete. These data points consist of compression-only and
flexural test data, and the vast majority of the data points are from pure compression tests on plain
concrete. As a minimum, the data sets include the type of test, the strength of concrete, maximum stress
ratio (Smax), minimum stress ratio (Smin), number of cycles to failure (Nf), and specimen dimensions. For
most tests, the loading frequency and waveform was specified. Figure 6 shows the measured relationship
between Smax and Nf (measured number of cycles to failure) and in which the type of marker identifies

 7

the range within which was Smin. As shown, there is a very large scatter in the test results within each
range of a few orders of magnitude in many cases. It is also noteworthy that there are few tests in which
Smax was less than 0.60 or the number of cycles to failure seldom exceeded 10 million.

ASSESSMENT OF MODELS AND CODE REGULATIONS
Many different fatigue models for concrete have been published in standards, other guideline documents
for industry, and researchers (for instance [1, 10-11]). These models are often derived from different sets
of test results as well as views on the appropriate degrees of conservatism that should be employed.
Figure 7 presents Smax versus Log Nf of plain concrete via selected models as well as test data when Smin
= 0.05. As shown, there is an exceptionally large scatter in test results, as well as in the fatigue capacity
suggested by different models. It is important to remember that the x-axis is in log-base 10. The labels
starting with “Factor” presents the ratio of (near the largest number of measured failure load cycles for a
stress range) divided by (near the lowest number of fatigue load cycles for the same stress range). That
these range from 50 – 2000 times is very problematic for the development of a model or the selection of a
suitable model to use in a design standard. It is therefore not surprising that model predictions also vary
by a factor of 100 or so as also shown in Figure 7. It is useful to consider this spread in data and models in
comparison to steel structures, which usually vary a factor of 3-10.
There are several explanations for this scatter, including those already discussed:
  Effect of Loading Rate on Monotonic Strength
  Effect of Fatigue Waveform on Capacity
  Scatter of Test Data and Impact of Variability in Monotonic Cylinder Strength
  Impact of Aggregate Type and Degree of Saturation

 8

Figure 7 Test data & Different Fatigue model life estimation based on minimum stress ratio (Smin=0.05)

CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK
 1. When concrete is subjected to fatigue loading between two stress levels (Smax, and Smin), the
 strain at the peak stress level and the valley stress level will increase very significantly with the
 number of loading cycles. The magnitude of the strain at Smax increase more rapidly than the
 strains at Smin such that there is a reduction in stiffness of the concrete with the number of fatigue
 loading cycles.
 2. There is an exceptionally large scatter in the measured compressive fatigue capacity of concrete,
 and at up to three orders of magnitude this makes it very difficult to develop accurate models and
 set fatigue capacity limits in design standards.
 3. The measured scatter is due to many causes beyond just the inherent (or aleatory) uncertainty in
 the behavior of concrete in fatigue. These causes include variability in monotonic strengths of
 concrete specimens cast from the same batch, differences in loading frequency, differences in
 loading waveform, and differences in aggregate types.
 4. The order of stressing affects fatigue capacity, and Palmgren-Miners linear damage accumulation
 rule is not appropriate for the assessing the fatigue capacity of concrete.
 5. The presence of longitudinal reinforcing steel is expected to have a very significant effect on
 fatigue capacity because the reduction in concrete stiffness will shift the load from concrete to
 steel, and result in a decreasing stress demand on the concrete with the number of loading cycles.

 9

 6. While there are a considerably large number of available test results, few papers or reports
 present the full strain evolution and other key information. This hampers the ability to develop
 more accurate models and suitable fatigue standards.
 7. A large percentage of the fatigue test results are not shared because they are funded by industry to
 advance an individual company’s interest. To further the development of improved models and
 standards, the sharing of test data is needed. This may require public funding of fatigue testing.
 8. The development of a fatigue testing standard, that includes measurement and reporting
 requirements, would provide significant value to advancing fatigue models.
 9. There are many gaps in the types of tests that have been done including those with longitudinal
 reinforcement, confinement reinforcement, other sources of confinement, transverse tension,
 concrete types (e.g. fiber reinforced, high-weight, 3D-printed), tensile stress ranges, loading
 frequencies, and variable amplitude loadings.

15 includes the design, modeling, and testing of structural concrete and steel structures, and his

16 research emphasis is now on the structural design and performance of onshore and offshore

17 support structures for wind turbines.

19 He received his BS in Civil Engineering from Universidad Nacional de Colombia; his MS

20 and PhD in Civil Engineering from Purdue University. He is a member of ACI Committee

21 564, 3-D Printing with Cementitious Materials. His research interests include the

22 development of sustainable and low-carbon mixtures for 3D-printing, life-cycle assessment

23 of construction materials and durability of concrete structures.

25 University. He received his BS from Cracow University of Technology, Krakow, Poland; his

 1

 1 MS from The University of Texas at Austin, Austin, TX; and his PhD from Purdue

 2 University, all in civil engineering. He is Chair of ACI Committee 552, Cementitious

 3 Grouting, as well as several other ACI committees. His research interests include mixture

 4 proportioning, characterization and use of supplementary cementitious materials, durability

 6 Morten Søgaard Andersen is a structural engineer working in DNV Renewables

 8 and has been working with wind turbine structures most of his career. His research interests

 9 are concrete structures and especially the topics which governs the design for wind turbine

10 concrete structures.

11

12 ABSTRACT

13 3D-printed concrete has emerged as an important new type of concrete construction material

14 that is gaining use across many applications including as towers that support onshore wind

15 turbines, and potentially as foundations for supporting offshore wind turbines. Unlike most

16 civil infrastructure (e.g. buildings, bridges), the design of support structures for wind turbines

17 are frequently controlled by high-cycles fatigue demands from wind, wave, rotor rotation,

18 and aerodynamic imbalances. While significant research has been done on 3D printed

19 concrete materials, [1, 2], this study presented in this paper may be the first to examine the

20 high-cycle compression fatigue characteristics of 3D-printed concrete. In addition, a new

21 fatigue loading protocol is presented to reduce the apparent scatter in fatigue capacities.

22

23 Keywords: Cyclic loading; Fatigue; Number of cycles to failure; Additive manufacturing;

24 Strain behavior; Stiffness degradation; Secant modulus; 3D-printed concrete

 2

 1 INTRODUCTION

 2 The relentless pursuit of renewable energy sources has led to the rapid expansion of wind

 3 energy technologies, with wind turbines emerging as prominent fixtures in the global energy

 4 landscape. As wind turbine structures continue to evolve to meet the demands of increasing

 5 power generation capacities and environmental challenges, the reliability and performance of

 6 these structures become paramount. Concrete, once primarily associated with traditional

 7 construction, has gained significant traction as a material of choice for wind turbine support

 8 structures, offering economic advantages and environmental sustainability over conventional

 9 steel counterparts. Concrete wind towers are proving to be more cost-effective, particularly at

10 greater hub heights, which accommodate taller wind turbine towers [3, 4].

11 The design and operation of wind turbine structures are subject to various mechanical forces,

12 including cyclic loading induced by wind turbulence, which can lead to fatigue failure over

13 time. Understanding the fatigue behavior of concrete under such conditions is crucial for

14 ensuring the structural integrity and longevity of wind turbine systems. Despite considerable

15 advancements in concrete technology and structural design, gaps persist in our understanding

16 of concrete fatigue characteristics, particularly concerning the unique loading conditions

17 experienced by wind turbine structures. Offshore structures are subjected to high-cycle

18 fatigue loading due to wind, wave, and operational loads of turbines over their design life

19 (typically 25 years).

20 There is an emerging interest in 3D-printed concrete as an alternative to conventional cast

21 concrete for construction industry [5, 6]. Constant challenges related to time-dependent

22 characteristics of cementitious materials [7], durability [8], integration of reinforcement [9,

23 10] and structural compliance [11, 12], have been continuously explored at different size

24 scales to allow for the production of innovative and high-quality structures for specific

25 applications. This includes concrete towers, fixed bottom gravity-based foundations, and

 3

 1 gravity-based anchors of floating offshore structures that support wind turbines. Numerous

 2 experimental fatigue investigations and analytical studies have been conducted on concrete

 3 materials with different material compositions [13-21]. This paper focuses on 3D-printed

 4 concrete material, and this is believed to be the first time that the compressive fatigue

 5 characteristics under compressive cyclic loads have been studied. The fatigue characteristics

 6 of 3D printed material hypothesized to be affected by the printing layout and the

 7 holes/defects between the filaments inside the concrete material. Most of this work has

 8 focused on fatigue life capacity (i.e. number of loading cycles to failure), with some data

 9 collected and reported on the behavior (i.e. developing strain behavior) of concrete under

10 cyclic fatigue loading [14, 22-28]. Authors on this paper have contributed to some of the

11 work that examines behavior [27-29] including the effect of parameters that have not been

12 given significant attention in earlier studies.

13 RESEARCH SIGNIFICANCE

14 This study offers insights into the fatigue capacity of 3D printed concrete by presenting

15 experimental data on the number of cycles to failure, and comparing them with several

16 fatigue life prediction models and conventional concrete experimental data, marking

17 presumably the first instance of such a study being conducted. For the first time, improved

18 estimated strength method is used to define the experimental fatigue load and reduce the

19 scatter of the final fatigue capacity results. Furthermore, the investigation delves into the

20 stress-strain and stiffness characteristics of 3D printed concrete under fatigue loading

21 conditions, contributing to a deeper understanding of its behavior.

22

23 EXPERIMENTAL INVESTIGATION

24 Materials and Specimens

25  Material development

 4

 1 The development of mortar mixtures for the 3D-printed specimens used in this study focused

 2 on the use of supplementary cementitious materials (SCMs) for high replacement (40%) of

 3 ordinary Portland cement (OPC) with the use of flyash, silica fume and limestone filler [7].

 4 An iterative process to define the proper combination of these materials was undertaken to

 5 identify the appropriate fresh-state properties for 3D-printing. Material M13 was designed

 6 based on the results of flow table tests, isothermal calorimetry and compressive strength of

 7 mortar cubes. The mixture proportioning for M13 consisted of 60% OPC, 20% fly ash class

 8 C, 10% silica fume, and 10% limestone filler. The water-to-cementitious material (w/cm)

 9 ratio was set at 0.36. Chemical admixtures to adjust the consistency of the mixture for

10 successful printability were used. These admixtures were a High-range water-reducer

11 admixture (Master Glenium 7700), and viscosity modifying admixture (Master Matrix 362)

12 with a dosage of 0.15% and 0.05% by weight of cementitious material used, respectively.

13  Preliminary durability assessment

14 Two major durability tests were performed on specimens made out of mixture M13: Rapid

15 chloride penetration tests and absorption of water, as indicators of the durability properties of

16 different printing configurations. It was observed that a lower overlap of adjacent filaments

17 would increase the porosity of specimens and susceptibility to the ingress of chlorides or

18 water. Configurations with moderate overlap levels (i.e., 26% and 60%) show comparable

19 performance to cast specimens in charge passed (100-1000 Coulombs) and absorption tests

20 (1.0-2.0 mm). These results indicate that control over the filament interfaces of 3D-printed

21 elements could enhance the durability and mechanical properties of 3D-printed specimens [7].

22  Printing process

23 A 6-axis robotic arm with about 1 in (25-mm) nozzle opening was used to produce 3D-

24 printed solid blocks with dimensions of 36 in (914.4 mm) x 15 in (381 mm) x 10 in (254 mm).

25 The filament height was set to a little bit higher than 3/8 in (10 mm) and the nominal filament

 5

 1 width for the toolpath was set to about 1 in (25 mm). The printing path selected consisted in

 2 the alternation of perpendicular layers to minimize the anisotropy of the overall 3D-printed

 3 element during mechanical testing, ensuring that the interfaces of the filaments were not

 4 aligned between consecutive layers; see Figure 1. The 3D-printed filaments had a cross-

 5 sectional area of 1” (25.4 mm) in width by 3/8” (9.525 mm) in height. The filament was

 6 extruded at a continuous speed of 200 mm/s.

 7  Coring process

 8 To obtain cored cylindrical specimens with dimensions 4 in (101.6 mm) x 8 in (203 mm) for

 9 fatigue testing, the 3D-printed blocks were cured for seven days by covering them with a

10 plastic sheet, and water was continuously applied on their surface to avoid excessive

11 evaporation. After seven days, the samples were extracted from the block using the sequence

12 illustrated in Figure 1. For this purpose, a concrete core-drill was used with a continuous

13 supply of water to avoid damage to the equipment and the concrete specimens.

14  Compressive strength

15 Mechanical testing of cast and 3D-printed samples showed a reduction in the compressive

16 strength of 3D-printed specimens of about 38% with respect to specimens cast with the same

17 material. Further investigation is needed to determine the effects of the processing or material

18 processing during the different stages of the printing process.

19

20 Test setup and data acquisition

21 A 200-kip compressive servo-hydraulic testing machine was used for the present testing

22 program. Monotonic tests were conducted in a quasi-static displacement-controlled loading

23 protocol to capture concrete's post-peak stress-strain response to compressive loading. The

24 force-control mode was used for the fatigue loading to maintain the peak and valley stress

25 targets within an acceptable range. Sinusoidal waveform defined for the cyclic loading. Two

 6

 1 extensometers mounted on both sides of the cylinder (see Figure 2) measured the change in

 2 the length over the gauge length (6 in or 150 mm) and captured the axial strain evolution of

 3 the material over the fatigue life (see Figure 9). The stress and strain hysteresis data were

 4 collected using a separate data acquisition system at a frequency of 25 Hz. The fatigue

 5 loading protocol is defined using the improved strength estimation method, ISEM, to have

 6 the fatigue loading protocol defined as precise as possible. Compressive loading protocol

 _
 7 consists of maximum and minimum loading ratio ( = and =
 _

 _
 8 ), where _ represents the monotonic strength, _ represents
 _

 9 the maximum imposed stress during fatigue cyclic loading, and _ represents the

10 minimum imposed stress during fatigue cyclic loading. Usually, the average strength of the

11 material from recent monotonic tests is used as monotonic strength, and this method can lead

12 to unrealistic results due to the variation in actual strength from one specimen to another

13 specimen. To resolve this issue, we implemented ISEM method to make an improved

14 estimate the strength of each cylinder before doing the fatigue testing. For this purpose, we

15 load the cylinder up to 0.95 ∗ level (defined based on average strength of the batch),

16 considering the standard loading rate defined in ASTM C39. Then, the stress-strain response

17 gets plotted versus the monotonic test results. The closest monotonic test result to the plotted

18 stress-strain response would be that cylinder's improved estimated/assumed strength. For

19 instance, Figure 3 shows that the fatigue test #7 has the closest stress-strain behavior to

20 monotonic test result #5 So the estimated strength of this specimen for defining the fatigue

21 loading protocol using ISEM method would be 7400 psi (51 MPa) (rather than 6800 psi (47

22 MPa) which is the average strength of this batch of concrete).

 7

 1

 2 EXPERIMENTAL RESULTS, ANALYSIS AND DISCUSSION

 3 Monotonic test results

 4 The average compressive strength of these cylinders at 28 days was 5300 psi (36.5 MPa).

 5 Seven monotonic tests were conducted following ASTM C39’s loading protocol on the 3D-

 6 printed cored concrete cylinders right before the start of the fatigue testing program. The

 7 average speed of loading was 31-34 psi/sec (0.21-0.23 MPa/sec). The results show

 8 considerable variation in strength among cylinders (5900-8700 psi or 40.5-60 MPa). The

 9 coefficient of variation is high relative to the casted concrete, and it is about 11%; see Figure

10 4 & Table 1. The variation might be due to holes and empty spaces between the filaments of

11 the printed concrete layers, which can vary by size and amount because of printing speed,

12 layout, and other factors still not well understood. [1, 2, 7].

13 Fatigue test results

14 I. Capacity

15 The fatigue load combination of ( , , Frequency) = (0.80, 0.05, 1 Hz) was selected

16 for most of the fatigue tests for three reasons: 1. Based on the optimum operational range of

17 the machine; 2. The time needed to do the tests; and 3. Comparability to the other test results.

18 In order to fully understand the fatigue behavior of a specific material more combinations of

19 (Smax, Smin, Frequency) should of course be studied. Using the method defined in the “Test

20 setup” section, using the improved estimate of the strength of the cylinders, their cross-

21 sectional area, and the load combination used to define the actual maximum (peak) and

22 minimum (valley) loads to be imposed on the test specimen.

23 Most of the tests were carried out up to failure by imposing uniaxial compressive constant

24 amplitude cyclic load (i.e. one value of and per test) using a sinusoidal waveform

25 with a predefined frequency of 1 Hz. Two tests were shut down before failure due to

 8

 1 technical difficulties in the electricity-related laboratory facilities. The fatigue capacity

 2 (Number of cycles to failure; , also called fatigue capacity) is presented in Table 2. Figure

 3 5 shows the average fatigue capacity of the 3D printed concrete compared with plain

 4 concretes and also to the 3 selected characteristic S-N (Stress ratio -number of cycles) curves.

 5 The formulation for the curves is given in the appendix (see Table 3). In this plot, the

 _
 6 maximum nominal stress level ( = ) is presented on the vertical axis,
 _

 7 and the logarithm of the number of cycles to failure, log10 ( ), is shown on the horizontal

 8 axis. This plot shows on average that the 3D-printed concrete values were usually much

 9 larger than that given estimation of fatigue life by selected models, and 3D printed concrete

10 can have at least the same fatigue capacity as ordinary casted of similar monotonic strength.

11 Figure 6 shows the actual fatigue capacity (log10 ) compared to the S-N curves. The main

12 observation is that the variation in fatigue capacity is still high (even by using the improved

13 strength estimation method, ISEM). The possible reason for that can be the location of the

14 holes between filaments, which can affect the rate of microcracks development. Additional

15 discussion on this is presented in the next section of the paper which is on “strain evolution”.

16 II. Strain evolution

17 The strain progress due to fatigue cyclic loading was captured by two extensometers mounted

18 on opposite sides of the cylinder. The data acquisition rate was 2000 Hz, then it was down-

19 sampled to 25 Hz and logged in a text file. Also, two high-resolution cameras were used to

20 get photos with predefined time intervals from both sides of the cylinder to show the progress

21 of the cracks and failure along with the measured data. The peak and valley stains are the

22 uniaxial strain related to the maximum and minimum uniaxial compressive load imposed on

23 the test specimen. The progression of strain depicted versus the number of cycles (or the

24 normalized number of cycles) is called the strain evolution plot (see Figure 7, right side).

25 Figure 7 shows the results of one of the fatigue tests (Test #9 or 3DPC8-CAT#9-P80-V5-F1)

 9

 1 in two plots. On the left side, the monotonic stress-strain response of the concrete with the

 2 same strength (3D-PC#3) is plotted along with a few down-sampled fatigue hysteresis curves

 3 (in green). It also depicted the secant stiffness lines for those hysteresis curves, which are

 4 plotted (in blue) by connecting the maximum and minimum stress-strain points ( , )

 5 and ( , ). The test failed when the last loading cycle crossed the descending branch of

 6 the monotonic test result. This is a very typical behavior observed by other researchers [27,

 7 28, 30, 31]. The peak and valley strain evolution plots are shown on the right side of Figure 8.

 8 The strain evolution plot has a S-inverted shape. Like other concrete materials, it can be seen

 9 that there are three distinctive phases of strain development. Phase I has a rapid strain

10 accumulation due to the closing of the holes and pore spaces inside the material, and it

11 usually continues up to about 0.2* . Phase II has a steady and constant linear strain

12 accumulation. In Phase II, the microcracks continue to progress. Phase III starts at about

13 0.8* and shows a rapid strain increase due to cyclic loading. The S-inverted behavior is

14 normally concluded to be due to microcracks merging and initiating macrocracks and failure

15   zones [24, 27, 28].

16 The photos taken by two cameras from the cylinder during the constant amplitude test are

17 shown in Figure 8. The goal was to capture visual crack initiation and progression during the

18 fatigue test. The 3D printed concrete showed a brittle behavior compared to casted concrete

19 that has been tested at Tufts University. Since the material has very small aggregate sizes

20 (because of printability and strength concerns), brittle behavior was expected. Therefore, the

21 visual cracks did not appear until the very end of the test. Figure 8 shows that concrete stayed

22 in contact on the surface up to at least 90% of the number of cycles to failure inside phase III,

23 and rapid strain accumulations were observed.

24 Figure 9 shows the average peak strain along with the individual strain development on both

25 sides of the cylinder. This plot shows that there was a pretty uniform strain behavior around

 10

 1 the perimeter of the cylinder, and the side strains start to deviate from each other at around

 2 half of the way to failure, but the amount of difference between these two sides measured

 3 strains is not very high compared to the other types of concrete. The reason for that might be

 4 the uniformity and homogeneity in the material itself and having very small aggregate sizes,

 5 and stronger cement paste which can make the delay the initiation of microcracks and

 6 progression/development of microcracks until the very end of the test (phase III). In other

 7 words, this is another indication of brittle behavior and this will require additional safety

 8 considerations in deisgn. All peak strain evolutions for all the tests are plotted in Figure 10.

 9 Figure 11 shows the normalized strain evolution plot. In the vertical axis, the average

10 measured peak strain normalized to the initial strain and the normalized number of cycles to

11 failure in the horizontal axis. There is an interesting observation in Figure 11. The specimens

12 with very close also have almost the same normalized strain evolution plot. It means that

13 the normalized strain slope directly correlates to the number of cycles to failure. This

14 observation is also made by other studies before [22, 24, 27, 30].

15 The peak strains at the final cycles for all the failed specimens are depicted along with

16 normalized monotonic stress-strain response in Figure 12. This shows that the last cycle

17 intersects with the descending branch of the monotonic stress-strain response of concrete to

18 uniaxial compressive loading. Since there was a high variation in monotonic responses, the

19 variation in pre-failure peak strain related to fatigue loading is expected, too.

20 III. Secant stiffness

21 The secant stiffness was calculated for each cycle of loading using peak and valley stress-

22 strain data points (see Equation 1). The secant stiffness at the start and the end of all the

23 fatigue tests versus the log of are shown in Figure 13. It can be observed that the secant

24 stiffness at the end of the tests is always lower than the start, and it shows the degradation of

25 concrete stiffness due to the fatigue loading, and this degradation has no correlation with .

 11

 1 The trend lines are shown for the secant stiffness of start and end. There is no considerable

 2 trend associated with , but still, there is a variation in the start and end secant stiffness

 3 which can be due to normal variations in concrete material mechanical characteristics. The

 4 difference between trend lines gets bigger with the increase in , therefore, it means the

 5 higher failure number of cycles has higher stiffness degradation. Since the has strong

 6 connections with the loading combination ( , ), thus, the stiffness degradation has

 7 correlation with the loading combination. In other words, the lower the loading ratio, the

 8 higher the , and the larger the stiffness degradation.

 9 = Equation 1
10 IV. Visual cracks

11 Using the photos taken from the test specimens in predefined time intervals, the approximate

12 number of cycles associated with the first visual crack appearance was calculated for all the

13 tests. Then, the peak strains at those number of cycles are plotted against the maximum stress

14 level along with the normalized monotonic stress-strain response to be compared. Some of

15 the points intersect some of the descending parts of stress-strain curves. This is an indicator

16 for brittle behavior. This observation is very consistent with the observations from the photos

17 of the test specimen during the test and the deviation of measured strain on both sides of the

18 concrete at the very end of the tests.

19 V. Fatigue capacity prediction using strain and stiffness gradients

20 The correlation between the gradient of strains or stiffness in phase II (which is fairly

21 constant) and as has been reported in the literature [24, 25, 27, 28]. To examine the

22 applicability of this theory in the 3D printed concrete material, the fatigue test results were

23 down-sampled to 23 equally distanced points based on the failure number of cycles. Figure

24 15-a shows the down-sampled peak strain evolution plot against the normalized . Figure

25 15-b shows the gradient of peak strain, which can be assumed constant in phase II. Figure 15-

 12

 1 c shows the zoomed-in version of the gradient peak strain versus the Normalized . The

 2 average of the middle 13 gradient values (excluding the 5 initial and 5 final points) is

 3 considered the representative gradient peak strain of the test specimen. The same process has

 4 been done for gradient valley strain and stiffness.

 5 Figure 16, Figure 17 & Figure 18, respectively, show the correlation of gradient peak strain,

 6 gradient valley strain, and gradient stiffness to the power of two against the normalized .

 7 This correlation is linear for these two parameters' logarithm (base 10). Since the stiffness

 8 gradient is negative and having a logarithm of a negative value is undefined, the gradient

 9 stiffness to the power of two was used. The corresponding linear regression equations and

10 their associated coefficients of correlation (R2) are provided in the plots. The high coefficient

11 correlation value shows a strong correlation between the parameters. The lower the gradient,

12 the higher the number of cycles to failure. These correlations can be used to estimate the

13 minimum number of cycles to failure of the run-out tests. In other words, in a run-out test that

14 got stopped around mid-life, the gradient of strains and stiffness can be used to predict the

15 remaining fatigue life (by finding and subtracting the number of cycles the run-out tests

16 had experienced). Using these three correlations, the failure number of cycles (fatigue life)

17 for all fatigue tests (including two cut-out compressive fatigue test) are estimated and the

18 average, minimum and maximum are listed in Table 4. The results have low error values

19 for the failed tests, showing the method's reliability.

20

21 CONCLUSIONS

22 In this study, the fatigue characteristics of 3D printed concrete have been investigated by

23 doing uniaxial compressive cyclic experimental tests. A new improved strength estimation

24 method, ISEM for predefining the fatigue load protocol more accurately has been used to

 13

 1 reduce the effect of strength variation on the outcome of the tests. Below is a summary of the

 2 key takeaways from the experiments and analysis.

 3 1. The experimental results of the constant amplitude compressive fatigue tests show a

 4 promising outcome. The average fatigue capacity of the 3D-printed concrete is greater

 5 than the estimated capacity by the three selected standard fatigue models. Also, the

 6 3D printed behaves similar to normally casted concrete, but the variation of the results

 7 is high. This variation is expected in fatigue tests, but for the 3D printed concrete, the

 8 different locations of the holes and pores among the filaments are the main suspects

 9 that can affect the progress of microcracks.

10 2. The progress of peak and valley strain and degradation of secant stiffness due to

11 cyclic loading have been studied. The results showed typical three distinct phases of

12 strain development and stiffness degradation (like what has been seen in normal

13 concrete). The strain was plotted against the failure number of cycles for each

14 specimen (called strain evolution plot), and it is a S-inverted shape plot. In contrast,

15 the stiffness degradation plot shows a S-upright shape. Observations about S-shape

16 behavior are similar to what previous studies have made [22, 24, 27, 30].

17 3. The strain measured on both sides of the specimen and the 3D printed concrete in

18 almost all cases showed a uniformly distributed strain accumulation on both sides. i.e.,

19 the difference between the measured strains was very small, and it was mostly in the

20 last phase of strain accumulation. This is due to the brittle behavior of concrete with

21 small aggregate size, stronger cement paste and high strength characteristics. The

22 normalized strain plot versus normalized showed that the specimens with almost

23 the same number of cycles have similar strain evolution curves.

24 4. Photos taken from two sides of the specimens during the tests were evaluated to find

25 the approximate number of cycles when the first visual crack appeared. The visual

 14

 1 cracks become visible on the circumferential surface of the cylinder at the very end of

 2 the fatigue life of the cylinders. The peak strains associated with that got plotted

 3 against the maximum stress level, along with the normalized monotonic stress-strain

 4 response of 3D-printed concrete. The intersection of these peak strains related to

 5 visual cracks with the softening part of monotonic responses proved the material's

 6 brittleness.

 7 5. The correlation of the average gradient of peak and valley strain, and stiffness in

 8 phase II and the number of cycles to failure have been investigated. There is a linear

 9 correlation between these parameters' logarithm and 's logarithm. The high

10 coefficient of correlation (R2) proves a direct relationship between these factors.

11 These linear equations are used to predict the fatigue capacity of run-out tests.
